About the Author

![](Images/author.jpg)

Lawrence G. Decamora III, Ph.D., is an accomplished computer science educator, author, and industry professional with extensive experience in software development and academia. Holding prestigious certifications including Sun Certified Java Programmer (SCJP) and Oracle Certified Professional Java Programmer (OCPJP), he has contributed significantly to the field of information technology.

Dr. Decamora has been a dedicated instructor in computer science, information systems, and information technology for over two decades. He has taught at renowned institutions such as the University of Santo Tomas, De La Salle-College of Saint Benilde, and Mapua Institute of Technology. His academic leadership includes serving as the Acting ITE Program Director and an Academic Head, mentoring countless students and aspiring IT professionals.

In the industry, Dr. Decamora has collaborated with leading organizations like Sun Microsystems Philippines, Phoenix One Knowledge Solutions Inc. , and ActiveLearning Inc., providing Java training and software development solutions. His expertise spans Java programming, enterprise applications, and web technologies.

Beyond teaching, Dr. Decamora is a sought-after speaker and trainer, having conducted seminars and workshops on Java programming, software engineering, and emerging technologies. His passion for knowledge sharing is evident in his commitment to empowering learners through practical insights and real-world applications.

This book is a testament to his dedication to making Java accessible to learners at all levels. Through clear explanations and hands-on examples, Dr. Decamora continues to inspire and guide the next generation of developers.

Preface

Java Essentials Volume 2: Object Oriented Programming and Beyond is a natural continuation of the foundational topics introduced in Volume 1\. While the first volume focused on building a solid grasp of Java fundamentals—covering syntax, control structures, arrays, strings, and the basic building blocks of Java programs—this second volume shifts the focus to more advanced, structured, and professional programming concepts using object-oriented programming (OOP) as the central theme.

This volume is written for learners who are ready to move beyond writing simple programs and begin understanding the true power of Java—encapsulation, inheritance, polymorphism, and the wide array of tools that make Java a platform for building scalable, maintainable, and modular software applications.

Starting with a bridge chapter that connects the procedural programming mindset to the object-oriented paradigm, each chapter builds on the previous one, reinforcing understanding through well-structured explanations, sample code, real-world analogies, coding tasks, and review quizzes.

From mastering Java’s reference data types to understanding class design, method overloading, inheritance structures, exception handling, and the collection framework, this book is designed to equip students with both theoretical concepts and practical skills. You will also explore Graphical User Interfaces (GUI) and the foundations of event-driven programming, ultimately culminating in building full-fledged desktop applications using Java Swing.

Whether you’re a computer science student, a self-taught programmer, or an instructor looking for a structured resource, Volume 2 serves as a guide to help you think like a Java developer and write code the way professionals do.

I hope that this volume not only helps solidify your Java skills but also inspires confidence in creating applications that are elegant, reusable, and scalable. Let this be your stepping stone toward becoming a proficient Java programmer.

– Dr. Lawrence Decamora
Author & Instructor

Introduction to the Book

Welcome to Java Essentials: Volume 2—a comprehensive guide designed to help you master the principles of object-oriented programming (OOP) and deepen your understanding of advanced Java topics.

In Volume 1, we laid the groundwork by exploring Java’s syntax, primitive data types, control structures, arrays, strings, and how to write and run basic Java applications. These topics formed the foundation for thinking like a programmer and writing correct, efficient Java code.

In Volume 2, we build on that foundation. This book shifts focus from procedural programming to a more structured and scalable approach using objects and classes—the heart of Java programming. Here, you will learn how to design and build real-world applications using OOP concepts such as encapsulation, inheritance, and polymorphism, and how to implement them in your own programs.

But we don’t stop there.

This volume also introduces you to:

●The Java API and how to navigate its documentation effectively

●The concept of exception handling to make your programs more robust and error-tolerant

●Working with Java’s Collection Framework and Generics for managing data efficiently

●Creating text-based and graphical user interface (GUI) applications using AWT and Swing

●Implementing event-driven programming and packaging your Java applications for distribution

Each chapter is designed with clarity and progression in mind. You’ll encounter:

●Illustrative code examples to guide your understanding

●Hands-on coding tasks to reinforce concepts through practice

●Quizzes and chapter summaries to help you assess your progress

●Reflection questions and real-world insights to deepen your critical thinking as a developer

Whether you’re a student preparing for exams, a self-taught programmer aiming to level up, or a developer looking to strengthen your Java fundamentals, this book is your next step toward becoming a confident and capable Java programmer.

Let’s continue your journey—one object, one method, one application at a time.

Happy coding!

Who Can Benefit From This Book?

Java Essentials Volume 2: Object Oriented Programming and Beyond is written with a wide audience in mind, particularly those who are ready to take the next step in their Java programming journey. If you’ve completed the basics—whether through Volume 1 or other introductory courses—this volume is designed to elevate your skills and deepen your understanding of object-oriented and advanced Java concepts.

Here’s who will benefit the most from this book:

#### Students taking object-oriented programming courses

This book aligns with most university-level curricula for second-semester Java programming courses. It introduces key object-oriented concepts like classes, inheritance, polymorphism, interfaces, exception handling, and file I/O in a structured, student-friendly way.

#### Self-taught programmers

If you’ve learned Java fundamentals on your own, or with Volume 1, and are now ready to go beyond writing simple scripts or programs, this book will guide you through more sophisticated techniques and design patterns used in real-world Java development.

#### Instructors and trainers

Educators looking for a well-organized, comprehensive teaching companion will find this volume valuable. The structured format—complete with summaries, quizzes, and coding tasks—makes it easy to build lectures, assignments, and exams around.

#### Career shifters and bootcamp graduates

If you’re shifting into software development or have just completed a coding bootcamp, this book will help you reinforce and expand your skills—especially in object-oriented design, Java Collections, and graphical user interface development.

#### Junior developers looking to strengthen their foundation

Early-career developers who need to solidify their understanding of core Java and object-oriented programming will find this book an excellent reference and practice guide for everyday development scenarios.

No matter your background, if you’re ready to move from beginner to intermediate-level Java programming, this book is designed to support and accelerate your learning journey.

How to Use This Book?

Java Essentials Volume 2 is designed as a step-by-step guide to help learners transition from basic Java knowledge to mastering object-oriented programming and other advanced Java features. Here's how to make the most of it:

#### 1\. Follow the chapters in sequence

The chapters in this book are organized progressively. It is strongly recommended to read them in order, as each chapter builds on the concepts covered in the previous ones. For example, understanding reference types and the this keyword in [Chapter 1](#14_Chapter01.xhtml) will make it easier to comprehend inheritance and polymorphism in later chapters.

#### 2\. Start with the bridge chapter

Before diving into OOP, [Chapter 0](#13_Chapter00.xhtml) offers a bridge from procedural thinking to object-oriented design. It refreshes key ideas from Volume 1 and prepares your mindset for writing structured, reusable code using objects and classes.

#### 3\. Pay attention to code examples

Each chapter contains carefully selected code snippets that illustrate new concepts. Type these examples on your own, run them in your IDE or terminal, and modify them to deepen your understanding.

#### 4\. Try the coding tasks

At the end of most chapters, you’ll find Coding Tasks that challenge you to apply what you’ve learned. These exercises range from reinforcing basic concepts to building small functional programs. Use them as practice or mini-projects to test your skills.

#### 5\. Use the chapter summaries and quizzes

Every chapter ends with a Chapter Summary that recaps the key takeaways, followed by a Quiz that reinforces comprehension through multiple-choice or short-answer questions. These tools are especially useful for exam preparation or self-assessment.

#### 6\. Explore further with reflections and projects

Some chapters include reflection questions or encourage small projects. These are meant to get you thinking critically about how concepts apply to real-world programming scenarios or future software projects.

#### 7\. Make use of additional resources

Where applicable, references and screenshots to Java API documentation, additional reading, or helpful tools are provided. Familiarizing yourself with the use of the Java API Documentation will improve your skills in developing Java Apps.

#### 8\. Practice consistently

Mastering Java requires consistent practice. Don’t just read—code! Use what you learn in each chapter to build small programs, experiment with variations, and try integrating new topics into your past exercises.

This book is more than just a collection of Java topics—it's a roadmap to becoming a confident object-oriented programmer. Take your time, code regularly, and don’t be afraid to make mistakes. Every error is an opportunity to grow.

Happy coding!

# [CHAPTER 0](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch0)

# [The Bridge to Volume 2: Bridging Fundamentals with Object-Oriented Programming](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch0)

![](Images/klo.jpg)Key Learning Objectives

●Review the foundational knowledge from Volume 1 and understand how it connects to object-oriented programming.

●Understand the role of reference data types in object modeling.

●Differentiate between primitive and reference data types through real-world analogies and practical code.

●Recognize the purpose of object-oriented concepts like encapsulation, inheritance, and polymorphism in software development.

●Prepare their learning environment for Volume 2’s topics, including multi-class projects, API documentation, and advanced file structures.

## [0.1Looking Back: What You Already Know](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch0-1)

Before we dive into the more advanced world of Java programming, let’s take a moment to look back at everything you’ve learned so far. In Volume 1, you started your journey by learning how to install Java and set up your development environment. This included installing the JDK (Java Development Kit) and a text editor (i.e., Notepad++). Once everything was set up, you were able to write and run your very first program.

Your first Java program probably looked something like this:

1 // Filename: HelloWorld.java

2 public class HelloWorld {

3    public static void main(String[] args) {

4        System.out.println("Hello, world!");

5    }

6 }

In this example, you learned about the basic structure of a Java program. The main method is the starting point of every Java application, and System.out.println() is used to display messages on the screen.

As you wrote more programs, you became familiar with Java’s syntax rules. You learned that every statement in Java ends with a semicolon (;), code is grouped into blocks using curly braces { }, and good indentation and spacing help make your code easier to read.

Here’s an example with proper indentation:

if (score >= 75) {
    System.out.println("You passed!");
} else {
    System.out.println("Try again.");
}

Java programs store and process data using primitive data types such as byte, short, int, and long for whole numbers, float and double for decimals, char for single characters, and boolean for true or false values. You might remember using them like this:

int age = 16;
double gpa = 3.75;
char grade = 'A';
boolean isEnrolled = true;

Each data type has a specific purpose, and choosing the right one makes your code more efficient and understandable.

To make your programs interactive, you learned how to ask for input using the Scanner class. This allowed users to type in answers and data at runtime.

1  // Filename: AskName.java

2 import java.util.Scanner;
3
4 public class AskName {
5    public static void main(String[] args) {
6       Scanner input = new Scanner(System.in);
7       System.out.print("Enter your name: ");
8       String name = input.nextLine();
9       System.out.println("Hello, " + name + "!");
10   }
11 }

This example asks the user for their name and then prints a greeting. You used similar programs to read numbers and make simple calculators.

You also explored how to make decisions in your code using if, if-else-if, and else statements. These control structures allowed your programs to choose different paths based on conditions.

int score = 85;
if (score >= 90) {
     System.out.println("Grade: A");
} else if (score >= 80) {
     System.out.println("Grade: B");
} else {
     System.out.println("Keep working!");
}

You learned about loops, which are used to repeat code. Java provides several types of loops, such as while, for, and do-while.

for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) {
     System.out.println(i);
}

Loops help automate repetitive tasks and make your code shorter and more powerful.

Text in Java is handled by the String class. You learned how to combine strings, check their length, and change their format.

String name = "Michelle";
System.out.println("Your name has " +
     name.length() + " letters.");
System.out.println(name.toUpperCase());

You also explored more advanced string tools like StringBuilder and StringBuffer for modifying text efficiently.

Sometimes you need to store a list of values, like test scores or student names. That’s where arrays came in.

int[] scores = {85, 90, 78, 92};
System.out.println("First score: " + scores[0]);

Arrays helped you manage multiple pieces of data using a single variable.

All these concepts—variables, data types, input/output, decisions, loops, strings, and arrays—are the building blocks of every Java program. They prepared you to move on to something even more powerful: object-oriented programming (OOP).

In the next chapters, you’ll learn how to use Java to model real-world objects using classes and objects, and how to build larger, smarter, and more organized applications. But don’t worry—you’ll still use everything you’ve learned so far!

## [0.2A Preview of What’s Ahead](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch0-2)

This volume begins with a deeper look at something called reference data types. These are different from the primitive data types you’ve already worked with. Reference data types do not store actual values like numbers or characters. Instead, they store references—or memory addresses—that point (references) to objects. You will learn how to create and use objects, which are the heart of object-oriented programming.

Once you understand objects, you'll move on to some of the most powerful concepts in Java: encapsulation, inheritance, and polymorphism. These are the core principles of object-oriented programming (OOP). Encapsulation means wrapping code and data together and hiding the details from other parts of the program. Inheritance allows one class to inherit traits from another class. Polymorphism lets different objects respond to the same method call in different ways.

You will also learn about constructors—special methods used to set up new objects—as well as the difference between instance methods and static methods. An instance method belongs to a specific object, while a static method belongs to the class itself and doesn’t require an object to be used.

Later in this volume, you’ll explore abstract classes and interfaces, which allow you to create flexible and reusable code. You will also be introduced to functional programming concepts in Java, including lambda expressions and functional interfaces. These allow you to write shorter and cleaner code.

In addition to learning about code, you’ll also discover how Java programs are packaged and shared. You’ll learn how to create and use JAR (Java Archive) files, how to read Java API documentation, and how to handle exceptions—errors that can happen while your program is running.

By the end of Volume 2, you’ll be writing larger, more organized, and more professional programs. You’ll have the tools to think like a software developer and build applications that are not only functional but also easy to maintain and understand.

## [0.3Why Object-Oriented Programming?](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch0-3)

As your programs grow in size and complexity, you’ll find that organizing your code becomes more and more important. This is where Object-Oriented Programming, or OOP for short, becomes incredibly useful. OOP is a programming style that focuses on using 'objects' to represent real-world things.

Instead of writing one long block of code that does everything, you use objects to break your program into smaller, manageable parts. Each object has its own properties (also called fields) and behaviors (also called methods). This helps make your program easier to understand, easier to fix when something goes wrong, and easier to expand when you want to add new features.

For example, imagine you’re writing a program to store information about students. In a basic program, you might use separate arrays like this:

String[] studentNames = {"Matthew","Mark","Luke"};
int[] studentAges = {17, 16, 18};
double[] studentGrades = {89.5, 92.0, 85.75};

But what if you have 30 students? Using separate arrays like this can become confusing and hard to manage, especially when your data grows. What if you accidentally mix up the names and grades? It’s easy to make mistakes when things aren’t grouped together.

Instead, using OOP, you can create a Student class that keeps all the information in one place:

1  // Filename: Student.java

2  public class Student {

3    String name;

4    int age;

5    double grade;

6

7    public void printDetails() {

8          System.out.println(name + " is " + age +

9          " years old and has a grade of " + grade);

10    }

11  }

Now, instead of keeping student data in separate arrays, you can create objects from the Student class. Each object keeps its own data and knows how to display its own details:

Student student1 = new Student();
student1.name = "Matthew";
student1.age = 17;
student1.grade = 89.5;
student1.printDetails();

This style of programming not only makes your code easier to read and understand but also helps prevent errors. OOP encourages you to think in terms of real-world objects, which makes programming more intuitive and more fun. As you move forward in this volume, you’ll learn how to design and build your own classes, create objects, and use the power of OOP to build better applications.

![](Images/note.jpg) [Chapter Summary](#08_Contents.xhtml_sum0)

# [CHAPTER 1](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch1)

# [The Reference Data Types](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch1)

![](Images/klo.jpg)Key Learning Objectives

●The reference data types

●Assigning references to variables

●Pass by value

●The this reference

●Local variables

In this section, we will delve into the reference data types in Java, which include objects and arrays, allowing variables to reference memory locations rather than storing the actual data. We’ll discuss how assigning references to variables works, where variables hold references to objects, enabling multiple variables to point to the same object. Next, we will explain Java’s "pass by value" mechanism, where method arguments are passed as copies, meaning changes inside the method do not affect the original variables. Finally, we’ll explore the "this reference", a keyword used within an instance method or constructor to refer to the current object, helping to resolve naming conflicts and to invoke other constructors or methods.

## [1.1The Reference Data Types](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch1-1)

Beyond all primitive data types are reference data types. A reference variable contains the address of the object and not the object itself. The object’s address is assigned by the Java Virtual Machine (JVM) during the object instantiation stage, commonly known as the creation stage. Hard-coding the object’s address in your source code is not possible.

Common examples of reference variables are Strings, Arrays, and Objects lik e Person or Button.

Example:

1  // Person.java

2  public class Person {

3    public String name = "~noname";

4    public int age = 0;

5  

6    public Person(String name, int age)    {

7        this.name = name;

8        this.age = age;  

9    }

10

11    public String toString()  {

12        return name + " at age " + age;

13    }

14  }

1  // TestPerson.java

2  public class TestPerson {

3    public static void main(String[] args) {

4        Person p1 = new Person("Lawrence", 20);

5    }

6  }

Allow me to explain step-by-step what happened in line 4 of the above code snippet.

Figure 1.1p1 initially set to null

![image](Images/fig1-1.jpg)

Person p1 created a space in the memory. The reference value will be assigned later. The variable p1 is called a reference variable because it will later hold a value that is a reference to an object of type Person.

Figure 1.2p1 initially set to null with initial values of name and age

![image](Images/fig1-2.jpg)

After the space in the heap memory has been reserved for the object, the action now takes place in the stack memory. In Java, stack memory is where local variables and method call information are stored. It operates in a last-in, first-out manner, meaning data is added and removed as methods execute. Heap memory, on the other hand, is used for dynamic memory allocation, where objects and reference data types are created. Based on your class declaration, you have two instance variables: the variable String name and the variable int age. These variables will also occupy spaces in the stack memory and will be assigned their default values. Table 1.1 shows the default values of each data type.

Table 1.1 Set of default values for variables

| Variable | Value |
| --- | --- |
| byte | 0 |
| short | 0 |
| int | 0 |
| long | 0L |
| float | 0.0F |
| double | 0.0D |
| char | ‘\u0000’ |
| boolean | false |
| All reference types | null |

As you can see, since the variable name was declared as a String, its initial value will be set to null. As for your variable age, it was declared as an int, therefore, an initial value of 0 will be assigned to it by the Virtual Machine.

Figure 1.3 Stack and heap representation of a Java object (Person p1)

![image](Images/fig1-3.jpg)

After the initial values are given by the Virtual Machine, the next set of values assigned will be based on the declared values in the class. In the class Person, the String name will now be assigned a value of “noname”, and the int age variable will be set to 0. These values will now be used to overwrite the values that were set by the Virtual Machine.

Figure 1.4 Setting of values for name and age

![image](Images/fig1-4.jpg)

After the class values have overwritten the values set by the Virtual Machine, the next step is to execute the constructor. Executing the constructor means assigning the values of the constructor’s parameters to the class’s instance variables.

Figure 1.5 Values assigned to the variables after the object is created

![image](Images/fig1-5.jpg)

After all parameters of the constructors have been set, all the values of the instance variables, we can safely say that the object has already been created. The last step is to assign the object to its reference variable by assigning its address to it.

## [1.2Assigning References to Variables](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch1-2)

In lines 1 and 2, both x and y are primitive values. It means that their actual values on the stack memory are 10\. While in line 3, you’ve created a new Person object having "Lawrence" as the value of its name and 20 for its age. Since p1 is a reference variable, the actual value of p1 is the address where the object resides.

Figure 1.6 Assigning Values for x, y, p1 and p2

![image](Images/fig1-6.jpg)

While in line 4, when you say Person p2 = p1, what you’re actually doing is assigning the value of p1 to p2, which are actually the memory addresses. This means that there are two variables pointing to the same object but having different variable names.

5   p2 = new Person("Walter", 30);

In line 5, we have created a new object passing a name "Walter" with age value of 30 and have assigned it to p2. You will see in the diagram below that p2 will now have a new memory address. A new memory address means a new object.

Figure 1.7 Values of p1 and p2 after line 5

![image](Images/fig1-7.jpg)

Understanding the difference between primitive variables and reference variables is crucial in Java. Primitive variables store actual values directly in the stack, while reference variables store memory addresses that point to objects in the heap. When you assign one reference variable to another, you’re copying the address, not the object itself, meaning both variables point to the same object. However, creating a new object and assigning it to a reference variable results in a new memory address, effectively changing the object that the variable refers to. This distinction is key to mastering object manipulation and memory behavior in Java.

In a single Virtual Machine, everything in Java is Passed by Value.

In a single Virtual Machine, we can pass values to a method, and those values are called arguments. Arguments can be the actual value of a variable, or they can be the address of an object. Here’s a sample code that will demonstrate the "Pass by Value" rule.

   1  public class PassTest {

   2

   3    // Methods to change the current values

   4    public static void changeInt(int value) {

   5        value = 55;

   6    }

   7

   8    public static void changeObjectRef(Person ref) {

   9        ref = new Person("Walter", 30);

  10    }

  11  

  12    public static void changeObjectAttr(Person ref) {

  13        ref.setAge(30);

  14    }

  15

  16    public static void main(String[] args) {

  17        Person per;

  18        int val;

  19

  20        // Assign the int

  21        val = 11;

  22        // Try to change it

  23        changeInt(val);

  24        // What is the current value?

  25        System.out.println("Int value is: " + val);

  26

  27        // Assign the person

  28        per = new Person("Lawrence", 20);

  29        // Try to change it

  30        changeObjectRef(per);

  31        // What is the current value?

  32        System.out.println("Person: " + per);

  33

  34        // Now change the day attribute

  35        // through the object reference

  36        changeObjectAttr(per);

  37        // What is the current value?

  38        System.out.println("Person: " + per);

  39    }

  40  }

If this code is executed, we will always start in the main method. In lines 17 and 18, we declared two variables, a primitive variable val and a reference variable per. If we recall, reference variables contain references to an object, which means they contain the memory address where the object resides. As for primitive types, they contain the actual values of that primitive variable.

In line 21, we initialized val to 11 and passed the value of val to the method changeInt(int). Since Java uses pass-by-value, the actual value that you’re passing to the changeInt(int) method is 11 (the actual value). Regardless of what happened inside the method changeInt(int), the actual value of val will still be used.

Figure 1.8 Passing the value of val to changeInt() method

![image](Images/fig1-8.jpg)

So when you print the output in line 25, the printed output will be:

Int value is: 11

In line 28, we’ve created a new Person object; thus, the variable per will have an address as its value. But in line 30, we passed the value of per (that actually contains a memory address) to the method changeObjectRef(Person ref) that accepted a Person object.

Now, in the method changeObjectRef(Person ref) in line 9, we instantiated a new Person object, thus letting the argument ref point to a new object, thus having a new memory address value to it. In the process, we disregarded its original value that came from the main method, which points to the object "Lawrence" 20. But that object will not be garbage collected just yet because the variable per from the main method still has a reference to it. Since the active method is changeObjectRef(Person ref), the variable per of the main method is dormant at the background. Once line 10 is reached, the changeObjectRef(Person ref) method ends, and along with that, all local variables are deleted from memory, including the variable ref. And since variable ref is the only variable that is handling the object "Walter" 30, that object will also be deleted through garbage collection.

Figure 1.9 Variable ref assigned to a new object in the changeObjectRef() method

![image](Images/fig1-9.jpg)

So, when you execute line 32, you will have the output:

Person: Lawrence at age 20

Lastly, in line 36, you passed the per object to the method changeObjectAttr(Person ref), thus passing again the memory address value of per to the said method. Inside the method changeObjectAttr(Person ref) in line 13, you used the variable ref to call a member method setAge(int) to change the age of the Person object that was passed to the changeObjectAttr(Person ref)method. While variable ref is inside the method changeObjectAttr(Person ref), it actually has access to the object "Lawrence" 20 that was created in the main method because ref contains the object’s memory address.

Figure 1.10 The variable ref accepts the reference address from the method main()

![image](Images/fig1-10.jpg)

Thus, after the method changeObjectAttr(Person ref) executes, the output of line 38 will be:

Person: Lawrence at age 30

## [1.3The this Reference](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch1-3)

The keyword this can be used to:

1.Resolve ambiguity between instance variables and parameters.

2.Pass the current object as a parameter to a method.

3.Pass the current object as a parameter to a constructor.

4.Call another constructor within the same class by using this() at the first line of the body of another constructor. Consider the given sample code:  

1  public class Person {

2  

3    private String name = "noname";

4    private int age = 0;

5

6    public Person(String name, int age) {

7    

8        this.name = name;

9        this.age = age;  

10    }

11    public Person(Person per) {

12    

13        this.name = per.name;

14        this.age = per.age;  

15    }

16    public void setName(String name) {

17    

18        this.name = name;  

19    }

20    public void setAge(int age) {

21    

22        this.age = age;

23    }

24    public String getName() {

25    

26        return name;  

27    }

28    public int getAge() {

29    

30        return age;  

31    }

32    public Person addAge(int moreAge) {

33    

34        Person newPerson = new Person(this);

35        newPerson.age = newPerson.age + moreAge;

36        return newPerson;

37    }

38    public String toString() {

39    

40        return name + " at age " + age;

41    }

42  }

1  public class TestPerson {

2  

3    public static void main(String args) {

4    

5        Person p1 = new Person("Lawrence", 20);

6        Person olderPerson = p1.addAge(10);

7        System.out.println(olderPerson);

8    }

9  }

In class Person, we have two constructors, and inside the body of the first constructor that has the signature public Person(String name, int age), we saw that the parameters String name and int age are identical to our instance variables (attributes); thus we used the keyword this to resolve the ambiguity inside the constructor’s body.

8    this.name = name;

9    this.age = age;

Variable name and age in lines 8 and 9 refer to the local variables name and age declared in the constructor’s argument list in line 6\. While in the same lines 8 and 9, this.name and this.age refers to the instance variables declared in lines 3 and 4.

Another use of the keyword this is to pass the current object to the method or to the constructor. The current method refers to the method in which the current operation is taking place. If the this keyword is used for the current constructor, it follows the same rule. If the keyword this is used for a constructor, it refers to the constructor where the operation is currently taking place. And while inside the said method or constructor, the keyword this refers to the object that was used to call the said method or constructor.

Consider the method public Person addAge(int moreAge){}, inside the said method, in line 34, you have a constructor call:

34    Person newPerson = new Person(this);

In this line, the keyword this was passed to the second constructor in line 11.

11    public Person(Person per) {

12    

13        this.name = per.name;

14        this.age = per.age;  

15    }

In line 11, the argument Person per will accept the current object that was passed to the said constructor.

By the end of the execution of the program code, you should have been able to identify the flow of the Person object from the main method, to the Person constructor, back to the main method for printing.

## [1.4Local Variables](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch1-4)

Local variables in Java are variables declared inside a method, a constructor, or a block and are only accessible within that specific scope. These variables are created when the method, constructor, or block is executed and are destroyed once the execution is complete. Since local variables are confined to their scope, they are not accessible outside of the method or block where they are defined. Local variables need to be initialized first before use; they are not automatically initialized.

Here’s a code snippet that will cause an error:

public void somethingIsWrongHere() {

     int x;

     System.out.println(x);       // error!

}

In the method somethingIsWrongHere(), we have a local variable x that was declared and printed in the next line. This will cause a compilation error because x was not initialized.

### 1.4.1Local Variables in a Method

1  public class LocalVariableExample {

2    public void displaySum() {

3        int num1 = 10;            // Local variable

4        int num2 = 20;            // Local variable

5        int sum = num1 + num2;    // Local variable

6        System.out.println("Sum: " + sum);

7    }

8

9    public static void main(String[] args) {

10         LocalVariableExample example =

11             new LocalVariableExample();

12     example.displaySum();

13    }

14  }

Here, num1, num2, and sum are local variables declared inside the displaySum() method. These variables exist only while the displaySum() method is being executed. Once the method completes, these variables are discarded.

### 1.4.2Local Variables in a Block

Local variables in a block are variables declared within a specific block of code, such as an if, for, while, or {} block. These variables are accessible only within the block where they are defined, meaning they cannot be used outside of that block. Once the block finishes executing, the local variables within it are destroyed, and their memory is released.

1 public class BlockScopeExample {

2 public static void main(String[] args) {

3     // This variable exists in

4     // the entire main method.

5     int outerVariable = 5;

6

7      if (outerVariable > 0) {

8        // Local to this if block

9       int innerVariable = 10;

10     System.out.println("Inner Variable: "

11             + innerVariable);

12     }

13

14    // If used, would cause a compilation error

15    // System.out.println(innerVariable);

16  }

17 }

### 1.4.3Method With Parameters as Local Variables

1 public class MethodWithParameters {

2  // ‘name’ is a parameter and a local variable

3  public void greet(String name) {

4     System.out.println("Hello, " + name + "!");

5  }

6

7  public static void main(String[] args) {

8     MethodWithParameters example =

9            new MethodWithParameters();

10  

11    // Passing "Lawrence" as an argument

12    example.greet("Lawrence");

13     

14    // Passing "Michelle" as an argument

15    example.greet("Michelle");

16   }

17 }

In the greet() method, String name is a parameter that acts as a local variable. The value of name is assigned when the method is called (e.g., "Lawrence" or "Michelle"). This variable exists only during the execution of the greet() method and is destroyed once the method finishes.

The name parameter is accessible only within the greet() method. But outside of the greet() method, name cannot be used or referenced.

Method parameters are local variables that exist within the method’s scope. They temporarily hold the values passed by the calling code (arguments).

Local variables are essential for temporary data storage within a method or block. They enhance code modularity and efficiency by ensuring variables are accessible only within their defined scope.

### 1.4.4Local Variable Type Inference Using var (JDK 10)

The keyword var is used in place of an explicit type declaration for local variables. The compiler figures out the type by analyzing the expression on the right-hand side of the assignment.

Syntax:

var variableName = expression;

Here, the expression must have a type that the compiler can determine.

Key Points to Remember:

●var can only be used for local variables, including inside methods, in loop indexes, and in try-with-resources blocks (which will be discussed in [Chapter 6](#19_Chapter06.xhtml)).

●var cannot be used for fields (instance or static variables), method parameters, or method return types.

●The variable must be initialized at the time of declaration.

●Once inferred, the type is fixed and cannot change later.

Here are simple examples of local variable type inference.

●Inferring an int

var count = 10;

System.out.println(count + 5);

●Inferring a String

var greeting = "Hello, world!";

System.out.println(greeting.toUpperCase());

●Inferring a double

var pi = 3.14159;

System.out.println(pi * 2);

●Loop Indexes - var works well in for loops:

for (var i = 0; i < 5; i++) {

    System.out.println("i = " + i);

}

Limitations and Bad Practices:

●Don’t use when it hurts readability

var x = someMethod(); // What does x hold?

String userName = someMethod(); // Clear and explicit

●You cannot use var without initialization

// Error: cannot infer type without initializer

// var total;

var total = 0; // OK

●It cannot be used for method parameters or return types

// public void print(var name) {} // Not allowed

// public var getName() {          // Not allowed

//   return "Alice";

// }

Benefits of Using var

●Reduces code verbosity

●Improves readability in some cases

In Java 10, the var keyword was introduced to simplify local variable declarations by allowing the compiler to infer the variable’s type from the value on the right-hand side of the assignment. This feature does not make Java a dynamically typed language; the type is still determined at compile time. When using var, it is best to do so where it improves the clarity of your code, rather than simply making it shorter. Additionally, variables declared with var must always be initialized at the time of declaration.

Here is an example of the use of the var keyword in a complete Java program.

1 public class VarExample {

2   public static void main(String[] args) {

3      // Using ‘var’ to declare local variables

4  

5      // inferred as String

6       var message = "Hello, Java 10 and beyond!";

7  

8      // inferred as int

9       var number = 42;                    

10

11      // inferred as double

12     var pi = 3.14159;

13

14      // inferred as boolean

15      var isJavaFun = true;               

16

17      // Printing the inferred variables

18      System.out.println(message);

19      System.out.println("Number: " + number);

20      System.out.println("Pi: " + pi);

21      System.out.println("Is Java fun? "

22                  + isJavaFun);

23     // Using ‘var’ in a loop

24     var fruits =

25        new String[] { "Apple", "Banana", "Cherry" };

26     

27     // ‘fruit’ inferred as String

28     for (var fruit : fruits) {

29        System.out.println("Fruit: " + fruit);

30     }

31

32     // 'var' with an object inferred as Person

33     var person = new Person("Lawrence", 25);

34     person.introduce();

35   }

36   }

37 

38  // A simple Person class

39  class Person {

40      private String name;

41      private int age;

42

43    Person(String name, int age) {

44         this.name = name;

45         this.age = age;

46  }

47

48  void introduce() {

49      System.out.println("Hi, I’m " + name +

50           " and I’m " + age + " years old.");

51   }

52 }

Key points in the example:

The program demonstrates how to use the var keyword in Java, which was introduced in Java 10\. The main purpose of var is to let the compiler automatically figure out the type of a variable based on the value you assign to it. This is called local variable type inference.

For example:

●var message = "Hello"; → the compiler knows message is a String.

●var number = 42; → the compiler knows number is an int.

●var pi = 3.14159; → the compiler knows pi is a double.

●var isJavaFun = true; → the compiler knows it’s a boolean.

Once the type is decided, it cannot be changed later. This means var does not make Java “dynamically typed”. It is still type-safe.

In the program, these values are printed to the console to show how var works.

Here’s the output of the given program saved as VarExample.java

Hello, Java 10 and beyond!

Number: 42

Pi: 3.14159

Is Java fun? true

Fruit: Apple

Fruit: Banana

Fruit: Cherry

Hi, I’m Lawrence and I’m 25 years old.

![image](Images/pin.jpg) Points to Remember

●var makes code shorter and easier to read while still being type-safe.

●You can use var inside methods, loops, and blocks.

●You cannot write var x; or var y = null; because the compiler would not know the type.

●var does not mean Java is dynamically typed — once the type is inferred, it is fixed.

![](Images/note.jpg) [Chapter Summary](#08_Contents.xhtml_sum1)

●Primitive data types are basic data types provided by Java. They store simple values directly in memory and are not objects.

●Reference data types include objects and arrays. They store references (memory addresses) to the actual data, rather than the data itself. Examples include instances of classes, interfaces, and arrays.

●User-defined classes are created by the programmer to represent custom data types. These are reference data types because variables of these classes hold references to objects created from the class, rather than the object data itself.

●When you assign one reference variable to another, both variables point to the same object in memory. Modifying the object through one reference will reflect when accessed through the other reference.

●In Java, method arguments are always passed by value. For primitive data types, the actual value is passed. For reference data types, the reference (memory address) is passed, but not the object itself.

●Local Variable Scopes: The scope of a local variable is limited to the block of code (e.g., method or loop) in which it is declared. It cannot be accessed outside this block.

●The this reference is a keyword in Java used within an instance method or constructor to refer to the current object. It helps to distinguish between instance variables and parameters with the same name, call other constructors, and return the current object instance.

# [CHAPTER 2](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch2)

# [An Introduction to Object-Oriented Concepts](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch2)

![](Images/klo.jpg)Key Learning Objectives

●Distinguish between a class and an object, and explain their relationship in Java.

●Instantiate objects using constructors and understand the role of the default constructor.

●Apply encapsulation to hide implementation details and safeguard object state.

●Differentiate between instance methods and static methods and identify when to use each.

●Describe the structure of a Java source file, including the use of package and import statements.

●Explain the purpose and structure of JAR files, manifest files, and Java API documentation.

In the world of Java programming, understanding object-oriented principles is essential to writing robust, reusable, and maintainable code. This chapter lays the foundation for object-oriented programming (OOP) by introducing the core concepts that define how Java applications are designed and structured. You will learn the differences between classes and objects, how to create and use them, and how encapsulation helps protect internal data. This chapter also covers constructors—including the rules of the default constructor—as well as the distinction between instance and static methods. Finally, we explore how Java source files are organized, how packages and imports work, and how to bundle and document your applications using JAR files, manifest files, and the Java API documentation.

## [2.1Objects vs Classes](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch2-1)

### 2.1.1What Are Objects?

Objects are anything real. They can be something conceptual or something physical. Anything around you right now is an object: the chairs, tables, books, and even your papers. But objects are not just limited to the physical things around us. When you use a desktop application or your favorite word processor, you also see objects in them. Buttons, toolbars, and even the work area or the dialog boxes are objects. Physical objects can be tangible or intangible, and visible, like Button and String, and invisible, like database connections and network connections.

Objects can also be something conceptual. Conceptual objects are objects that are not seen but exist in a meaningful way. These types of objects are also useful, and they represent a set of data as well. Examples of conceptual objects are errors, exceptions, database connections, network connections, triggered events, etc. We will discuss errors, exceptions, and events in a separate chapter. For now, it is important to understand that these are examples of conceptual objects.

### 2.1.2What Are Classes?

A class is the blueprint of an object. It is the declaration of data attributes and methods (also known as behavior) that each object will have every time it is being instantiated. The term "instantiate" is synonymous with the term "to create". So, every time you instantiate an object, you are actually creating an object from a class, and the said object will reside in the heap memory (where Java stores objects and instance variables dynamically at runtime, allowing for flexible management of memory during program execution). You can virtually create an unlimited number of objects from a class. Each object has its own identity. This means that whatever changes take place in one object, the other objects remain unaffected.

In older versions of Java (Java 7 and earlier), objects were always stored in the heap, while class metadata (information about classes, methods, etc.) was kept in a special part of the heap called PermGen (Permanent Generation).

Starting from Java 8, the PermGen area was removed. Objects are still stored in the heap, but class metadata is now stored in Metaspace, which lives in native memory (outside the heap). This change helps avoid memory issues that used to happen with PermGen.

In newer JVMs, the compiler can also optimize very short-lived objects so they don’t always end up in the heap—sometimes they may be replaced with variables or stored temporarily on the stack. But from a programmer’s perspective, you can think of objects as always living in the heap.

Here’s a clear comparison table to summarize Java Memory Structure: Java 7 vs. Java 8+.

Table 2.1Comparison of Java Memory Structures

| Memory Area | Java 7 and Earlier | Java 8 and Later |
| Heap | Stores all objects | Stores all objects (no change) |
| Stack | Stores method calls, local variables, and references. | Same as Java 7 (no change) |
| PermGen | Stores class metadata (class definitions, methods, static variables, etc.) | Removed in Java 8 |
| Metaspace | Not available | Replaced PermGen; stores class metadata in native memory |
| Garbage Collector (GC) | Cleans up heap and PermGen | Cleans up heap and Metaspace (if needed) |

Figure 2.1Diagram that shows the relationship between Classes and Objects

![image](Images/fig2-1.jpg)

Within this book, we will use several types of UML (Unified Modeling Language) diagrams to graphically represent several figures. In the example in [Figure 2.2](#15_Chapter02.xhtml_fig2-2), we have used a UML Class diagram that will depict the structure of a class. Other types of UML Diagrams are UML Package Diagram, UML State Diagram, etc.

Consider the example in [Figure 2.2](#15_Chapter02.xhtml_fig2-2).

Figure 2.2UML Diagram of class Person

![image](Images/fig2-2.jpg)

This UML will have the following Java Class equivalent:

public class Person

{

    public String name;

    public int age;

    public void showDetails() {...}

}

In the given UML Diagram, the top part (in grey) contains the name of the class, while the second part (white) contains the attributes or variables in the class, and the third part (black) contains the methods in the class. As for their accessibility, the plus (+) means public, while in the latter part of this chapter, you will also see a minus (-) symbol that is used to declare something as private. Both public and private access modifiers will be discussed later in a more detailed manner.

## [2.2Object Instantiation](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch2-2)

Object instantiation in Java is the process of creating an object from a class. You use the new keyword along with the class name and parentheses to call its constructor, which sets up the object. This creates a new instance that you can use to access the class's methods and properties.

Let us create two objects.

Person p1 = new Person();

Person p2 = new Person();

This means that p1 and p2 are reference variables that handle two different objects. Since you have two objects now, it means that each object will have its own copy of name and age attributes, as well as the showDetails() method. If you want to access a member (a member is another term for both attributes and methods), you can use the dot (.) notation.

For example, you would like to assign the name for the p1 and p2 objects, respectively:

p1.name = "Lawrence";

p2.name = "Walter";

Now, let us assign their respective ages:

p1.age = 20;

p2.age = 5;

What we've done so far is assign values to the attributes of p1 and p2. Now let us print their values:

System.out.println(p1.name + " at age " + p1.age);

System.out.println(p2.name + " at age " + p2.age);

This will give us an output of:

Lawrence at age 20

Walter at age 5

## [2.3Encapsulation - AKA Data Hiding and Implementation](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch2-3)

Encapsulation helps protect data by keeping it private and only allowing direct access. In Java, you can use the private keyword to restrict direct access to a variable, so no one can change it from outside the class. To allow safe access or changes, you use public methods, like getters and setters, which can check or validate values before updating the private variable. This ensures your data stays valid and secure.

What's the problem with this class declaration?

Figure 2.3UML Diagram of class Person with public variables

![image](Images/fig2-3.jpg)

What if we do this?

7    Person p1 = new Person();

8    Person p2 = new Person();

9

10    p1.name = "Lawrence";

11    p2.name = "Walter";

12

13    p1.age = 20;

14    p2.age = -5;

Will there be any programmatic problems? Will the compiler have a problem with line 14? No. This one will compile and run. The compiler should not have any problems on line 14, because the value -5 that you're assigning to p2.age is a valid integer value. The only problem that this code has is that it represents an unrealistic object.

Java is said to be an object-oriented programming language, which means it has the capability to mimic the behavior of real-life objects. In this case, a real person cannot have a negative value for age. In our real world, a person's age must be greater than or equal to zero.

So, how are we going to solve this? This is where encapsulation comes in. Encapsulation is an object-oriented feature that hides or protects data from direct access. So, instead of declaring all attributes as public, we can declare them as private to disable the direct accessibility of the attributes from other classes. Encapsulation helps protect your data by restricting its accessibility.

Figure 2.4UML diagram of class Person with private variables

![image](Images/fig2-4.jpg)

Now, the name and the age attributes are declared as private, which means these attributes can only be accessed within the class Person and cannot be accessed outside of this class, including the TestPerson class.

What if we assign values to name and age again? We will now have a compilation error because the name and age attributes were declared as private, which means accessing them directly from outside the class is not allowed.

10  p1.name = "Lawrence";// compilation error

11  p2.name = "Walter";// compilation error

12

13  p1.age = 20;// compilation error

14  p2.age = -5;// compilation error

The accessibility of a private variable is limited only within the same class where the variable is declared. So, if we declare the name and age attributes as private, how are we supposed to assign values to them and retrieve values from them?

The solution is to provide a method that is publicly accessible and allows access to private data; this is where a pair of "setters" and "getters" comes in.

Setters and getters are conventions used to create JavaBeans. They are also used to assign values to the name and age attributes and can provide verification checks as well.

A Java Bean is just a POJO—Plain Old Java Object—a conventional term for a well-encapsulated class. JavaBeans are not the same as Enterprise Java Beans (EJB). EJBs are the business component of a Java EE Application and are out of our scope.

JavaBeans are reusable software components in Java that follow certain rules, making them easy to use in different programs. One key rule is that a Java Bean must have private variables (fields) and provide public methods to access or modify these variables, known as "getters" and "setters". The getters let you read the value of a variable, while the setters allow you to change it. This structure ensures that data inside a bean can be managed securely and consistently across different parts of an application.

Here is an example of a Java Bean.

Figure 2.5UML Diagram of Java Bean class Person

![image](Images/fig2-5.jpg)

Translated into Java code, it will look like this:

1  // Person.java

2  public class Person {

3  

4      private String name;

5      private int age;

6  

7      // setters

8      public void setName(String n) {

9      

10          name = n;

11      }

12      public void setAge(int a) {

13      

14          if (a >= 0)

15                age = a;

16          else

17                age = 0;

18      }

19

20      // getters

21      public String getName() {

22    

23         return name;

24      }

25      public int getAge() {

26    

27         return age;

28    }

29

30  }

In this revised Person class, you will notice that even if you try to directly assign a negative value for the person's age using p2.age = -5, it will not be possible; a compilation error will occur. All values passed directly to the private variables from a different class will not be allowed. By providing public setters, all values that will be passed to the private variables will now be forced to be passed through the public setters. With this, you can now provide validation checks inside the public setters if needed. All values passed can either be checked or assigned directly.

Let's see our revised TestPerson class.

1    // TestPerson.java

2    public class TestPerson {

3  

4      public static void main(String[] args) {

5    

6        Person p1 = new Person();

7        Person p2 = new Person();

8    

9        // assign names

10        p1.setName("Lawrence");

11        p2.setName("Walter");

12  

13        // assign age

14        p1.setAge(30);

15        p2.setAge(-5);  

16  

17        System.out.println(p1.getName() + " is "

               + p1.getAge() + " years old.");

18        System.out.println(p2.getName() + " is "

               + p2.getAge() + " years old.");

19    }

20  }

21

In lines 10 and 11, as well as lines 14 and 15, values intended for name and age are now forced to pass through the public setters. Therefore, the Person class can perform validation checks on these values.

In retrieving the values of name and age, we will now be forced to call public getters.

17    System.out.println(p1.getName()+ " is "

                     + p1.getAge()+ " years old.");

18    System.out.println(p2.getName()+ " is "

                     + p2.getAge()+ " years old.");

Public getters are methods intended to return a value. Once called in lines 17 and 18, a String value and an integer value will be returned, respectively. The p1.getName() will return a String value, and p1.getAge() will return an integer value that will be printed via the System.out.println() method. The returned values of the getters can either be printed to the screen or stored in a variable. Once compiled and executed, it will have this output:

Lawrence at age 30
Walter at age 0

## [2.4The Constructor](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch2-4)

The constructor is a method-like construct that has the same name as your class name. Constructors do not have any return type, not even void.

Here's an example:

1 public class Person {

2

3   public Person (String n, int a) {

4  

5      name = n;

6      age = a;

7   }

8 }

Lines 3 to 7 are your constructor body. To create a Person object, you call your constructor this way.

Person p1 = new Person("Lawrence", 20);

Person p2 = new Person("Walter", 18);

Person p3 = new Person();    //compilation error

The Default Constructor's Rule:

"There is always at least one constructor for each class; if the writer does not provide a constructor, Java will provide the default, no-argument, empty-bodied constructor." For example:

public class MyClass {

    // nothing here

}

Does this class contain a constructor? The answer is YES. That is because of the default constructor's rule. This is the reason we can instantiate an object from this class. The given class will actually look like this after compilation:

1 public class MyClass {

2

3      // this is the default constructor

4      public MyClass() {        

5  

6        // nothing here

7      }

8 }

Lines 4 to 7 will be automatically inserted after compilation, but will only be present inside the class file and not inside the Java source file. The default constructor is provided so that even if the writer does not provide a constructor, we can still create an object from a class such as this one:

MyClass mc = new MyClass();

Creating an object from this class is actually a call to the default constructor provided by Java.

You are also allowed to have multiple constructors in a class. Having multiple constructors will allow you to have different ways to instantiate (or create) an object. Take, for example, the java.lang.Character class. It only contains a single constructor, which means you can create a Character object only one way. If you look at the java.lang.String class, it contains several constructors, which means that there are two different ways of creating a String object.

## [2.5Instance Methods and Static Methods](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch2-5)

Methods are used to create a subroutine that is called any number of times from the main method or from another method. A method can execute with or without passing values to them; these values are called arguments or sometimes called parameter lists.

Here is the syntax for declaring a method in Java:

<modifiers> <return_type> methodName(<parameter_list>)

{

    // method body

}

The common modifiers can be any of the following:

- public / private / protected

- static / abstract / final

The return_type can be any data type or even void.

The methodName can be any valid identifier

The parameter_list can be any variable and value pair. While calling an instance method, an object must first be created. An instance method is called through the created object. Given the sample instance method call:

p1.setName("Lawrence");

System.out.println(p1.getName());

int nameLength = p1.getName().length();

Instance variables and methods belong to an object and are used through that object. To access them, you first create an object of the class using the new keyword. Then, you can use the object’s name followed by a dot ('.') to call its methods or access its variables.

Whereas a static method is called through the class name.

String strNum = "100";

int num = Integer.parseInt(strNum);

The Integer class is found under the java.lang package and has a static method parseInt() that can accept a String integer format that will return an int value. Since the parseInt() method is static, we call the method through the name of the class, Integer.parseInt(strNum).

## [2.6The Java Source File Layout](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch2-6)

Just remember P-I-C, which stands for Package—Import-Classes. All these parts of the Java Source file are optional. You can have the following number of statements per part:

package --> 0 or 1 statement

import  --> 0 to many

classes  --> 0 to many

A Java source code can have multiple classes, but it is only allowed to have one public class. Once a public class is declared, then the filename of your Java source code must match the said public class.

### 2.6.1Java package Statement

Packages in Java refer to subdirectories. A package may contain classes or sub-packages. Take the diagram in [Figure 2.6](#15_Chapter02.xhtml_fig2-6) as an example.

Figure 2.6Sample Package Diagram

![image](Images/fig2-6.jpg)

We have MyProj as a package, and within MyProj, we have three sub-packages, namely: subA, subB, and test. And within subA, we have subAA as a sub-package.

A package may also contain classes inside it. The package subA contains A.class, subAA contains AA.class, package subB contains B.class, and package test contains TestMe.class, which serves as our test class.

In using packages, we also need to be aware of the following rules.

1.The basic syntax of a package is this:

package <top_level_pkg_name>[.<sub_pkg_name>]*;

Example:

package subA.subAA;

2.The package declaration can only be placed at the beginning of a Java Source File

3.Only one (1) package declaration per source file is allowed.

4.In the absence of the package statement, the generated class file will be placed in the default package (the present working directory).

5.Package statements must be separated by dots (.) to make the subdirectory representation platform-independent.

### 2.6.2The import Statement

Import statements are used to tell the compiler where to find the needed classes that your application will use. There are two packages that do not need any import statements:

1.the present working directory and;

2.the java.lang.*; package.

Classes inside the above-mentioned packages do not require any import statement declarations. However, classes found on other packages must be declared using an import statement. Here's an example:

1 import java.util.ArrayList;

2 import java.io.*;

3 import subA.subAA.AA;

Line 1 states that the ArrayList class can be accessed inside the java.util package, while Line 2 states that all classes under the java.io package can be used. The asterisk (*) signifies all classes, while line 3 states that this application is using the class AA, which is located under subA.subAA package.

Putting it all together, here's an example. This is the TestMe.java source file.

1    package test;

2  

3    import subA.*;

4    import subA.subAA.AA;

5    //import subB.B;

6  

7    public class TestMe {

8    

9       public static void main(String[] args) {

10    

11          A a = new A();

12          AA aa = new AA();

13

14          // fully-qualified name

15          subB.B b = new subB.B();  

16    

17          a.printMe();  

18          aa.printMe();

19          b.printMe();

20     }  

21  }

Line 1 states that once this source file compiles, the TestMe.class that will be generated will be placed under the test subdirectory.

Lines 3 and 4 state that classes A.java and AA.java are needed inside this application and are located under the declared packages.

Line 5 is commented out. If this is the case, you need to use the fully qualified name in line 15 so that you can still get access to the B.java source file. In effect, the import statements found on lines 3, 4, and 5 tell the compiler where to find the necessary classes so that you don't always use the fully-qualified name of a class. Instead of always using subB.B b = new subB.B(); you just have to use:

import subB.B;

....

B b = new B();

[Figure 2.7](#15_Chapter02.xhtml_fig2-7) shows a preview of the developer's directory structure.

Figure 2.7 Complete Project Directory Structure

![image](Images/fig2-7.jpg)

Compiling the test class TestMe.java under the src folder will also compile the rest of the *.java files within the src folder. This is because, inside the test class, there are dependencies with other Java source files.

To compile on Windows, use the following command:

MyProj\src> javac -d ..\classes test\TestMe.java

If this command is successful, the generated class files should be under the classes folder.

To run the sample application on Windows, you can type in:

MyProj\src> java -cp ..\classes test.TestMe

Here, the -cp ..\classes option sets the classpath to the classes directory so that the JVM knows where to find the compiled TestMe.class file. The fully qualified class name test.TestMe is used because the file is inside the test package.

For modern IDEs (Integrated Development Environment), you don’t have to worry about executing these commands;, they’re all automatically managed by your IDE.

## [2.7Introduction of modules](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch2-7)

When working with modules (introduced in Java 9), the idea is to group related packages together and describe them using a special file called module-info.java. For example, if your project has a module named myapp, you would create a module-info.java file inside the src\myapp folder. This file declares the module name and can also specify which packages it exports and which other modules it requires. To compile a module, you use a command like javac -d ..\out --module-source-path src src\myapp\module-info.java src\myapp\test\TestMe.java.

Here, the --module-source-path tells the compiler where to find the source modules, and -d ..\out saves the compiled classes in the out folder, keeping them separate from the source.

To run the program, you use a command such as java --module-path ..\out -m myapp/test.TestMe.

The --module-path tells the JVM where to find compiled modules, -m specifies the module and the class with the main method to run.

Table 2.2Packages vs. Modules in Java

| Feature | Packages | Modules |
| --- | --- | --- |
| What it is | A way to group related classes into folders. | A higher-level way to group packages into a single unit. |
| File needed | No special file required (just a folder with .java files). | Requires a module-info.java file that describes the module. |
| Organization | Keeps classes organized by functionality (e.g., test, utils). | Keeps whole packages organized and defines clear boundaries. |
| Visibility | Classes in a package can be imported by any other code. | A module can export only the packages it wants to share and hide others. |
| Dependencies | No direct way to state which packages are required. | module-info.java can declare which other modules are required. |
| Introduced in | Available since the very beginning of Java. | Introduced in Java 9 as part of Project Jigsaw. |
| Example | package test; → groups classes inside test. | module myapp { exports test; } → defines a module named myapp and exports test package. |

Packages are used to organize classes into folders so that related code is grouped together in a neat and structured way. Starting from Java 9, modules were introduced as a bigger building block — they allow you to group several packages into one unit and also control which parts of your code can be accessed by other modules. In short, packages help organize classes, while modules help organize packages.

## [2.8The JAR (Java Archive) File](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch2-8)

A complex application is normally composed of a set of class files and not just one class file. When these class files need to be delivered to our client, we need to put them inside one file called the JAR File or the Java Archive File. This JAR file represents a group of classes that work together.

Consider the given directory structure earlier. After a successful compilation, the generated class files are placed under the classes folder.

Figure 2.8 Partial Project Directory Structure, the classes folder

![image](Images/fig2-8.jpg)

These class files, which are under the classes folder, can now be archived. Archiving a set of classes can be done by the jar utility that comes with the JDK installation.

There are two ways to create a Jar File.

1.Without a manifest file

to create:

  jar cvf <JarFile.jar> <Files>

i.e.

  jar cvf MyJar1.jar *

to run:

  java -cp <JarFile.jar> <fullyQualifiedName>

i.e.

  java -cp MyJar1.jar test.TestMe

2.With a manifest file, also known as the executable Jar File.

to create:

  jar cvfm <JarFile.jar> <manifestFile> <Files>

i.e.

  jar cvfm MyJar2.jar manif.txt *

to run:

  java -jar <JarFile.jar>

i.e.

  java -jar MyJar2.jar

## [2.9The Manifest File](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch2-9)

A manifest file is a configuration file that is used by an archive file. All JAR files contain a manifest file. If the creator of the JAR file did not include their own manifest file, there will always be the default MANIFEST.MF file that contains the default configuration. By putting your own version of your manifest file, you can customize its behavior and override the default manifest file inside your JAR file.

The manifest file has four parts.

<Main-Class tag/s><separator><value><ENTERKEY>

A manifest file looks like this:

Manifest-Version: 1.0 <ENTERKEY>

Main-Class: test.TestMe <ENTERKEY>

Be sure to add a next line character after the last line so that during runtime, the Java Virtual Machine can read the said line input. In the absence of the ENTER KEY character in your manifest file, the JVM will treat this as an invalid manifest file because it will not be able to read its contents.

For most IDEs, the manifest file is also automatically created. You just have to edit it and include the fully qualified name of the test class in the Main-Class tag. This is required if you want the JAR file to be an executable (runnable) JAR file.

## [2.10Java API Documentation](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch2-10)

As discussed in Java Essentials Volume 1, the Java Runtime Environment (JRE) is composed of two main components:

1.The Java Virtual Machine (JVM) and the;

2.Application Program Interface (API)

To know the classes inside your API, you could download the API documentation on the Oracle® website.

You can also access the API documentation online via the URL:

[http://docs.oracle.com/javase/**<javaSEversion>**/docs/api/index.html](http://docs.oracle.com/javase/javaSEversion/docs/api/index.html)

Example:

[https://docs.oracle.com/javase/23/docs/api/index.html](https://docs.oracle.com/javase/23/docs/api/index.html)

Note: Links are also provided in the Online Resources provided along with the book.

Figure 2.9 Java 23 API Documentation Page

![image](Images/fig2-9.jpg)

Source: [https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/23/docs/api/index.html](https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/23/docs/api/index.html)

Try to use the Search bar on the upper right part of the screen and key in the name of the class, i.e., String. You should see the write-ups about the said class, the constructors, and the methods of the said class, and other details about it.

![](Images/note.jpg) [Chapter Summary](#08_Contents.xhtml_sum2)

In this chapter, we talked about the following:

●Objects can be physical or conceptual entities, representing real-world items or abstract concepts like database connections and network links. In software, objects include elements like buttons and toolbars, as well as errors and events. Classes, on the other hand, are blueprints for objects, defining their data attributes and behaviors. When a class is instantiated, it creates an object stored in heap memory, allowing flexible memory management. Multiple objects can be created from a single class, each with its own identity, unaffected by changes in others. UML diagrams, such as class, package, and state diagrams, are used to visually represent these structures.

●A constructor is a special method in Java that shares the same name as the class and has no return type. Every class has at least one constructor; if none is defined by the programmer, Java automatically provides a default, no-argument constructor. This allows objects to be created even if the class appears empty. For instance, when an object of 'MyClass' is instantiated, Java uses the default constructor to enable this, ensuring smooth object creation.

●Methods in Java are reusable blocks of code that can be called from the main method or other methods, with or without passing values (called arguments or parameters). Methods can have various access modifiers (like public, private, or protected) and can be instance methods (requiring an object to be called) or static methods (called directly through the class name). For example, p1.setName("Lawrence") is an instance method called on an object, while Integer.parseInt(strNum) is a static method accessed via the class Integer.

●A Java Source File Layout can have either one package statement or none at all. For import statements, you can have multiple import statements or none at all. For classes, you can have multiple non-public class declarations or have a single public class with several non-public classes or none at all. Packages, import statements, and class declarations are all optional. If they are present, their sequence must start with packages, followed by import statements, and then the list of classes.

● A complex application is normally composed of a set of class files and not just one class file. When these class files need to be delivered to our client, we need to put them inside one file called the JAR File or the Java Archive File. This JAR file represents a group of classes that work together.
