# [CHAPTER 3](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch3)

# [Inheritance](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch3)

![](Images/klo.jpg)Key Learning Objectives

●Define and implement inheritance in Java using extends.

●Distinguish between superclasses, subclasses, and the "is-a" relationship.

●Override methods safely following the rules of overriding.

●Overload methods and constructors while adhering to overloading rules.

●Utilize super to refer to superclass members and constructors.

●Explain the role of Object and override its methods toString(), equals(), and hashCode().

This chapter covers the fundamentals of inheritance in Java, from understanding the "is-a" relationship and single inheritance to accessing members of a parent class with the super keyword. It focuses on the essential principles of how subclasses are created based on existing classes, allowing for code reuse and clear hierarchies. You’ll learn how to override and overload methods, handle constructors in subclasses, and appreciate the role of Object—the root of all Java classes—through its toString(), equals(), and hashCode() methods. We will also explore the significance of access control and method behavior, particularly when extending functionality, and how these techniques come together to form a strong, reusable, and well-structured code base.

## [3.1Introduction to Inheritance](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch3-1)

Inheritance is another fundamental concept of object-oriented programming. The idea behind inheritance is that you can create new classes that are built on existing ones. When you inherit from an existing class, you reuse (or inherit) its methods and variables, and you add new methods and variables to adapt your new class to new situations. This technique is essential in most object-oriented programming languages, particularly in Java.

If you are coming from a procedure-oriented language like C, Visual Basic, or COBOL, you will want to read this chapter carefully. For experienced C++ programmers or those coming from another object-oriented language like Smalltalk, this chapter will seem largely familiar. There are many differences between how inheritance is implemented in Java and how it is done in C++ or in other object-oriented languages.

## [3.2Classes, Superclasses, and Subclasses](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch3-2)

Let’s look at the Employee class.

Figure 3.1 The Employee class

![image](Images/fig3-1.jpg)

Suppose you are currently an employee object. As an Employee, you have the following attributes: name, salary, birthDate, and a method that will display all your information, let’s call it getDetails().

public class Employee {

  public String name = "";

  public double salary;

  public Date birthDate;

  public String getDetails() {...}

}

Managers, in most cases, are also Employees. Both Employees and Managers have their respective names, salaries, and birthDates. On top of these attributes, Managers also have their assigned departments to manage and run.

Figure 3.2 The Manager class

![image](Images/fig3-2.jpg)

public class Manager {

     public String name = "";

     public double salary;

     public Date birthDate;

     public String department;

     public String getDetails() {...}

}

This is the kind of situation that cries out for inheritance. Inheritance is a way to reuse an existing class by extending it to be its superclass (aka parent class). In our example, the Manager class is inheriting the Employee class, which means that whatever the Employee class has, the Manager class will also have.

Figure 3.3 The Employee and Manager class

![image](Images/fig3-3.jpg)

## [3.3The "is-a" Relationship](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch3-3)

In all inheritance cases, there is an obvious "is–a" relationship between the parent class and the child class. In Java, an "is-a" relationship means that a subclass is a specific type of its superclass. It implies a hierarchical connection where the subclass inherits characteristics and behaviors from the superclass.

For Java technology, we will use the terms superclass and subclass. The Manager and Employee classes are examples of an “is-a” relationship. Every manager is an employee. This “is–a” relationship is a way to show inheritance.

Here is how you define a Manager class that inherits from the Employee class. You use the Java keyword extends to denote inheritance.

This is the syntax to apply inheritance:

class <SubClass> extends <SuperClass> {

  // added fields, constructors, and methods

}

To apply the given syntax in the Employee and Manager classes, we can write the inheritance relationship this way:

class Manager extends Employee {

  // added fields, constructors, and methods

}

This shows that the Manager class is the subclass and the Employee class is the superclass, and if we apply the "is-a" relationship to both classes, we can say that a Manager "is an" Employee.

Figure 3.4 The Manager class extending the Employee class

![image](Images/fig3-4.jpg)

// Employee.java

public class Employee {

     public String name = "";

     public double salary;

     public Date birthDate;

     public String getDetails() {...}

}

// Manager.java

public class Manager extends Employee {

     public String department;

}

The "is-a" relationship is not just limited to the immediate subclass; it can go further down the hierarchy.

## [3.4Single Inheritance](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch3-4)

In the Java Programming Language, we can only implement single inheritance, which means you can only inherit from a single superclass (parent class). Although it does not limit the superclass to have a single subclass, it can have multiple subclasses.

Notice the UML diagram of the classes in [Figure 3.5](#16_Chapter03.xhtml_fig3-5). Each class only has one superclass. In this UML, you will also notice that a superclass has multiple subclasses, like the class Employee has several direct subclasses like Manager, Engineer, and Secretary, while the class Director is a subclass of the class Manager, which is also a subclass of the Employee class. To apply the "is-a" relationship, we can therefore say that the Director "is-a" Manager. It is also true to say that the Director "is an" Employee. The Director may not be an immediate subclass of the Employee class, but it still belongs to the same hierarchy, so a Director "is an" Employee as well.

Figure 3.5 Single inheritance hierarchy example

![image](Images/fig3-5.jpg)

In C++ and other object-oriented programming languages, multiple inheritance is allowed, but not in Java. This begs the question: Is Java inferior to C++? The answer is "NO". Multiple inheritance has several drawbacks. Take [Figure 3.6](#16_Chapter03.xhtml_fig3-6) for example.

Figure 3.6 Multiple inheritance concept

![image](Images/fig3-6.jpg)

Let us say that class A and class B are both parent classes, and they have a common method called doStuff() that contains two different method implementations. Another class named class C is a child class of both class A and class B.

Which doStuff() method will be inherited by class C? The doStuff() method is present in both parent classes. This causes the problem as to which doStuff() method should class C inherit, i.e., should it inherit the doStuff() method in class A or the one from class B.

This is a potential problem that can occur with multiple inheritance. This is NOT allowed in Java, but in other programming languages like C++ and Python, this is allowed. Multiple inheritance can lead to errors or ambiguity in design.

In Java, we can simulate multiple inheritance by using interfaces. Interfaces and their advantages will be discussed in [Chapter 5](#18_Chapter05.xhtml). Simply put, Java requires more organization to ensure it is more robust when it comes to object-oriented concepts such as inheritance.

## [3.5The Java Access Modifiers](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch3-5)

Access modifiers are used for classes, methods, and fields. It tells the compiler the extent of accessibility it has. We have four access modifiers in Java. [Table 3.1](#16_Chapter03.xhtml_tab3-1) presents a summary of these modifiers and their respective accessibility.

Table 3.1 Java access modifiers

| Access Modifiers | Same Class | Same Package | Subclass | Anywhere |
| private | ![image](Images/tic.jpg) | ![image](Images/wrong.jpg) | ![image](Images/wrong.jpg) | ![image](Images/wrong.jpg) |
| <default> | ![image](Images/tic.jpg) | ![image](Images/tic.jpg) | ![image](Images/wrong.jpg) | ![image](Images/wrong.jpg) |
| protected | ![image](Images/tic.jpg) | ![image](Images/tic.jpg) | ![image](Images/tic.jpg) | ![image](Images/wrong.jpg) |
| public | ![image](Images/tic.jpg) | ![image](Images/tic.jpg) | ![image](Images/tic.jpg) | ![image](Images/tic.jpg) |

Once an inner class, a method, or a variable is declared as private, its accessibility is limited only within the class where it was declared. Top-level classes are not allowed to be declared private. If none of the access modifiers are used, it implicitly means that you are using the <default> access modifier, which limits its accessibility inside the class where it was declared and within the same package-private (sub-directory).

The protected access modifier, on the other hand, allows the accessibility to be limited only within the same class, same package, or within the same subclasses, even if they are in different packages. A construct that uses the protected access modifier cannot be accessed outside the same package or by classes that are not a subclass.

Lastly, the public access modifier can be accessed anywhere. It has universal accessibility. Top-level classes can only have a <default> or public accessibility. Inner classes, variables, and methods can have any of the aforementioned access modifiers.

## [3.6Method Overriding](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch3-6)

There are certain scenarios when you inherit a method from a superclass, but you realize that the method you have inherited is not quite enough to perform the duties required by the subclass. To resolve this dilemma, you can override the method you have inherited.

// Employee.java

1   public class Employee {

2    public String name = "";

3    public double salary;

4    public Date birthDate;

5    public String getDetails() {

6         return "Name: " + name +

7            "\nSalary: " + salary;

8    }

9 }

// Manager.java

1 public class Manager extends Employee {

2    public String department;

3    @Override

4    public String getDetails() {

5         return "Name: " + name +

6            "\nSalary: " + salary +

7            "\nDepartment: " + department;

8    }

9 }    

In this example, the getDetails() method of the Manager class overrides the getDetails() method of the Employee class. It is because the Manager’s getDetails() method is supposed to give out more information than the inherited getDetails() method of the Employee class.

## [3.7Rules for Overriding a Method](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch3-7)

In the source code given, we can say that the getDetails() method of the Manager class is the overriding method. In overriding a method, a couple of rules need to be observed.

1.The overriding method must have the same name.

●For example, in the given example, both the Employee class and the Manager class have the getDetails() method.

2.The overriding method must have the same argument list.

●In this example, both the getDetails() method of the Employee and the Manager class have an empty argument list.

3.The return type of the overriding method can be the same.

●In this example, both the return type of the getDetails() method of the Employee and the Manager classes are String objects.

4.The overriding method must not be less accessible.

●In the Employee class, the getDetails() method was declared as public; this means that the getDetails() method of the subclass must also be declared public.

5.The return type of the overriding method can be a subclass of the return type of the method of the superclass.

●In this example, the return type of the getDetails() method is of type String, and there are no subclasses of the String class.

Assuming the getDetails() method returns a non-final object, say a non-String object, then it is possible for the overriding getDetails() method to have a different return type. Here is another alternative version of method overriding to emphasize rule #5:

    // Employee.java

    1  public class Employee {

          . . .

    5    public Employee getDetails() {

             // returns Employee object...

    8    }

    9 }

    // Manager.java

    1  public class Manager extends Employee {

          . . .

          @Override

    3    public Manager getDetails() {

             // returns a Manager object  

    7    }

    8 }

A subclass can modify the implementation of the inherited method with a different method implementation than the superclass’s method, but it must have the same name, same argument list, and return type. But for Java 5.0 and newer versions the return type of the overriding method can be a subclass of the return type of the superclass’s method. Also, the access modifier of the overriding method must not be less accessible.

## [3.8The super Keyword](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch3-8)

In most cases, overriding a method can produce redundancies, which is a poor programming practice. Having redundancies in your code slows down both the development and debugging time by increasing code issues during renaming or refactoring of code, and it also slows down your runtime.

To minimize the problem of redundancy, we can use the super keyword. The super keyword is used in a class to refer to its superclass’s member data – both attributes and methods. The behavior does not have to be in the immediate superclass; it can be further up in the hierarchy.

Here are sample codes for both the Employee and Manager classes with overriding methods that have redundancies in the getDetails() method.

 // Employee.java

1 public class Employee {

2    public String name = "";

3    public double salary;

4    public Date birthDate;

5    public String getDetails() {

6         return "Name: " + name +

7           "\nSalary: " + salary;

8    }

9  }

// Manager.java

1 public class Manager extends Employee {

2   public String department;

3     @Override

4   public String getDetails() {

5        return "Name: " + name + // redundant! Bad.

6           "\nSalary: " + salary +   // redundant! Bad.

7           "\nDepartment: " + department;

8    }

9  }

Here’s a revised version of the Employee and Manager code:

// Employee.java

1 public class Employee {

2    public String name = "";

3    public double salary;

4    public Date birthDate;

5    public String getDetails() {

6         return "Name: " + name +

7           "\nSalary: " + salary;

8    }

9 }

// Manager.java

1 public class Manager extends Employee {

2    public String department;

3    @Override

4    public String getDetails() {

5         return super.getDetails() +   // this is better.

6             "\nDepartment: " + department;

7    }

8 }  

In line 5 of the Manager class, we used super.getDetails() to call the getDetails() method of the Employee class instead of copying lines 6 and 7, which allows us to get rid of the code redundancy.

## [3.9Overloading Methods](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch3-9)

In most object-oriented programming languages, we can declare multiple methods with the same name and different argument list and with different method implementations. This language feature is known as method overloading.

Figure 3.7 Screenshot of the Java API documentation - PrintStream class method summary

![image](Images/fig3-7.jpg)

Source: [https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/23/docs/api/java.base/java/io/PrintStream.html](https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/23/docs/api/java.base/java/io/PrintStream.html)

In the Java API documentation, there are several overloaded methods in most of the Java classes. For example, in [Figure 3.7](#16_Chapter03.xhtml_fig3-7), if you will examine the PrintStream class under the java.io package, you will notice that there are several declarations of the print() and println() methods, all having a different argument values and because of this we can conveniently use the println() method in printing any value, may it be an int, a double, or even a String value. If we need to print a value, we just call System.out.println() method and Java will then match the value that was passed to the println() method to the corresponding println() method in the PrintStream class. No need to check on the argument type. Because the print() and the println() methods are overloaded methods that can accommodate any type of data that needs to be printed.

Overloaded methods can be declared within the same class or within the same class hierarchy. This feature enables us to have a convenient way of writing and calling methods that may have a different set of arguments.

## [3.10Rules for Method Overloading](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch3-10)

If you want to write your own overloaded method, you may want to consider the following rules:

1.All overloaded methods must have the same method name.

2.All overloaded methods must have a different parameter/argument list.

3.As for the return type, all overloaded methods may or may not have the same return type.

In the sample code below, we have created several versions of the sum() method that is supposed to compute the sum of all integer values passed as a parameter. Here’s a sample code that shows method overloading:

1   public class Overloading1 {

2  

3  public static void main(String[] args){

4  

5    System.out.println(sum(10, 20));

6    System.out.println(sum(10, 20, 30));

7    System.out.println(sum(10, 20, 30, 40));

8  }

9  public static int sum(int num1, int num2) {

10  

11    return num1 + num2;

12  }

13  public static int sum(int num1,

14            int num2, int num3) {

15    return num1 + num2 + num3;

16  }

17  public static int sum(int num1, int num2,

18            int num3, int num4) {

19  

20      return num1 + num2 + num3 + num4;

21    }

22 }

In this example, we see that there are several declarations of the sum method, all of which can be seen in lines 9, 13, and 17.

If the user then needs to add up 2, 3, or 4 integer values, all we need to do is to call a sum() method and pass the integer values as arguments, all the declared sum() methods will accept the int values passed to it and compute for the sum and return the integer sum of all parameters passed to it. The only limitation is that the sum() methods in the given code can only accept 2, 3, or 4 int values. It would be impractical to write a method for every number of int values.

To overcome limitations like this, a new language feature was introduced in Java SE 5.0, it is called var-args (variable arguments). This means that any number of arguments can now be passed to a var-args method.

Every new language feature comes with a handful of rules. Here are the rules for declaring var-args methods:

1.There can only be one var-args per method.

Correct:

protected void doStuff(double… args){}

public static void main(String… args){}

public void doSomething(int… args){}

Incorrect:

protected void doStuff(double… arg1, int… args2){}

// Java does not allow multiple varargs parameters in

// a single method. Only one varargs parameter is

// allowed, and it must be the last parameter.

public static void main(String… args[]){}

// The syntax incorrectly combines varargs (...)

// and array notation ([])

public void doSomething(int[] args…){}

// Placing the varargs (...) after the array

// is incorrect. The proper way is int... args.

2.If there are multiple parameters, the var-arg parameter must be the last parameter in the list of parameters.

Correct:

protected void doStuff(String str, Object… args){}

public void doSomething(char ch, int… args){}

Incorrect:

protected void doSomething (double… arg1, int args2){}

// Varargs (...) must be the last parameter in a

// method signature. Here, double... arg1 is

// incorrectly placed before int args2.

public void doStuff(String param1, char… param2, int param3){}

// Similar issue, char... param2 should be the

// final parameter, but an int parameter follows

// it. Varargs can only appear at the end.

Here’s a sample code that uses var-args:

1 public class Overloading2 {

2

3   public static void main(String[] args) {

4  

5       System.out.println(sum(10, 20));

6       System.out.println(sum(10, 20, 30));

7       System.out.println(sum(10, 20, 30, 40));

8       System.out.println(sum(1.0, 2.0, 3.0, 4.0));

9   }

10   public static int sum(int... num) {

11  

12     int total = 0;

13     for (int n : num) {

14    

15        total += n;

16    }

17    return total;

18   }  

19

20   public static int sum(double... num) {

21  

22    double total = 0;

23    for (double n : num)

24    {

25        total += n;

26    }

27    return total;

28   }

29 }

In line 10, the argument list is now using the var-args notation. The method sum() can now accept any number of int values in the method sum(). It can now accept 2 numbers, 3 numbers, 4 numbers, 10 numbers, and even a hundred numbers. After the sum() method receives all the int values, these values will be treated as an array of int inside the sum() method. There’s also line 20, where we can accept any number of double values in the method sum().

A common mistake in calling the var-args method is when an array value is passed. Typically, they accept primitive data types and then implicitly convert them into an array once the values are read inside the var-args method, as you can see in line 12, where an enhanced for loop was used to traverse the values.

## [3.11Inheritance and Constructors](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch3-11)

We mentioned that inheritance is a feature that allows you to carry over methods and variables to a subclass. This is true only for methods and variables, but not with constructors. In Java, constructors are not inherited by subclasses.

Consider this example:

// ConstructorChaining.java

1  class A {

2  

3    A() {

4  

5        System.out.println("A");  

6    }  

7  }

8  class B extends A {

9  

10     B() {

11  

12            System.out.println("B");  

13    }  

14  }

15  class C extends B {

16

17    C() {

18  

19            System.out.println("C");  

20    }  

21  }

22

23  public class ConstructorChaining {

24

25    public static void main(String[] args) {

26  

27        C c = new C();

28    }

29 }

After instantiating the object of class C, you will notice that the output will be:

Output:
A
B
C

The output gives the impression that the superclass constructors were inherited; however, this is untrue.

There is a rule with regard to inheritance and constructors. Constructors are not inherited, but they are chained. We have what we call constructor-chaining.

The given example above is transformed after compilation by inserting the super() keyword in the first line of each constructor.

Here is the updated version of the given code. In this version, Java has already made some changes.

1 class A extends Object {

2

3    A() {

4  

5       super();

6        System.out.println("A");  

7    }  

8  }

9  class B extends A {

10

11    B() {

12  

13        super();

14        System.out.println("B");  

15    }  

16  }

17  class C extends B {

18

19    C() {

20  

21        super();

22        System.out.println("C");  

23    }  

24  }

25

26  public class ConstructorChaining {

27

28    public static void main(String[] args) {

29  

30        C c = new C();

31    }

32  }

Once compiled and run, it will have the output:

A
B
C

This code will have the same output as the previous one.

Notice lines 5, 13, and 21\. The keyword super() was inserted in the first line of each constructor.

As a rule, if a constructor does not have a call to this() or a call to super(), Java will add a default call to super() in the first line of the constructor, just like what you had in lines 5, 13, and 21\. The call to super() allows the current constructor to call the constructor in the superclass. This is where the term constructor-chaining was coined.

How about the changes we had in line 1?

1  class A extends Object

Another rule. If a class does not extend any superclass, it automatically extends the class Object. All classes in the API and the user-defined classes will have this rule. The class Object is basically the root of all class hierarchies.

## [3.12Overloading Constructors](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch3-12)

In [Chapter 2](#15_Chapter02.xhtml), we had a discussion about Constructors. We said that constructors are method-like constructs and, just like methods, constructors can also be overloaded. Here is a sample code that demonstrates constructor overloading.

// Employee.java

1  public class Employee {

2

3     private String name;

4     private MyDate birthDate;

5     private double salary;

6     private static final double BASE_SALARY = 15000.00;

7

8    // Constructor

9   public Employee(String name, double salary,

                  MyDate DoB) {

10

11      this.name = name;

12      this.salary = salary;

13      this.birthDate = DoB;

14   }

15   public Employee(String name, double salary) {

16  

17     this(name, salary, null);

18   }

19   public Employee(String name, MyDate DoB) {

20  

21     this(name, BASE_SALARY, DoB);

22   }  

23   public String getDetails() {

24  

25   return "Name: " + name + "\nSalary: "

26     + salary + "\nBirth Date: " + birthDate;

27   }

28 }

The Employee class has three constructors, just like the rule for method overloading, all constructors must have different argument lists.

Using this() or super() is optional for all constructors. The call to this(), is actually a call to a fellow constructor or a constructor within the same class, while a call to super() is a call to the constructor of the superclass.

The Employee class has three constructors, just like the rule for method overloading, all constructors must have different argument lists.

Using this() or super() is optional for all constructors. The call to this(), is actually a call to a fellow constructor or a constructor within the same class, while a call to super() is a call to the constructor of the superclass.

If the call to this() or the call to super() is used, they must be placed on the first line of the constructor. Placing them elsewhere will cause a compilation error. But in the absence of either of the two, a default call to super() will be placed on the first line of the constructor.

Here is the Manager class, which demonstrates the call to super().

// Manager.java

1  public class Manager extends Employee {

2  

3     private String dept;

4

5     public Manager(String name,

6                double salary, String dept) {   

7          super(name, salary);

8          this.dept = dept;

9     }

10     /* This code will fail. A default call

11       to super() will be in place

12     public Manager(String dept) {     

13       this.dept = dept;

14     }  

15     */

16     // additional Manager code...

17  }

In line 7 of the Manager class, a call to super passes the name and salary values to the constructor in the Employee class. This will allow you to reuse one of the constructors in the Employee class, thus removing the possibility of having redundancies in your code.

As for line 12 of the Manager class, it will cause an error because the first line of the constructor does not have a call to this() or a call to super(), therefore Java will put a call to super() in the first line of the constructor, telling it to look for a constructor in its superclass (in this case, the class Employee) to run a constructor that does not have any arguments.

## [3.13The Object Class](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch3-13)

The Object class is the root of all classes. Whether it is a pre-written class in the API or a user-defined class, ALL classes are a subclass of the Object class.

And because of these, all methods in the Object class are implicitly inherited by all classes, so there is no need to use "extends Object". Methods like equals(), hashCode(), and the toString()method.

Consider the class Person.

1 // Person.java

2 public class Person extends Object {

3  

4    private String name;

5    private int age;

6    

7    public Person(String name, int age) {

8    

9       this.name = name;

10       this.age = age;

11  }

12  // getters

13  public String getName() {

14  

15       return name;

16  }

17  public int getAge() {

18  

19       return age;

20  }

21 }

Since the class Person is a user-defined class, there is still no user-defined implementation of the equals(), hashCode(), and toString()methods. The current implementation of these methods is the one inherited from the Object class.

In Java, comparing objects using the == operator and the .equals() method can produce different results. By default, they have the same behavior. The equals() method is inherited from the Object class and compares based on the == operation. The == operator checks if two object references point to the same memory location, while the .equals() method is typically used to compare the content or logical equality of objects. The equals() method is typically overridden when creating a user-defined class.

In Java, comparing objects using the == operator and the .equals() method can produce different results. The == operator checks if two object references point to the same memory location, while the .equals() method is typically used to compare the content or logical equality of objects.

The following code demonstrates this concept by creating two Person objects with identical attributes and comparing them using both approaches.

Consider the given code snippet below that creates two Person objects, p1 and p2, and compares them using the == operator and the equals() method.

7  Person p1 = new Person("Lawrence", 30);

8  Person p2 = new Person("Lawrence", 30);

9    

10  System.out.println(p1 == p2);

11  System.out.println(p1.equals(p2));

The output of lines 10 and 11 will be:

false
false

Ideally, since p1 and p2 have the same name and age, the output should be true. But since the implementation of the equals() method came from the Object class, we will have both statements print false values because the equals() implementation of the Object class is the same as the == implementation.

As for the hashCode() method output of both objects, it can be printed by inserting this:

14   System.out.println(p1.hashCode());

15   System.out.println(p2.hashCode());

A possible output of lines 14 and 15 will be:

2018699554
1311053135

The hashCode() method is tied to object identity by default, unless you explicitly override it. If you do not override the hashCode() in your class, then two different objects (p1 and p2), even if they have the same attributes and values, will usually produce different hash codes because the default hashCode() is based on the object’s memory addresses, as seen in the output above.

If you do override hashCode() properly (along with equals()), then objects with the same attribute values should return the same hash code. This is important for collections like HashMap, HashSet, and Hashtable to work correctly.

If two objects are equal according to equals(), they must return the same hashCode(). But if two objects have the same data without overriding equals() and hashCode(), they are still considered different objects with potentially different hash codes.

Another problem with the current Person objects is the String representations of both p1 and p2. If we try to print the values of p1 and p2 by doing this:

16   System.out.println(p1);       // same as //p1.toString()

17   System.out.println(p2.toString());

We will have this output:

Person@7852e922
Person@4e25154f

These are actually the hexadecimal values of their generated hashCode values.

So far, the method implementation we got from the inherited equals(), hashCode(), and toString() methods from the Object class is not the one that we needed.

To provide our own implementation of these methods, we need to override the equals(), hashCode(), and toString() methods from the Object class. Consider the updated version of the Person class.

1  // Person.java

2  public class Person extends Object {

3  

4       private String name;

5       private int age;

6  

7       // constructor

8       public Person(String name, int age) {

9    

10          this.name = name;

11          this.age = age;

12     }

13

14     // getters

15     public String getName() {

16    

17          return name;

18     }

19     public int getAge() {

20    

21          return age;

22     }

23

24     // override the equals method

         @Override

25     public boolean equals(Object obj) {

26    

27          boolean result = false;

28          if (obj != null && obj instanceof Person) {

29        

30              Person p = (Person) obj;    

31              if (this.getName().equals(p.getName())

32               && this.getAge() == p.getAge()) {

33          

34                result = true;

35              }

36         }

37         return result;

38     }

39  

40     // override the hashCode method

       @Override

41     public int hashCode() {

42    

43          return getName().hashCode() ^ getAge();

44     }

45

46     // override the toString method

       @Override

47     public String toString() {

48    

49         return getName() + ", at age " + getAge();

50     }

51  }

To test the output, we create a test class called TestPerson.java with the following code:

1  // TestPerson.java

2  public class TestPerson {

3

4     public static void main(String[] args) {

5  

6          Person p1 = new Person("Lawrence", 30);

7          Person p2 = new Person("Lawrence", 30);

8

9          System.out.println(p1 == p2);

10          System.out.println(p1.equals(p2));

11

12          System.out.println(p1.hashCode());

13          System.out.println(p2.hashCode());

14

15          System.out.println(p1);  

16          System.out.println(p2.toString());

17     }

18  }

If we compile and run this code, we should have this output:

false
true
2314851
2314851
Lawrence, at age 30
Lawrence, at age 30

The first two lines in the output:

false
true

With the equals() method implementation in the Person.java, these are outputs for lines 9 and 10 of the TestPerson.java code.

9    System.out.println(p1 == p2);

10    System.out.println(p1.equals(p2));

The hashCode() values of both p1 and p2 are now identical because of the hashCode() implementation in the Person class.

As for the toString() method, you can override the toString() method of the Object class if you want your object to be printed using your preferred format.

![](Images/note.jpg) [Chapter Summary](#08_Contents.xhtml_sum3)

●Introduction to Inheritance was discussed, which was one of the fundamental pillars of OOP. This promotes code reuse.

●Classes, superclasses, and subclasses are essential concepts that one should know when talking about the concepts of inheritance. In other OOPs, certain terms can be used instead, like parent and child classes. But in Java, we use the terms superclass and subclass.

●The "is-a" relationship is another term that is also used in software engineering. In some cases, we use the term ‘a subclass of’ or ‘derived from’.

●Java is unique among other object-oriented programming languages in that it uses the concept of single inheritance. However, we will talk about how to use the ideas of multiple inheritance in Java in Chapter 5: Other Class Features.

●In chapter two, we have introduced the public and private access modifiers to explain the concepts of encapsulation, but in this chapter, we have discussed all four access modifiers.

●Method overriding in Java occurs when a subclass provides a specific implementation of a method that is already defined in its superclass. This allows the subclass to customize or completely replace the behavior of that method while maintaining the same method signature. There are five method overriding rules discussed in this chapter.

●The super keyword is used within a subclass to refer to its immediate superclass. It can be used to call the superclass's constructor, access superclass methods, or access superclass fields, thereby allowing the subclass to inherit and utilize the functionality of its superclass.

●Overloading methods, sometimes called method overloading in other resources, allows a class to have multiple methods with the same name but different parameters. This provides flexibility by enabling different ways to call a method based on the number or types of parameters passed, improving code readability and reusability within the same class. We also discussed the rules for method overloading in this chapter.

●In Java, inheritance and constructors are closely related but have distinct roles. When a subclass is instantiated, its constructor implicitly or explicitly calls the constructor of its superclass to ensure proper initialization of the inherited fields. This is typically done using the super keyword, which must be the first statement in the subclass constructor if used to call the superclass constructor. We also looked at the use of the super() and the use of this() in this chapter.

●Overloading constructors in Java allows a class to have multiple constructors with different parameter lists. This provides flexibility in creating objects with various initial states, as each constructor can perform different initialization tasks based on the arguments passed during object creation.

●The Object class, which is the root of all classes, was also discussed. It is also suggested that when you are creating your own class, it is better to override the toString(), equals(), and the hashCode() methods in your own user-defined class.

# [CHAPTER 4](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch4)

# [Polymorphism](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch4)

![](Images/klo.jpg)Key Learning Objectives

●Learn the concept of polymorphism

●Virtual method invocation

●Heterogeneous array

●Methods with polymorphic arguments

●The instanceof operator

●Casting of objects

This chapter introduces the concept of polymorphism, one of the core features in object-oriented programming (OOP) that allows objects of different classes to be treated as instances of a common superclass. We'll explore how virtual method invocation enables dynamic method resolution at runtime, enhancing flexibility in code. You’ll learn how to work with heterogeneous arrays and use methods that accept polymorphic arguments, broadening the scope of what functions can handle. Additionally, we will cover the instanceof operator for type-checking and how object casting is used to convert between different types within an inheritance hierarchy.

## [4.1Introduction to the Concept of Polymorphism](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch4-1)

Polymorphism was derived from two Greek words, "poly", which means many, and "morph", which means forms. So, polymorphism can literally mean "having many forms". In Java, the object itself is non-polymorphic, while a reference variable that holds the object is the one that is polymorphic. This means that in Java, an object is just a specific thing created from a class, like a car made from a blueprint. That object is not flexible—it is what it is.

However, a reference variable (the variable that points to the object) can be flexible. If the reference variable is of a parent class, it can point to different types of objects that belong to its family (like a Vehicle variable pointing to a Car or Bike). This is called polymorphism—the ability of one reference type to represent different object types. Think of it like a universal remote control that can control different devices. The remote is like the reference variable, and the devices (TV, AC, DVD player) are like the objects.

In our example for this chapter, I’ll be using the Employee class, referring to different types of Employees like Managers, Directors, Engineers, and Secretaries. Let us consider the hierarchy in [Figure 4.1](#17_Chapter04.xhtml_fig4-1) again.

Figure 4.1 Example of single inheritance hierarchy

![image](Images/fig4-1.jpg)

Here's an example of how polymorphism is used.

5   Employee e = new Manager();// this is allowed

6   e.department = "Sales";// this is illegal

7   System.out.println(e.getDetails());// this is ok!    

Storing an instance of the Manager class as an Employee type leverages polymorphism, allowing you to treat all employees uniformly regardless of their specific roles. Since Manager is a subclass of Employee, it inherits the basic characteristics of an employee while adding unique features. This is particularly useful in scenarios where you need to manage various employee types collectively, such as in a payroll system where a list of Employee objects may include managers, developers, and interns. By generalizing behavior through the Employee type, your code becomes more flexible and easier to maintain. If a specific behavior unique to the Manager is needed, the object can be downcast to its original type. This approach simplifies code while enabling dynamic behavior based on the actual object type.

Line 5 is allowed because the reference variable e is a polymorphic variable, which means the variable e can be assigned to any Employee type object, like Manager, Director, Engineer, or even Secretary.

Consider the class hierarchy in [Figure 4.1](#17_Chapter04.xhtml_fig4-1). The following Java statements can be substituted in line 5:

Employee e = new Employee();

Employee m = new Manager();

Employee d = new Director();

Employee s = new Secretary();

Employee en = new Engineer();

These are all legal declarations because all of these classes are subclasses of the Employee class. As long as there is a class that is a subclass of Employee, you can assign it to the reference variable Employee e.

To easily recall this rule, you can follow this syntax:

<superclass> var = new <class or subclass>;

However, in line 6, this statement will cause a compilation error.

6   e.department = "Sales";// this is illegal

This is because upon compilation, the department attribute is not found in the class Employee. The department attribute can only be found in the class Manager and, by virtue of inheritance, also in its subclasses.

When the e.department call happened in line 6, the department attribute that was only declared in the Manager class was checked by the compiler, and it was not found in the Employee class.

During compilation, the left-hand side of an assignment (=) operator was checked and evaluated, while the right-hand side was evaluated at runtime.

On the left-hand side of the assignment operator (=), we have;

5   Employee e = new Manager();

The statement Employee e was evaluated at compile time. At the same time, the next statement in line 6 was also evaluated;

6   e.department = "Sales";

It will cause a compilation error because the reference variable e can only access the name, salary, birthDate attributes, as well as the getDetails() method, which are members of the Employee class. The department attribute is not a member of the Employee class, and therefore caused the compilation error.

## [4.2Virtual Method Invocation: The getDetails() Method](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch4-2)

Consider the code snippet:

5   Employee e = new Manager();

         ....

7   System.out.println(e.getDetails());

In line 7, the getDetails() method was called, which is present in both the Employee and Manager classes. The Employee class has a declared getDetails() method, while the Manager class inherited it (and possibly overrides it).

At runtime, the getDetails() method that will execute is the one that is associated with the object. In this example, the getDetails() method that will run is the version declared under the Manager class. This process is called the Virtual Method Invocation (VMI).

The Employee class's implementation of the getDetails() method will only return the String representation of the name and salary of the object, while the Manager class's getDetails() method implementation will return something more. It can return a String representation of the name and salary, and the department values of the Manager object.

This polymorphic behavior allows the method implementation to change at runtime. The method name must be a member of the declared class—in this case, the Employee class is checked at compile time. While at runtime, the method implementation of the getDetails() method will come from the instantiated object, which, in this case, comes from the Manager class.

Here’s a working code that contains additional methods that will demonstrate polymorphism better. The source code, Employee.java that contains all three classes: the Employee class, the Manager class, and the Director class.

1 class Employee {

2    String name;

3    double salary;

4    public String getDetails() {

5      return "Name: " + name +

6          "\nSalary: " + salary;

7    }

8    public void work() {

9      System.out.println("Work work work...");

10  }

11 }

12 class Manager extends Employee {

13  String department;

14  public String getDetails() {

15    return super.getDetails() +

16         "\nDepartment: " + department;

17  }

18  public void callMeeting() {

19    System.out.println("Call Department Meeting..");

20  }

21 }

22 class Director extends Manager {

23  double carAllowance;

24  public String getDetails() {

25    return super.getDetails() +

26         "\nCar Allowance: " + carAllowance;

27  }

28  public void playGolf() {

29    System.out.println("Play Golf..");

30  }

31 }

Together with the Employee.java, we also have the TestEmployee.java. Here’s the code:

1    public class TestEmployee {

2      public static void main(String[] args)  {

3          Employee e = new Employee();

4          Employee m = new Manager();

5          Employee d = new Director();

6    

7        doEmployeeStuff(e);       // an Employee object

8        // doEmployeeStuff(m);    // an Manager object

9        // doEmployeeStuff(d);    // an Director object

10    }

11

12    public static void doEmployeeStuff(Employee e)

13    {

14        e.work();

15        if (e instanceof Director)

16        {

17            Director d = (Director) e;

18            d.callMeeting();

19            d.playGolf();

20        }

21        else if (e instanceof Manager)

22        {

23            Manager m = (Manager) e;

24            m.callMeeting();

25        }

26     }

27  }

If the doEmployeeStuff() method takes in an Employee e object from line 7, the output will be:

Work work work...

If changed to a Manager object by using line 8’s doEmployeeStuff(m); then the output will be:

Work work work...
Call Department Meeting..

And if changed to a Director object by using line 9’s doEmployeeStuff(d); then the output will be:

Work work work...
Call Department Meeting..
Play Golf..

## [4.3Heterogeneous Array](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch4-3)

In most programming languages, arrays are usually homogeneous in nature. A homogeneous array will always have element values of the same type. Consider this array of String objects that represents our favorite coffee concoction. This is an example of a homogeneous array.

String coffee[] = {"Espresso", "Java", "Mochachino"};

With polymorphism, a heterogeneous array is also possible. Consider the hierarchy in [Figure 4.1](#17_Chapter04.xhtml_fig4-1) given earlier. Let us say you have an array of Employee objects called staff[], and your boss wants you to display all the details of all the employee objects, which include a variety of different employee objects like Director, Manager, Secretary, Employee, and Engineer objects.

Consider this code snippet:

// declares an array of Employees called staff

// that demonstrates a heterogeneous array

Employee staff[] = new Employee[5];  

// each element of the array staff[] may be

// assigned to a different type of Employee object.

// With polymorphism, this is possible as long as

// the object type is a subclass of the Employee class

staff[0] = new Manager();

staff[1] = new Director ();

staff[2] = new Employee();

staff[3] = new Secretary();

staff[4] = new Engineer();

for (int i = 0; i < staff.length; i++)

{

  System.out.println(staff[i].getDetails());

}

This sample code will display all the details of all your employees by calling the getDetails() method of each object.

Every time the loop iterates, the variable i increments starting from 0, and the statement staff[i].getDetails() will return different values depending on what object staff[i] is referring to.

If i = 0, then staff[0] is referring to a Manager object, and it will return the name, the salary, and the department information for the Manager object.

If i = 1, then staff[1] is referring to a Director object and will return the name, the salary, the department, and the carAllowance information for the Director object.

If i = 2, then staff[2] is referring to an Employee object; thus, it will return the name and the salary information for the Employee object.

This is one area where virtual method invocation in Java is useful.

## [4.4Methods With Polymorphic Arguments](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch4-4)

Another way to demonstrate polymorphism in Java is by declaring methods with polymorphic arguments. This feature allows you to create a method that can accept an object of any type within the subclass of the data type in the argument.

Consider the UML Diagram in [Figure 4.2](#17_Chapter04.xhtml_fig4-2) that shows the hierarchy of the Animal class.

Figure 4.2 Single Inheritance Hierarchy Example for Animal Class Hierarchy

![image](Images/fig4-2.jpg)

The Animal class has two subclasses, the Mammal and the Bird class. Each subclass has its own unique methods. For the Mammal class, it has a unique method of feedYoung(), while the Bird class has a unique set of methods, layEggs() and buildNests().

Here are the sample codes for Animal, Mammal, and Bird classes.  

1   // Animal.java

2  public class Animal {

3    // some animal-based methods

4    public void eat() { some implementation here }

5    public void breathe() { some implementation here }

6    public void sleep() { some implementation here }

7    public void play() {

8       System.out.println("Have fun.");

9    }

10  }

1  // Mammal.java

2  public class Mammal extends Animal {

3     // override all animal-based methods

4     @Override

5    public void eat() {

6        System.out.println("Mammal Eats");  

7   }

8   @Override

9   public void breathe() {

10      System.out.println("Mammal Breathe");  

11  }

12  @Override

13  public void sleep() {

14      System.out.println("Mammal Sleeps");  

15  }

16  

17  // my own mammalian methods

18  public void feedYoung() {  

19      System.out.println("Feed Young");

20  }

21 }

1  // Bird.java

2  public class Bird extends Animal {

3  

4   // override all animal-based methods

5   @Override

6   public void eat() {  

7        System.out.println("Bird Eats");  

8   }

9   @Override

10  public void breathe() {

11      System.out.println("Bird Breathe");  

12  }

13  @Override

14  public void sleep() {  

15      System.out.println("Bird Sleeps");  

16  }

17  

18  // my own birdly methods

19  public void layEggs() {  

20      System.out.println("Lay Eggs");

21  }

22  public void buildNest() {

23      System.out.println("Build Nests");

24  }

25 }

Here is a sample code that demonstrates the use of polymorphic arguments for methods:

1  // TestAnimal.java

2  public class TestAnimal {

3

4    public static void main(String[] args) {

5          

6        Mammal dog = new Mammal();

7        Bird eagle = new Bird();

8  

9        doAnimalStuff(dog);

10        doAnimalStuff(eagle);

11    }

12    public static void doAnimalStuff(Animal a) {

13  

14        a.eat();

15        a.breathe();

16        a.sleep();

17        a.play();

18  

19        if (a instanceof Mammal) {

20    

21            Mammal m = (Mammal)a;

22            m.feedYoung();  

23        }

24        else if (a instanceof Bird) {

25    

26            Bird b = (Bird) a;

27            b.layEggs();

28            b.buildNest();

29        }

30    }

31 }

In lines 9 and 10, the method doAnimalStuff() passes a dog and an eagle object.

9  doAnimalStuff(dog);

10  doAnimalStuff(eagle);

Line 12 of the code TestAnimal.java, the method doAnimalStuff(Animal a) accepts any type of Animal object as its parameter. You may pass a Mammal object or a Bird object in the said method. This is possible as long as the objects that you pass are subclasses of the class Animal. In this case, the Mammal and Bird classes are subclasses of the Animal class. This is an example of how polymorphic arguments are used.

## [4.5The instanceof Operator](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch4-5)

The instanceof operator can be used to test what type of object is assigned to a reference variable.

Given lines 19 and 24, the instanceof operator was used with the if-else or the if-else-if ladder to test if variable a is assigned to an object of a specific data type.

In this case, line 19 is testing if the object assigned to variable a is an object instance of class Mammal. While on line 24, the variable a is being tested to check if it is assigned to an object instantiated from the class Bird.

Using the instanceof operator, if the passed argument is an "instance of" a certain class that is tested, it will return either a true or a false value.

19    if (a instanceof Mammal) {

20    

   . . .

23    }

24    else if (a instanceof Bird) {

25    

      . . .

29    }

The instanceof operator is a type of relational operator that yields a boolean value.

### 4.5.1Pattern Matching With instanceof (JDK 14 and Later)

The instanceof operator in Java is used to check whether an object belongs to a particular type. Before JDK 14, whenever you used instanceof, you usually had to follow it with an explicit cast before working with the object. This made code longer and slightly harder to read.

In the old way (Before Java 14):

if (obj instanceof String) {

   String text = (String) obj; // manual casting

   System.out.println(text.toLowerCase());

}

Starting in JDK 14 (and finalized in JDK 16), Java introduced pattern matching for instanceof. This feature lets you check the type and assign the result to a variable in a single step. The variable is automatically cast, so you can use it right away without extra code.

New Way (JDK 14+):

if (obj instanceof String text) {

   System.out.println(text.toLowerCase());

}

Here, text is called a pattern variable. If the condition is true, Java automatically treats it as the correct type inside the if block.

Why is this better?

●Less code: No need for separate casting.

●Clearer intent: The type check and variable creation happen in one place.

●Fewer mistakes: Reduces the chance of casting errors at runtime.

Here's an example for matching a String:

Object data = "Pattern Matching in Java";

if (data instanceof String str) {

   System.out.println("Length: " + str.length());

}

If data is a String, str is created and ready to use without any casting.

Here's another example for matching a custom class:

if (shape instanceof Circle c) {

   System.out.println("Radius: " + c.getRadius());

}

If shape is a Circle, c gives direct access to its methods and fields.

Pattern matching for instanceof makes type-checking in Java more concise, readable, and safe, especially when working with complex object hierarchies.

We can also combine Pattern Matching and Logical Operators together:

Object obj = "Hello Java"; 

if (obj instanceof String s && s.length() > 5) {

   System.out.println("String 's' is longer than"

          + " 5 characters: " + s);

}

The type pattern in this case is obj instanceof String s. It binds obj to a new variable s of type String after determining whether obj is an instance of String. This type check is then combined with a condition on the newly bound variable s (s.length() > 5) using the && (short-circuit AND) operator. This makes it possible to carry out type checking and other actions on the object in a more condensed and readable manner.

## [4.6Casting of Objects](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch4-6)

Lines 14 to 17 use virtual method invocation because the implementation of these methods will depend on what type of object the reference variable a currently has.

While other methods that are exclusive to the Mammal and Bird classes cannot be accessed if we use the Animal a reference type, to be able to access the feedYoung() method of the Mammal class and the buildNest() and layEggs() method of the Bird class, we need to perform an explicit object casting.

The Animal a reference variable, can be cast to either a Mammal or a Bird object, or to any of the Animal’s subclass. But for good programming practice, before casting, it is always best to test the reference data type first by using the instanceof operator so that a ClassCastException can be avoided during runtime. Refer to lines 21 and 26 for object casting.

14    a.eat();

15    a.breathe();

16    a.sleep();

17    a.play();

18  

19    if (a instanceof Mammal m) {

20    

21        m.feedYoung();  

22    }

23    else if (a instanceof Bird) {

24    

25        b.layEggs();

26        b.buildNest();

27    }

Testing first before applying a data type casting to objects should be done to a reference variable’s subclass to prevent a runtime problem called ClassCastException. This is why checking the type of object using an if-else statement and the instanceof operator is considered a good coding practice.

![](Images/note.jpg) [Chapter Summary](#08_Contents.xhtml_sum4)

●Polymorphism in Java is another core principle of object-oriented programming that allows objects of different classes to be treated as objects of a common superclass, enabling a single interface to represent different underlying forms (data types). This is achieved through method overriding and method overloading, allowing the same method to perform different functions based on the object that invokes it.

●Virtual method invocation in Java occurs when a method call is resolved at runtime rather than compile-time, allowing the Java runtime to determine the appropriate method implementation to invoke based on the actual object's class. This mechanism supports polymorphism by ensuring that overridden methods in a subclass are called even when referenced through a superclass variable.

●A heterogeneous array in Java is an array that can hold objects of different types, all derived from a common superclass. Using polymorphism, each element in the array can be treated as an instance of the superclass, but at runtime, the specific method implementations of the actual object types are invoked, allowing for diverse object behaviors within the same array.

●Methods with polymorphic arguments in Java can accept parameters of a superclass type, allowing for any subclass object to be passed to the method. This enables the method to operate on a variety of objects, with the specific implementation details handled at runtime, providing flexibility and code reusability.

●The instanceof operator in Java is used to check whether an object is an instance of a specific class or implements a particular interface. It allows for safe type casting by verifying the object's type before performing operations specific to that type.

●Casting of objects in Java allows you to convert an object reference of a superclass type to a subclass type, enabling access to subclass-specific methods and fields. This type of casting requires explicit syntax and must be verified with the instanceof operator to ensure the object is actually an instance of the target subclass to avoid encountering a ClassCastException.

7.Which of the following is TRUE about Polymorphism?

a.Polymorphism is the process of hiding the implementation details from the user by declaring the variables with private accessibility and creating public methods to enable the setting and the getting of the values of the private variables.

c.Polymorphism lets us reuse the attributes and methods from the parent class.

d.Polymorphism uses methods either from the parent class or in the current class to perform different tasks. This allows us to perform a single action in different ways.

8.Consider the class Device that contains the method togglePower(). The class Device has a subclass Tablet, which has browse(). The class SmartPhone has a method makeCall() and is a subclass of class Device. Which of the following statements IS NOT TRUE?

a.SmartPhone "is-a" Tablet

b.SmartPhone "is-a" Device

c.Tablet "is-a" Device

d.Tablet "has-a" togglePower()

9.Consider the class Employee, which has a subclass, the Manager, and the class Director is then a subclass of Manager. Which of the following object instantiations is NOT ALLOWED?

a.A Director d reference type with a Manager object instance.

b.An Employee e reference type with a Manager object instance.

c.An Employee e reference type with a Director object instance.

d.A Manager m reference type with a Director object instance.

10. Given:

class A {

    System.out.println("Class A");

  }

}

class B extends A {

}

}

a.Class B
Class C

b.Class A
Class A

Class B

Class C

Coding Task 2: The Animal Kingdom

Description:

You are tasked with creating a hierarchy of animal classes. The base class will be Animal, and you will have derived classes Dog, Cat, and Bird. Each animal will have a method makeSound which will print the sound the animal makes. You will demonstrate polymorphism by creating a method that takes an Animal reference and calls the makeSound method.
