# [CHAPTER 5](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch5)

# [Other Class Features](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch5)

![](Images/klo.jpg)Key Learning Objectives

●Understand and use static members, wrapper classes, and static imports.

●Apply the final keyword to variables, methods, and classes.

●Define and use enums to represent a set of predefined constants.

●Declare and implement abstract classes and methods.

●Utilize interfaces, including static and default methods.

●Implement functional interfaces and employ lambda expressions to simplify code.

This chapter explores important and specialized Java keywords and concepts that enhance code structure, functionality, and flexibility. We'll revisit the static keyword — understanding how static variables and methods operate at the class level — and see its role in classes like Math and System. We'll also cover wrapper classes and static imports, and delve into key object-oriented constructs such as final, abstract, enum, and record. Additionally, you'll learn how Java 8 introduced powerful functionality to interfaces — including static methods, default methods, and functional interfaces — allowing you to streamline code and implement behavior in a more flexible and reusable way. The chapter concludes by exploring how lambda expressions enable a more concise, functional style of coding, making your Java code more adaptable and elegant.

## [5.1Revisiting the static Keyword](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch5-1)

The static keyword is used in the Java Programming Language to allow you to declare a single copy of a construct (variable or method) and have that single copy be owned by the class instead of having several copies of it that are owned by each instance object.

Figure 5.1 Diagram that shows the relationship between classes and objects

![image](Images/fig5-1.jpg)

In this analogy, you can see that the class is represented by a blueprint, and the houses represent the instances of each object from the class. The green lawn can be interpreted as a static variable that is shared by all instances of the object.

The static keyword can only be used for attributes, methods, and inner classes. Top-level classes cannot be declared static.

Once an attribute, method, or inner class is declared as static, it means the ownership of the said construct will be owned by the class and not by any instance of any object. Even if you create several instances of a class, there will still be a single copy of a static variable.

Consider the following example:

// StaticExample1.java

1 public class StaticExample1 {

2   public static int counter = 0;

3   private int serialNumber;

4

5   public StaticExample1() {

6     counter++;

7     serialNumber = counter;  

8   }

9

10   public static void main(String[] args){

11     System.out.println("Creating the first object: ");

12     StaticExample1 se1 = new StaticExample1();

13     System.out.println("Creating the second object: ");

14     StaticExample1 se2 = new StaticExample1();

15  

16     System.out.println("A total of " +

17           counter + " objects were created.");

18   }

19 }

In line 2, we've declared the variable counter as static, and on line 3, the serialNumber variable is non-static. A static variable, also known as a class variable, will only have a single copy regardless of the number of objects that were created. Unlike a non-static variable, also known as an instance variable, it will always have the same number of copies as the number of objects created. This means that for every object instance of the class StaticExample1, there will exist an attribute serialNumber per object instance.

In lines 12 and 13, two instances of StaticExample1 were created. Therefore, there are two copies of the instance variable serialNumber, one for each instance. But the static variable counter will always have a single copy because serialNumber is declared as static.

## [5.2How Do We Access/Call a Static Variable or a Static Method?](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch5-2)

We can access static variables/methods by using the class name instead of the instance of that class. In line 17, we have accessed counter directly, but if you want to access the static variable counter outside the class, you need to access it through the name of the class:

public class SomeOtherClass

{

     public void doSomething()

     {

         System.out.println(StaticExample1.counter);

     }

}

The way we access static methods and static variables is the same, through the name of their respective classes. Unlike instance variables, both static methods and static variables are accessed through the name of the class. So if we print the serialNumber of both objects, se1 and se2, we need to call them this way:

// printing the serialNumber of se1

System.out.println(se1.serialNumber);

// printing the serialNumber of se2

System.out.println(se2.serialNumber); 

// printing the value of counter

System.out.println(StaticExample1.counter);

## [5.3The Math Class and the System Classes](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch5-3)

Consider the Math class and the System class. Both of these classes belong to the java.lang package and are considered as special classes (utility classes) because all attributes and methods in both classes are static; therefore, every time you access an attribute or a method from any of these classes, you need to access them using their class names.

Check your Java documentation and examine the methods and attributes of the System and Math classes.

System.out.println(Math.PI);

System.out.println(Math.max(20,30));

In addition, the Math and System classes are the only classes that do not have any constructors in them, not even the default constructors. Neither the Math nor System classes have a public constructor. In fact, their constructor is private. The reason behind this is that all methods in both the Math class and the System classes are static, and therefore no object instance is needed to access the class members—both attributes and methods. They are designed so that you never make objects from them. Instead, you access their features directly through class name + static members.

## [5.4The static Imports](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch5-4)

This feature was introduced in Java SE 5.0 and is used to import static members (attributes and methods) individually or collectively.

Here's the syntax of static imports:

import static <pkg_list>.<class_name>.<member_name>;

OR

import static <pkg_list>.<class_name>.*;

For example, we can rewrite the previous example this way.

1   import static java.lang.Math.*;

2   import static java.lang.System.out;

  . . .

7   out.println(PI);

8   out.println(max(20,30));

In line 1, you have imported all the static members of the Math class, so every time you call a static member inside the Math class, you only need to call the variable or the method directly without involving the name of the class, just like in lines 7 and 8.

In line 2, you have imported only one static member inside the System class, and that is the static member out. This means only the static member out can be accessed without the System class name. So, if you want to access other static members like System.gc() or System.exit() in the System class, you still need to access them using the name of the class.

2   import static java.lang.System.out;

    . . .

8   out.println("This is how you print a message..");

9   System.err.println("This is an error message..");

    . . .

18  System.arraycopy(src, 0, dest, 0, src.length);

    . . .

28  System.exit(0);

## [5.5The Wrapper Classes](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch5-5)

Wrapper classes are the class equivalent of all Java primitive data types. They can be found inside the java.lang package and are used to create an object representation of a primitive value. The wrapper classes can be easily memorized. They are all spelled out completely, and the first characters are in uppercase.

Table 5.1  Wrapper class and primitive data types

| Wrapper Class | Primitive Data Type |
| --- | --- |
| Boolean | boolean |
| Byte | byte |
| Character | char |
| Double | double |
| Float | float |
| Integer | int |
| Long | long |
| Short | short |

### 5.5.1How Do We Use the Wrapper Classes?

7   int num = 100;

8   Integer i = new Integer(num);// box-in

9   Integer j = new Integer(200);// box-in

10  int num2 = j.intValue();// box-out

Box-in and box-out refer to the automatic conversion between primitive data types and their corresponding wrapper class objects. Box-in happens when a primitive type like int, double, or char is converted into its matching wrapper class, such as Integer, Double, or Character. On the other hand, box-out is when a wrapper class object is converted back to its primitive type. For example, assigning an int value to an Integer object is a box-in operation, while retrieving the primitive int from an Integer object is a box-out operation. Starting with Java 1.5, autoboxing and unboxing were introduced to handle these conversions automatically, making code easier to read and write.

In this example, we declared an int primitive data type with 100 as its value, and in line 8, we created an Integer reference variable i with 100 as its object value. In line 9, we also declared an Integer object j with 200 as its value.

Is this statement legal?

11 int sum = i + j;// This is illegal in Java 1.4

 // and earlier versions       

In Java 1.4 and earlier versions, line 11 is not allowed. Because i and j are reference variables that contain reference values (or addresses) of the Integer objects that they represent. They do not contain the actual primitive values. Instead, they contain memory addresses of the Integer object.

But starting with Java SE 5 up to the present version, a feature called AutoBox-In and AutoBox-Out was introduced, which allows us to assign primitive values to Wrapper class reference variables.

The given code can be rewritten in this manner by applying autoboxing and unboxing features.

7   int num = 100;

8   Integer i = num;// auto boxing

9   Integer j = 200;// auto boxing

10   int num2 = j;// unboxing    

11   int sum = i + j;// unboxing   

However, starting with Java 9, these constructors were deprecated, and eventually their use was strongly discouraged. The reason is simple: constructors always create a new object, even if another object with the same value already exists. This can waste memory and hurt performance.

Instead, developers are encouraged to use factory methods like valueOf() or rely on autoboxing. Both approaches reuse existing cached objects when possible, making the code more efficient.

The wrapper classes (Integer, Double, Float, Long, Short, Byte, Character, and Boolean) still have constructors, but they are deprecated as of Java 9\. Instead of using new Integer(10), always prefer Integer.valueOf(10) or simply write Integer num = 10;.

Deprecated constructors still work, but they should be avoided in new code, since they may eventually be removed in future Java versions.

Objects created from a wrapper class are commonly used to put primitive values inside a Collection. More discussions on the Collection framework in [Chapter 8](#21_Chapter08.xhtml): Introduction to Java Collection and the Generics Framework.

## [5.6The final Keyword](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch5-6)

The final keyword can be used for attributes, methods, and classes.

Classes once declared final cannot be subclassed. This means that extending final classes will cause a compilation error. Consider the String class, which is declared a final class. This class declaration will produce a compilation error.

public class MyString extends String {

}

The String class under the java.lang package is a final class, and therefore, you cannot create a subclass of the String class.

Methods that are declared final cannot be overridden. Final methods can still be reused and inherited; it is just their implementation that you cannot change or override.

As for attributes, once declared as final, you cannot change their values. They are considered constants. Final attributes have two forms, the first is an initialized constant value. For example:

public static final double SALES_VAT = 0.12;

The SALES_VAT is not a variable anymore; it is now a constant value. The value assigned to SALES_VAT cannot be changed during the life span of your entire application; any attempt to change it will cause a compilation error.

The second type of constant declarations is the blank final variables. A blank final variable is a constant declaration that does not have any initial values assigned to it. The rules for using blank final variables are simple:

1.Values for blank final variables can be assigned only once. Once assigned, you cannot change its value.

2.A value must be assigned to a blank final variable within the body of the constructor. Otherwise, you cannot create an object if you have not assigned a value to a blank final variable.

Consider this sample code.

// Student.java

1   public class Student {

2  

3      private final int studentNumber;

4  

5      public Student() {

6    

7        studentNumber = generateStudentNumber();

8   }

9  

10   private int generateStudentNumber () {

11  

12      // codes that will generate the

13      // student number of each created student.

14   }

15 }

At the beginning of each student’s university life, they usually fill out the university application form giving their name, birthday, address, and other pertinent information. But the student number is initially left blank for the university registrar to fill in. Upon passing the entrance exam and university interview, the university registrar will then assign a student number to each admitted student. Only then will that the student applicant be a full-fledged university student with their own student number.

The student will then carry this student number during their entire stay in the university. Even after several years pass and the student graduates from the university, the student will have to give their student number in order for the university registrar to find their records.

## [5.7The record Keyword](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch5-7)

The record keyword was introduced as a ‘preview feature’ in Java 14 that was included as a fixed feature in Java 16\. The record keyword is used in a class that intends to have only final variables that are set in the constructor.

Before Java 14, we could use this code:

1 // TestPoint.java

2 final class Point {

3   public final int x;

4   public final int y;

5  

6   public Point(int x, int y) {

7        this.x = x;

8        this.y = y;

9   }

10

11   public boolean equals(Object obj) {

12   boolean result = false;

13

14     if (obj != null && obj instanceof Point) {

15          Point p = (Point)obj;

16          if (this.x == p.x && this.y == p.y) {

17              result = true;

18          }

19      }

20      return result;  

21   }

22   public int hashCode() {

23          return x ^ y;  

24      }

25

26   public String toString() {

27     return this.getClass().getName() +

28          "[x=" + this.x + ", y=" + this.y + "]";

29   }

30  }

31

32

33 public class TestPoint

34 {

35    public static void main(String[] args)

36   {

37     Point p1 = new Point(0, 0);

38     Point p2 = new Point(10, 20);

39     Point p3 = new Point(10, 20);

40

41     System.out.println("Point 1 is at: "

             + p1.x + " and " + p1.y);

42     System.out.println("Point 2 is at: "

             + p2.x + " and " + p2.y);

43     System.out.println("Point 3 is at: "

             + p3.x + " and " + p3.y);

44

45     System.out.println(p1);

46     System.out.println(p2.toString());

47     System.out.println(p3);

48  

49     System.out.println("Comparing p1 and p2: "

             + p1.equals(p2));

50     System.out.println("Comparing p2 and p3: "

             + p2.equals(p3));

51   }

52 }

This pre-Java 14 code will compile and run with the following output:

Point 1 is at: 0 and 0
Point 2 is at: 10 and 20
Point 3 is at: 10 and 20
Point[x=0, y=0]
Point[x=10, y=20]
Point[x=10, y=20]
Comparing p1 and p2: false
Comparing p2 and p3: true

Since Point.java is a user-defined class, the equals(), hashCode(), and toString() methods need to be overridden from the Object class.

Java 14 onwards, we can write the same code using the record preview feature, which implicitly overrides the implementation of the equals(), hashCode(), and toString() methods from the Object class. This version is shorter and easier to write.

1 // TestPoint.java

2 record Point(int x, int y) { }

3  

4 public class TestPoint

5 {

6     public static void main(String[] args)

7   {

8      Point p1 = new Point(0, 0);

9      Point p2 = new Point(10, 20);

10      Point p3 = new Point(10, 20);

11

12      System.out.println("Point 1 is at: "

             + p1.x() + " and " + p1.y());

13      System.out.println("Point 2 is at: "

             + p2.x() + " and " + p2.y());

14      System.out.println("Point 3 is at: "

             + p3.x() + " and " + p3.y());

15    

16      System.out.println(p1);

17      System.out.println(p2.toString());

18      System.out.println(p3);

19  

20      System.out.println("Comparing p1 and p2: "

             + p1.equals(p2));

21      System.out.println("Comparing p2 and p3: "

             + p2.equals(p3));

22

23  }

24 }

This version can be compiled using the --enable-preview and the -source options. To compile using Java 14:

javac –-enable-preview -source 14 TestPoint.java

After a successful compilation, we execute the program by using Java 14 and a virtual machine option of –-enable-preview during execution. To run:

java –enable-preview TestPoint

When executed, this is the expected output:

Point 1 is at: 0 and 0
Point 2 is at: 10 and 20
Point 3 is at: 10 and 20
Point[x=0, y=0]
Point[x=10, y=20]
Point[x=10, y=20]
Comparing p1 and p2: false
Comparing p2 and p3: true

Notice that the Java 14 code is way shorter than the pre-Java 14 code. The output, however, is the same.

To compile using Java 16 and newer versions, you can compile it directly:

javac TestPoint.java

## [5.8The enum Keyword](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch5-8)

Before Java 1.5 came out, the concept of enumeration already existed, but Java had another way of handling it. The enum keyword is used to define a special data type called an enumeration, which represents a fixed set of constants, such as days of the week or directions.

1 public class InnerPlanets {

2    public static final int MERCURY   = 0;

3    public static final int VENUS   = 1;

4    public static final int EARTH    = 2;  

5    public static final int MARS    = 3;  

6    

7    public static void main(String args[]) {

8     for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++) {

9       switch(i) {

10     case MERCURY: System.out.println("Mercury");

11                  break;

12     case VENUS   : System.out.println("Venus");   

13                  break;

14     case EARTH   : System.out.println("Earth");

15                  break;

16     case MARS    : System.out.println("Mars");

17                  break;

18     default      : System.out.println("Other planets");

19     }

20    }

21  }

22 }

Starting with Java 1.5, enum was added as a new language feature that allows the declaration of a fixed set of constant values. Some examples where you can use enums are as follows: the days of a week, the number of hours per day, the months of the year, or even the suits and ranks of a set of playing cards. These examples will always have a fixed set of values.

All the values declared inside an enum are constants and, therefore, usually written in all uppercase. It also allows a set of values to be declared in a descriptive manner.

Here is a sample code demonstrating the use of enum.

1 // InnerPlanets.java

2 public enum InnerPlanets   {

3    MERCURY, VENUS, EARTH, MARS;

4 }

1 // TestInnerPlanets.java

2   public class TestInnerPlanets {

3   public static void main(String args[]) {

4   for(InnerPlanets planets:InnerPlanets.values()){

5       switch(planets) {

6         case MERCURY : System.out.println("Mercury");

7                      break;

8         case VENUS   : System.out.println("Venus");

9                      break;

10         case EARTH   : System.out.println("Earth");

11                      break;

12         case MARS    : System.out.println("Mars");

13      }

14     }

15   }

16 }

In the old enumeration, there is no type-safety feature checking done at compile time. But with this Java 5 feature, it limits the values of a set of enum values that are checked at compile time, thus eliminating the need for the default statement in your switch-case.

The enumerated types can also be declared inside a class body, like in this example:

1   // TestInnerPlanets.java

2   public class TestInnerPlanets {

3     enum InnerPlanets {

4        MERCURY, VENUS, EARTH, MARS;

5   }

6  

7     public static void main(String args[]) {

8      System.out.println("Our home planet: "

9               + InnerPlanets.EARTH);

10   }

11 }

Since enumerated types are like classes, the rules on access modifiers apply to both top-level classes and top-level enum types. Both can only have either a public or a default access modifier.

If an enum type is declared inside another class, it can have any access modifier assigned to it. Also, all values declared inside an enum type are static and final values and should be accessed through the name of the enum type, like InnerPlanets.EARTH in lines 8 and 9.

Here are some valid enum declarations.

enum TrafficLight { RED, YELLOW, GREEN }

enum Day { MONDAY, TUESDAY, WEDNESDAY,

           THURSDAY, FRIDAY, SATURDAY, SUNDAY }

enum Month {JANUARY, FEBRUARY, MARCH,

           APRIL, MAY, JUNE

           JULY, AUGUST, SEPTEMBER,

           OCTOBER, NOVEMBER, DECEMBER}

enum Direction { NORTH, SOUTH, EAST, WEST }

## [5.9The abstract Keyword](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch5-9)

The abstract keyword can only be used for classes and methods. The abstract keyword means "there is no concrete implementation"; therefore, abstract methods do not have any implementation in their method body, just the method signature. As a rule, if a class contains an abstract method, the class must also be declared as abstract itself.

If a class is declared as abstract, you cannot instantiate objects from an abstract class. Abstract classes serve as a template that forces their subclasses to override methods that should be present in all animal subclasses. In our example, the abstract class Animal contains both abstract and non-abstract methods that must be present in all types of animal objects. All animals eat, breathe, and sleep, but the implementation of these methods differs from each type of animal. Therefore, we have declared eat, breathe, and sleep methods as abstract, forcing all subclasses of the Animal class to have a concrete implementation of these methods. The play method, on the other hand, is a non-abstract method and has a concrete implementation, also known as a method body. Overriding the method play is optional and is not required because it is a non-abstract method.

Figure 5.2 Abstract class Animal diagram

![image](Images/fig5-2.jpg)

Consider the given UML. The Animal class has abstract methods. Therefore, all subclasses of the Animal class MUST override all abstract methods of the superclass Animal.

Here is the Java code equivalent of the given UML above.  

1 // Animal.java

2   public abstract class Animal {

3    // abstract methods

4    public abstract void eat();

5    public abstract void breathe();

6    public abstract void sleep();

7  

8    // non-abstract methods

9    public void play() {

10        System.out.println("Have fun.");

11  }

12 }

Lines 4, 5, and 6 are abstract method declarations. They do not have any body in them, and they end with a semicolon (;).

The abstract class Animal serves as a template for all types of animal objects. If you declare a class of type Animal (a subclass of class Animal), that class must override all abstract methods of class Animal, or else that class will end up being an abstract class itself because it will inherit all abstract methods.

Here's the class Mammal and the class Bird.

1 // Mammal.java

2 public class Mammal extends Animal {

3    // override all abstract methods

4    public void eat() {  

5        System.out.println("Mammal Eats");  

6    }

7    public void breathe() {  

8        System.out.println("Mammal Breathe");  

9    }

10   public void sleep() {

11  

12        System.out.println("Mammal Sleeps");  

13   }

14

15   // Mammal only methods

16   public void feedYoung() {

17      System.out.println("Feed Young");

18   }

19 }

1 // Bird.java

2 public class Bird extends Animal {

3    // override all abstract methods

4    public void eat() {

5        System.out.println("Bird Eats");  

6    }

7    public void breathe() {  

8        System.out.println("Bird Breathe");  

9    }

10    public void sleep() {  

11        System.out.println("Bird Sleeps");  

12    }

13

14    // my own birdly methods

15    public void layEggs() {  

16        System.out.println("Lay Eggs");

17    }

18    public void buildNest() {  

19        System.out.println("Build Nests");

20    }

21 }

Here is our test class.

1 // TestAnimal1.java

2 public class TestAnimal1 {

3    public static void main(String[] args) {    

4        // cannot instantiate an object

5        // from an abstract class

6        //! Animal a = new Animal();

7  

8        Mammal dog = new Mammal();

9        Bird eagle = new Bird();

10  

11        doAnimalStuff(dog);

12        doAnimalStuff(eagle);

13    }

14    public static void doAnimalStuff(Animal a) {  

15        if (a instanceof Mammal m) {        

16        

17            m.eat();// redundancy

18            m.breathe();// redundancy

19            m.sleep();// redundancy

20            m.play();// redundancy

21            m.feedYoung();  

22        }

23        else if (a instanceof Bird b) {    

24        

25            b.eat();// redundancy

26            b.breathe();// redundancy

27            b.sleep();// redundancy

28            b.play();// redundancy

29            b.layEggs();

30            b.buildNest();

31        }

32    }

33 }

But this test class is not that well written; there are a lot of redundancies. Lines 17 to 20 and lines 25 to 28 are redundant code. To remove redundancies in our code, let us refactor the code by moving the repeated method calls to a shared superclass. For instance, since Mammal and Bird both extend the Animal class and Animal has methods like eat(), breathe(), sleep(), and play(), you can invoke these directly on the Animal reference, removing the need for type-specific casts.

Consider the revised test class:

1  // TestAnimal2.java

2  public class TestAnimal2 {

3    public static void main(String[] args) {

4        Mammal dog = new Mammal();

5        Bird eagle = new Bird();

6  

7        doAnimalStuff(dog);

8        doAnimalStuff(eagle);

9    }

10    public static void doAnimalStuff(Animal a) {

11        a.eat();

12        a.breathe()

13        a.sleep();

14        a.play();

15    

16        if (a instanceof Mammal m) {

17        

18            m.feedYoung();  

19        }

20        else if (a instanceof Bird b) {    

21        

22            b.layEggs();

23            b.buildNest();

24        }

25    }

26 }

Now there are no more redundancies, and the code is cleaner. Lines 11 to 14 that call the methods from both Bird and Mammal were now called once using the Animal a reference variable.

## [5.10Java Interfaces](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch5-10)

In Java, interfaces are used to define a contract or a set of methods that a class must implement. They allow you to specify behaviors that different classes can share, even if the classes don’t share a common parent. This makes it possible for unrelated classes to provide consistent functionality, ensuring code flexibility and modular design. By using interfaces, developers can write code that is more reusable and maintainable, as it separates the "what" from the "how," focusing on defining actions without dictating the specific implementation details. Interfaces allow you to specify methods that a class must implement without dictating how those methods are executed, enabling different classes to provide their own specific implementations. All methods in the interface are abstract and should be overridden by the implementing class, and like an abstract class, we cannot instantiate an object using the keyword new because an interface is not a "concrete" class. This is particularly useful for designing loosely coupled and modular systems, as interfaces define what a class can do rather than how it does it.

Figure 5.3 The Java interface diagram

![image](Images/fig5-3.jpg)

An interface can also have variables inside it, but once a variable is declared, it is automatically converted into a public static final variable. These variables are now treated as constants, which means we cannot change the values of the variables in an interface. All classes that implement the interface will inherit these constant values. Consider the set of interfaces:

interface I1 { double pi = 3.14; }

interface I2 { public double pi = 3.14; }

interface I3 { static double pi = 3.14; }

interface I4 { final double pi = 3.14; }

interface I5 { public static double pi = 3.14; }

interface I6 { static final double pi = 3.14; }

interface I7 { public final double pi = 3.14; }

All the interfaces declared a variable pi, which translates to:

public static final double pi = 3.14;  

Any class that will inherit pi will not be able to change its value because it is inherently a constant.

To declare an interface, we use the following syntax:

<modifier> interface <name> [extends

                 <superInterface>*]

{

     <member_variables>*

     <member_methods>*

}

Take note that interfaces cannot have constructors. It would be senseless to have a constructor because we cannot create an object from an interface. An interface can also extend other interfaces. In an event that there are multiple interfaces that need to be extended, a comma is needed to separate the inherited interfaces.

interface I1 { public void doStuff(); }

interface I2 { public void doSomething(); }

interface I3 extends I1, I2 {

   public void doSomethingElse();

}

Figure 5.4 The FlyingObject interface

![image](Images/fig5-4.jpg)

The FlyingObject interface is implemented by an Airplane class that overrides the takeOff(), land(), and fly() methods of the interface.

The syntax for using an interface is as follows:

<modifier> class <name> [extends <superclass>]

            [implements <interface>

            [,<interface>]*]

  {

        <member_declaration>*

  }

  // FlyingObject Interface: FlyingObject.java

1    public interface FlyingObject {

2  

3        public void takeOff();

4        public void land();

5        public void fly();

6    } 

// Airplane class that implements

// FlyingObject: Airplane.java 

1   public class Airplane implements FlyingObject {

2  

3        public void takeOff() {

4    

5            // increase speed

6            // lift landing gear

7        }

8        public void fly() {

9    

10            // keep those engines running

11        }

12        public void land() {

13    

14            // lower flaps

15            // reduce speed

16        }

17 }

An interface can also be used to simulate multiple inheritance by allowing a class to implement multiple interfaces or to implement an interface while extending a class.

Consider the given diagram below, the class Bird extends the Animal class while implementing the FlyingObject interface. Since all methods in the interface FlyingObject are abstract classes, the Bird class needs to override the methods takeOff(), land(), and fly(). The same goes for the abstract class Animal, the Bird class should also override the abstract methods eat(), breathe(), and sleep(). As for the play() method, overriding it is optional because it is a concrete method and not an abstract method. On top of that, the class Bird can also declare its own unique set of methods exclusively found in a Bird object, like feedYoung() and buildNest().

Figure 5.5 The FlyingObject interface used to apply multiple inheritance in Java

![image](Images/fig5-5.jpg)

The Bird class will now have this code:

1 // Bird.java

2 public class Bird extends Animal

3 implements FlyingObject {

4   // override all abstract methods

5   public void eat() {

6  

7       System.out.println("Bird Eats");  

8   }

9   public void breathe() {

10  

11       System.out.println("Bird Breathes");  

12   }

13   public void sleep() {

14  

15       System.out.println("Bird Sleeps");  

16   }

17

18   // my own birdly methods

19   public void layEggs() {

20  

21       System.out.println("Lay Eggs");

22   }

23   public void buildNest() {

24  

25       System.out.println("Build Nests");

26   }

27   // overriding all methods

28   // from FlyingObject interface

29   public void takeOff() {

30  

31       // flap birdy wings

32   }

33   public void fly() {

34  

35       // flap...fly...soar

36   }

37   public void land() {

38  

39       // ready to land little birdy

40   }

41 }

In Java 8, a new set of language features was included. Interfaces in Java 8 can now have default methods and static methods. Also, the concept of FunctionalInterfaces was introduced, as well as the lambda (->) operator.

## [5.11The Interface default Methods](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch5-11)

Before Java 8, all methods in an interface were abstract methods. This means that once a class implements an interface, all the methods of that interface should be overridden by the implementing class. Since Java 8, the default method can now be included in an interface.

This feature allows developers to create a scalable interface, an interface that can be updated without causing an error to the implementing classes.

Consider this pre-Java 8 code:

1    // Animal.java

2   public interface Animal {

3      public void eat();

4      public void sleep();

5      public void breathe();

6  }

1  // Mammal.java

2  public class Mammal implements Animal {

3      @Override

4      public void eat() {

5        // some codes here

6      }

7      @Override

8      public void sleep(){

9        // some codes here

10       }

11      @Override

12      public void breathe(){

13        // some codes here

14    }

15  }

This code will compile, and it looks good for now, but what if we update the Animal interface by adding another method to it? Then the Mammal class will have a compilation error complaining that the newly added method in the interface Animal was not overridden.

To address the problem of scalability, a default method can be added in the Animal interface so that the implementing Mammal class will not have any compilation error after the updated Animal interface is compiled.

1  // Animal.java

2  public interface Animal {

3    public void eat();

4    public void sleep();

5    public void breathe();

6    public default void play() {

7       System.out.println("Animal is having fun.");

8   }

9  }

1 // Mammal.java

2 public class Mammal implements Animal {

3   @Override

4   public void eat() {

5      // some codes here

6   }

7   @Override

8   public void sleep(){

9      // some codes here

10   }

11   @Override

12   public void breathe(){

13       // some codes here

14   }

15 }

A default method in an interface must explicitly be declared default, not to be confused with the default statement in a switch-case construct or even the default (package-friendly) access modifiers. Default methods are implicitly public methods and can be overridden by the implementing class. If the implementing class chooses not to override the default method, then the implementation of the default method will execute once it is called.

A default method is only allowed to be either public or have a default access modifier. The protected access modifier is not allowed to be used.

If the default method is overridden in the implementing class, it can still call the default method in the interface by using the syntax for calling the default method in the super interface: InterfaceName.super.defaultMethod();.

Consider the example below:

1 // DefaultInterface.java

2 interface MyInterface {

3   public void sayHi();

4

5   default void myDefaultGreeting() {

6     System.out.println("Hello default world.");

7     }

8  }

9  public class DefaultInterface

10           implements MyInterface {

11  @Override

12  public void sayHi(){

13      System.out.println("Hi");

14  }

15

16  @Override

17  public void myDefaultGreeting() {

18      MyInterface.super.myDefaultGreeting();

19      System.out.println("Overriding default method.");

20  }

21 public static void main(String args[]) {

22      DefaultInterface di = new DefaultInterface();

23      di.sayHi();

24      di.myDefaultGreeting();

25  }

26 }

This should give us a runtime output of:

Hi
Hello default world.
Overriding the default method.

If this code causes a compilation error, you may need to recheck your installed JDK version. This feature will not work in pre-Java 8 versions. If you have Java 8 or newer Java versions, then this code should compile.

The default methods in an interface solve the scalability issue, enabling the interface to declare new methods while being implemented by existing classes. However, the default methods will not force their implementation on the existing classes that use that interface.

## [5.12The Use of Default Methods in Implementing Multiple Interfaces](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch5-12)

One of the advantages of interfaces in Java is its ability to simulate multiple inheritance without the drawbacks of implementing a method that is present in both parent classes.

If two identical default methods are present in two interfaces that are implemented by the same class, the InterfaceName.super.defaultMethod(); syntax can be used in this scenario.

1   interface MyInterface1 {

2  public default void myDefaultGreeting() {

3       System.out.println("Inside MyInterface 1");

4   }

5  }

6  

7 interface MyInterface2 {

8  public default void myDefaultGreeting() {

9      System.out.println("Inside MyInterface 2");

10  }

11 }

12

13 public class DefaultInterfaceMultipleInheritance

14     implements MyInterface1, MyInterface2 {

15  // overriding default method myDefaultGreeting()

16  @Override

17    public void myDefaultGreeting() {

18    // calling the method in MyInterface1

19    MyInterface1.super.myDefaultGreeting();

20

21    // calling the method in MyInterface2

22    MyInterface2.super.myDefaultGreeting();

23

24    System.out.println("Overriding the " +

25            "default method.");

26    }

27   public static void main(String args[]) {

28    DefaultInterfaceMultipleInheritance di =

29      new DefaultInterfaceMultipleInheritance();

30    di.myDefaultGreeting();

31    }

32 }

When executed, we will get this output:

Inside MyInterface 1
Inside MyInterface 2
Overriding the default method.

## [5.13The Interface static Methods](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch5-13)

Since Java 8, interfaces can also declare static methods, in addition to public and abstract methods. These are methods declared inside an interface that have an actual method body and are declared static. Since these are static methods, they can be called through the name of the interface. Here is an example of an interface static method:

1 // MyInterface.java

2 public interface MyInterface {

3  // a public abstract method

4  public void doStuff();

5

6  // a static method

7  public static void doSomething() {

8      System.out.println("Inside doSomething..");

9  }

10 }

To test the given static method, here is an example of a test class:

1 public class StaticInterface

2           implements MyInterface {

3

4   @Override

5   public void doStuff() {

6     System.out.println("Inside doStuff..");

7   }

8   public static void main(String[] args) {

9     StaticInterface si = new StaticInterface();

10     si.doStuff();

11     MyInterface.doSomething();

12   }

13 }

The output will be:

Inside doStuff..
Inside doSomething..

The static methods are used to create utility or helper methods to be declared inside an interface. It is a part of the interface where it is declared and can only be used by calling the method through the interface name (ie, MyInterface.doSomething()).

## [5.14Private Methods in Interfaces (JDK 9)](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch5-14)

Private methods in interfaces allow you to share code between default and static methods inside the same interface, without exposing those helper methods to the implementing classes.

Here’s an example:

1   // MyInterface.java

2   public interface MyInterface {

3  

4   // A public abstract method

5   void doStuff();

6  

7   // A static method

8   static void doSomething() {

9       System.out.println("Inside doSomething..");

10       logCommon(); // calling private helper method

11  }

12

13  // A default method

14  default void doMore() {

15      System.out.println("Inside doMore..");

16      logCommon(); // calling private helper method

17  }

18

19  // A private helper method (JDK 9+)

20  private static void logCommon() {

21    System.out.println("[LOG] This is a private "

22          + "helper inside the interface.");

23  }

24 }

1   // StaticInterface.java

2   public class StaticInterface

3             implements MyInterface {

4   @Override

5   public void doStuff() {

6    System.out.println("Inside doStuff..");

7   }

8  

9   public static void main(String[] args) {

10     StaticInterface si = new StaticInterface();

11     si.doStuff();

12     si.doMore();    // calls default method

13                     // in the interface

14

15    MyInterface.doSomething(); // calls static

16                     // method in the interface

17  }

18 }

In this code, the interface MyInterface demonstrates how private methods can be used to support code reuse inside interfaces (a feature added in JDK 9). The interface defines three kinds of methods: an abstract method (doStuff()), a static method (doSomething()), and a default method (doMore()). Both the static and default methods call the private helper method logCommon(), which prints a log message. This private method is not visible to the implementing class (StaticInterface), but it allows common logic to be shared between doSomething() and doMore() without repeating code. In the main method, an object of StaticInterface is created. Calling si.doStuff() runs the class’s own implementation, while si.doMore() executes the default method defined in the interface. Meanwhile, MyInterface.doSomething() runs the interface’s static method. Both the default and static methods successfully reuse the hidden logging code from the private method, showing how private methods in interfaces improve code organization and maintainability.

## [5.15Sealed Classes (JDK 17+)](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch5-15)

Starting in JDK 17, Java supports both sealed classes (and interfaces) and pattern matching for instanceof as standard features. When these two features are used together, your code becomes:

●More readable

●Safer (thanks to exhaustive type checking)

●Cleaner, often eliminating the need for a final else clause

This combination improves how we model limited hierarchies, especially in domain-specific designs like shapes, operations, or events.

What Are Sealed Classes?

A sealed class or interface allows you to strictly define which other classes can extend or implement it.

sealed interface Shape permits Circle, Rectangle {}

This tells the compiler that only Circle and Rectangle are allowed to implement the Shape interface. No other class can implement Shape, which helps the compiler reason better about the program.

Here’s the full code:

1   // Main.java

2   sealed interface Shapes

3        permits Circle, Rectangle {}

4  

5   final class Circle implements Shapes {

6      int radius = 3;

7   }

8  

9   final class Rectangle implements Shapes {

10      int width = 4, height = 6;

11 }

12

13 public class Main {

14   public static void describeShape(Shapes shape){

15     if (shape instanceof Circle c) {

16        System.out.println("Circle with radius "

17            + c.radius);

18     } else if (shape instanceof Rectangle r) {

19     System.out.println("Rectangle of "

20         + r.width + " x " + r.height);

21     }

22  }

23

24  public static void main(String[] args) {

25      describeShape(new Circle());

26      describeShape(new Rectangle());

27   }

28 }

Here's the code breakdown:

1\. Sealed Interface Definition

sealed interface Shape permits Circle, Rectangle {}

This restricts the Shape hierarchy to only two known types: Circle and Rectangle.

2\. Concrete Classes

final class Circle implements Shape {

int radius = 3;

}

final class Rectangle implements Shape {

int width = 4, height = 6;

}

These are the only permitted implementers of Shape, and they are marked final, which means they can’t be subclassed further.

3\. Pattern Matching in Action

if (shape instanceof Circle c) { ... }

else if (shape instanceof Rectangle r) { ... }

Each instanceof condition not only checks the type but also binds the matched object to a variable (c or r) for use in the block. No need to cast manually – the compiler handles it.

4\. No else Clause Needed

Because the compiler knows all possible subtypes of Shape, it can verify exhaustiveness. This means if any subtype is missing in the logic, the compiler can warn you – much like how switch on enums works.

Why Is This Useful?

●Compile-time safety: The compiler knows all allowed subtypes, so it can catch missing cases.

●Better readability: Fewer casts and clearer control flow.

●Improved maintainability: Adding a new permitted subclass will cause a compilation error in the pattern match until it’s handled.

All permitted subclasses must be final, or sealed

## [5.16The Functional Interface and the Lambda (->) Operator](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch5-16)

A functional interface is essentially an interface that has only one abstract method declared. On top of the interface declaration, an @FunctionalInterface annotation is placed to indicate the interface is intended to be functional.

An annotation is a form of metadata that provides additional information about the code without affecting its behavior. Annotations can be used to give instructions to the compiler, define configurations, or assist during runtime processing. In this example, the @FunctionalInterface annotation tells the compiler that this interface contains only one method, which makes it a FunctionalInterface.

@FunctionalInterface

interface Greeter {

    public void sayHello(String name);

}

What is important is that the functional interface should only have one abstract method, and it can also have any number of default methods. Functional Interfaces can also be found in the standard API, like the Runnable, Comparable, and ItemListener interfaces. These interfaces only have one abstract method, and therefore, they are considered functional interfaces.

Using the lambda ( -> ) operator, we can easily provide an implementation of the methods without creating any additional classes. Consider this sample code:

1 // LambdaExample.java

2 @FunctionalInterface

3 interface Greeter {

4  public void sayHello(String name);

5 }

6

7 public class LambdaExample {

8  public static void main(String args[]) {

9    Greeter g = (name) ->

10    System.out.println("Hello " +

11             name + "!");

12    g.sayHello("Lawrence");

13  }

14 }

This example will pass the value "Lawrence" to the method sayHello and display it.

We can also have a lambda ( -> ) operator to point to an entire code block. If there are two or more statements that need to be executed, then an enclosing pair of curly braces ( { } ) is needed.

Greeter g = (name) -> {

     System.out.print("Hello ");

          System.out.println(name);

          };

g.sayHello("Lawrence");

A method with multiple arguments and a return type can be called by a lambda ( -> ) operator.

1 // LambdaAnotherExample.java

2 @FunctionalInterface

3 interface Greeter {

4  public String afterBirthday(String name,

5             int age);}

6

7 public class LambdaAnotherExample {

8   public static void main(String args[]) {

9    Greeter g = (name, age) -> {

10         return name + ", next year you will be "

11         + (age + 1) + " years old.";

12    };

13    System.out.println(g.afterBirthday("Joe", 30));

14  }

15 }

This will give us an output of:

Joe, next year you will be 31 years old.

![](Images/note.jpg) [Chapter Summary](#08_Contents.xhtml_sum5)

●In Java, the static keyword is used to define class-level variables and methods that can be accessed without creating an instance of the class. Static members are shared among all instances of the class, meaning they belong to the class itself rather than any specific object.

●Both the Math and System classes in the java.lang package contains static attributes and static methods. In both classes, there are constructors because there’s no need for us to create an object in these methods, as they are designed to only have a single copy (or single instance) of their respective attributes and methods.

●The static imports allow for the import of static members (fields and methods) from other classes, enabling them to be used without specifying the class name. This feature simplifies code by allowing direct access to static members, improving readability and reducing verbosity.

●Wrapper classes in Java provide a way to use primitive data types (like int, char, etc.) as objects, enabling them to be used in contexts that require objects, such as collections. They also offer utility methods for converting between primitives and their corresponding wrapper objects, as well as useful constants and methods for handling data.

●The final keyword in Java is used to declare constants, prevent method overriding, and restrict inheritance. When applied to variables, it makes them immutable; when used with methods, it prevents them from being overridden; and when used with classes, it ensures they cannot be subclassed.

●The record keyword in Java is used to define a special kind of class that automatically generates boilerplate code for immutable data carriers, such as constructors, getters, equals(), hashCode(), and toString() methods. Records simplify the creation of classes that are primarily used to store data by reducing verbosity and improving code readability.

●The enum keyword in Java is used to define a set of named constants, representing a fixed collection of values. Enums provide type safety, making code more readable and less error-prone by ensuring that variables can only hold predefined constant values.

●The abstract keyword in Java is used to define abstract classes and methods that cannot be instantiated or directly implemented. Abstract classes serve as a blueprint for subclasses, while abstract methods must be overridden by concrete subclasses, enforcing a contract for specific behavior.

●In Java, the interface keyword is used to declare a contract for classes to implement certain methods without specifying how they should be implemented. Interfaces allow the application of multiple inheritance concepts in Java, enabling classes to adhere to common behaviors across unrelated class hierarchies. They promote code reusability, flexibility, and maintainability by defining a clear separation between API and implementation details.

●In Java 8, the introduction of default methods in interfaces allows method implementations to be included directly within the interface, providing a way to add new functionality without breaking existing implementations. Default methods enable interfaces to evolve over time while maintaining backward compatibility, as classes implementing the interface can either inherit the default implementation or override it with their own. This feature promotes greater flexibility and reusability in code design.

●Another language feature update in Java 8, interfaces can now contain static methods, which are utility methods that belong to the interface itself rather than any implementing class. These static methods can be called directly on the interface, providing common functionality related to the interface without requiring an instance of any implementing class.

●A Functional Interface is an interface that contains exactly one abstract method. They are used to represent functions as objects, allowing them to be passed around as method parameters or returned from methods. The Lambda (->) Operator in Java is used to define anonymous functions quickly and concisely, making it easier to implement Functional Interfaces without the need for verbose anonymous inner classes. This combination enables more expressive and functional-style programming in Java, promoting cleaner and more readable code.

Coding 2: Implementing an Abstract Class and Interfaces

Objective:

The objective of this task is to test students' understanding of the abstract keyword and interfaces in Java. Students will be required to design and implement a small program that uses both an abstract class and interfaces to model a simple application.

Task Description:

You are tasked with creating a small application that simulates a simple content management system (CMS) for managing different types of content: Articles, Videos, and Podcasts. Each content type will have certain attributes and behaviors.

Requirements:

1.Abstract Class: Content

Create an abstract class named Content that includes:

●Two attributes: title (String) and author (String).

●A constructor that initializes the title and author.

●Two abstract methods:

void displayInfo() - This method should be implemented by subclasses to display the content's information.

Double calculateReadingTime() - This method should be implemented by subclasses to calculate the estimated time to consume the content.

●A concrete method:

void publish() - This method should print a message indicating that the content is published.

2.Interfaces:

Create two interfaces:

●Readable

Define a method void read() that will be implemented by content types that can be read.

●Watchable

Define a method void watch() that will be implemented by content types that can be watched.

3.Classes Implementing Content:

Class Article:

●This class should extend the Content abstract class and implement the Readable interface.

●Add an attribute wordCount: int.

●Implement the displayInfo() method to display the title, author, and wordCount.

●Implement the calculateReadingTime() method to calculate the reading time based on an average reading speed of 200 words per minute.

●Implement the read() method to print a message indicating that the article is being read.

Class Video:

●This class should extend the Content abstract class and implement the Watchable interface.

●Add an attribute duration: double representing the duration of the video in minutes.

●Implement the displayInfo() method to display the title, author, and duration.

●Implement the calculateReadingTime() method to return the duration (since the time to consume a video is just its duration).

●Implement the watch() method to print a message indicating that the video is being watched.

Class Podcast:

●This class should extend the Content abstract class and implement both Readable and Watchable interfaces.

●Add attributes duration: double and transcript: boolean, indicating whether a transcript is available.

●Implement the displayInfo() method to display the title, author, duration, and whether a transcript is available.

●Implement the calculateReadingTime() method to calculate the time either based on duration (if watched) or on a word count (assume a fixed word count if the transcript is available).

●Implement both read() and watch() methods based on whether the podcast has a transcript or not.

4.Main Method:

●In the main method, create instances of Article, Video, and Podcast.

●Display their information using displayInfo().

●Demonstrate calling the read() or watch() methods as appropriate.

●Publish each content type.

Estimated Reading Time: 6.0 minutes

Publishing content: Learn Java The Right Way

Duration: 30.0 minutes

Publishing content: Java Tutorial

Transcript Available: true
