# [CHAPTER 6](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch6)

# [Exceptions and Assertions](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch6)

![](Images/klo.jpg)Key Learning Objectives

●Distinguish between exceptions and errors in Java.

●Handle exceptions gracefully using try-catch and finally blocks.

●Utilize multiple catch blocks and parameterized try to handle different exception types.

●Apply the handle-or-declare rule and override methods while honoring exception conditions.

●Create custom Exception subclasses for application-specific scenarios.

●Validate assumptions and invariants during development with assertion checks.

In Java programming, building robust and reliable applications is essential for delivering a smooth and uninterrupted user experience. To achieve this, Java provides powerful mechanisms — exceptions and assertions — that help you gracefully handle unexpected situations and validate your code’s assumptions. Exception handling lets you respond to problems, such as invalid input or unavailable resources, without causing your application to crash, making your code more resilient and easier to debug and maintain. Assertions, on the other hand, serve as an additional layer of assurance during development, allowing you to check conditions and invariants to help identify bugs early and avoid future issues. In this chapter, we’ll explore the Exception and Error hierarchy in Java, how try-catch blocks, finally, and throw work, when and how to create custom exceptions, and how assertions can be effectively used to produce more reliable and robust code.

## [6.1The Exception Class and the Error Class](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch6-1)

An Exception is an object representation of an abnormal condition that arises in a code sequence either at compile time or at runtime. The Exception object handles unexpected situations like network failure, misspelled or missing file name, printers that suddenly go offline, etc. The Exception class is a subclass of the Throwable class that represents errors that can be caught and handled.

The Error class is a subclass of Throwable that represents serious problems in the Java runtime environment, which applications are not expected to handle.

Exceptions can be classified either as a checked exception that can be encountered at compile time or as an unchecked exception that can be encountered at runtime. Unchecked exceptions are commonly known as RuntimeException.

### 6.1.1Why Do We Need to Have Exception Handling?

The following are the reasons why Exception handling is needed:

1.To notify the users, in layman's terms, of what happened during a program crash

2.To enable the users to save all, if not most, unsaved data during a program crash

3.To enable users to exit the program gracefully

Let’s see what a program crash looks like if an exception is thrown and not handled. Here's a sample code called AddArgs1.java that will accept integer values via the command line, add them up, and display the sum.

// AddArgs1.java

1 public class AddArgs1 {

2

3   public static void main(String[] args) {

4  

5       int sum = 0;

6       for (String arg : args) {

7    

8           sum += Integer.parseInt(arg);

9       }

10       System.out.println("Sum: " + sum);

11   }

12 }

Runtime execution via command line:

java AddArgs1 10 20 30 40
Sum: 100

The output looks good because the values passed via the command line are all integer values. Now let’s try to do another runtime. This time, let’s include non-integer values in the command line.

java AddArgs1 10 2.2 thirty 40
Exception in thread "main" java.lang.NumberFormatException: For input string: "2.2" at java.lang.NumberFormatException.forInputString(Unknown Source)
      at java.lang.Integer.parseInt(Unknown Source)
      at java.lang.Integer.parseInt(Unknown Source)
      at AddArgs1.main(AddArgs1.java:8)

When an exception is thrown and not handled, a stack trace will appear. A stack trace tells the user information about the thrown exception. The last line of the stack trace is:

at AddArgs1.main(AddArgs1.java:8)

This means that the exception originated in line 8 of the source code AddArgs1.java, in the main method.

## [6.2Java’s Approach: The Call Stack Mechanism](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch6-2)

If a method is unable to complete the task in the usual way, Java allows it an alternative exit route. It does not seek to return a value from the method where the exception object occurred. Instead, it "throws" an object encapsulating the error information. Java then begins a search for a handler that can deal with that error.

The following code snippet shows the process of the call stack mechanism:

1 // SomeClass.java

2 public class SomeClass {

3

4   public static void main(String[] args) {

5  

6    // unhandled exception will be thrown

7    // here from line 15, this will cause

8    // the abnormal termination of the program

9    method1();

10  }

11  public static void method1() {

12  

13    // unhandled exception object from

14    // line 21 will be rethrown to line 9

15    method2();  

16  }

17  public static void method2() throws Exception {

18  

19    // this line will throw the

20    // Exception object to line 15

21    throw new Exception();  

22  }

23 }

Possible output would be:

Exception in thread "main" java.lang.Exception
at SomeClass.method2(SomeClass.java:21)
at SomeClass.method1(SomeClass.java:15)
at SomeClass.main(SomeClass.java:9)

All Java SE applications always start at the main method. Inside the main method, at line 9, method1() was called; and inside method1() in line 15, method2() was called. However, in line 21 of method2(), an unhandled Exception was thrown. The said unhandled Exception object inside method2() will be thrown back to the calling method inside method1() in line 15\. Since inside method1() the thrown Exception object from method2() is still unhandled, it will be thrown again back to the main method at line 9\. Finally, inside the main method, once the Exception object is still unhandled, then an abnormal termination will take place, printing out the stack trace.

Figure 6.1 Image that shows the flow of the call stack mechanism

![image](Images/fig6-1.jpg)

Here’s a clear explanation of the Call Stack Mechanism in Java with the image in [Figure 6.1](#19_Chapter06.xhtml_fig6-1) and sample code SomeClass.java.

The numbers in the image correspond directly to the flow of method calls and eventual Exception propagation:

Step 1 — main() is called first

●The Java Virtual Machine starts executing from main().

public static void main(String[] args) {

   method1();

}

Step 2 — main() calls method1()

●The stack grows with a new stack frame for method1().

public static void method1() {

   method2();

}

Step 3 — method1() then calls method2()

●The stack grows again with another stack frame for method2().

public static void method2() throws Exception {

   throw new Exception();

}

Step 4 — Inside method2() an Exception is thrown

throw new Exception();

Step 5 — Exception returns back to method1()

●method1() has no try-catch block to handle it; therefore, it exits and drops from the stack.

// Exception not caught here

Step 6 — Exception then reaches main()

●The Exception is not dealt with in main() either.

●So the main stack frame exits, and the program terminates abnormally.

// Exception not caught here either

[Figure 6.1](#19_Chapter06.xhtml_fig6-1) illustrates this flow — starting from main() (1), then method1() (2), then method2() (3), then an Exception is thrown (4), and the stack starts unwinding upward (5 and 6).

Here’s another visual flow.

![image](Images/pg266.jpg)

The five keywords used for Exception handling or Exception declaration are:

●try

●catch

●finally

●throw

●throws

## [6.3General Syntax of an Exception-Handling Block](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch6-3)

The syntax below demonstrates a structured approach to handling exceptions in Java using the try-catch-finally block. The try block contains code that might throw an exception, allowing you to monitor and catch potential errors during program execution. Each catch block is designed to handle a specific type of exception (ExceptionType1, ExceptionType2, etc.), enabling the program to recover gracefully or provide meaningful error messages when exceptions occur. The exOb (exception object) parameter in each catch block represents the exception object, giving access to methods and details about the error. The finally block contains code that executes regardless of whether an exception was thrown or handled, ensuring cleanup actions, like closing resources, always occur. This structure ensures robust error management while maintaining application stability.

try {

   // block of code to monitor for errors or statements

   // that might cause an exception

}

catch(ExceptionType1 exOb) {

   // exception handler for ExceptionType1 and / or

   // statements to handle the Exception object

}

catch(ExceptionType2 exOb) {

   // exception handler for ExceptionType2 and / or

   // statements to handle the Exception object

}

finally {

    // block of code to be executed even if no Exception

    // occurred

}

### 6.3.1The try–catch Block

The try–catch block is Java’s way of handling the Exception object. Here is the revised code for adding command-line arguments:

// AddArgs2.java

1 public class AddArgs2 {

2

3   public static void main(String[] args) {

4  

5      try {

6    

7           int sum = 0;

8           for (String arg : args) {

9        

10              sum += Integer.parseInt(arg);

11           }

12           System.out.println("Sum: " + sum);

13       }

14       catch(NumberFormatException nfe) {

15    

16           System.err.println("One of the values"

17              + " is not an integer.");

18       }

19   }

20 }

In lines 5 to 13, you have the try block, which serves as a test area. Each line of the code is run, and if an exception occurs, we move to the catch block. If an Exception occurs, the catch block in lines 14 to 18 will run. But if the code inside the try block runs smoothly, then the catch block will not execute.

java AddArgs2 10 2.2 thirty 40
One of the values is not an integer.

This code (AddArgs2.java) is an improved version of the previous one (AddArgs1.java). But the problem with this scenario is in the try block. The entire body of the main method was placed inside the try block. Placing thousands of lines of code and method calls inside a single try block is not recommended. It’s less about runtime speed and more about writing clean, focused code that makes it easier to identify where an exception might occur. While the overhead of simply using a try block is minimal, performance costs become noticeable if an exception is actually thrown.

A better approach is to include only the problematic line or lines of code inside the try block and not include the entire body of the main method. Here’s an improved version:

// AddArgs3.java

1 public class AddArgs3 {

2

3    public static void main(String[] args) {

4  

5        int sum = 0;

6        for (String arg : args) {

7    

8           try {

9        

10               sum += Integer.parseInt(arg);

11           }

12           catch(NumberFormatException nfe) {

13        

14              System.err.println(arg + " is not"

15               + " an int and will not "

16               + " be included in the sum ");

17           }

18        }

19        System.out.println("Sum: " + sum);

20    }

21 }

java AddArgs3 10 2.2 thirty 40
2.2 is not an int and will not be included in the sum
thirty is not an int and will not be included in the sum
Sum: 50

### 6.3.2The finally Block

The finally block is an optional code block that is usually placed after the last catch block. The finally block contains code that executes regardless of whether there is an exception object or not, such as closing resources like files or network connections. It ensures that important cleanup actions are always performed, maintaining resource integrity and preventing potential resource leaks. Here’s a sample code snippet that demonstrates the use of the finally block.

1   try {

2  

3   startFaucet();

4   waterLawn();

5   }

6   catch (BrokenPipeException bpe) {

7  

8    logProblem(bpe);

9 }

10 finally {

11

12    stopFaucet();

13 }

We have a try block, a catch block, and a finally block. Now, the placement of the finally block is usually found at the last catch block. When your code snippet executes, you will enter the try block first and call startFaucet() and waterLawn().

After calling the methods startFaucet() and waterLawn(), assuming there’s no exception between these two method calls, your catch block will not run. Next, it will execute the body of the finally block, and it will end up calling the stopFaucet() method.

In an alternative scenario, this time, let's call again the methods startFaucet() and waterLawn(). If an exception object is thrown in between these two methods startFaucet() and waterLawn(), say a pipe is broken, which is represented by a BrokenPipeException, the catch block provided in the code will catch that BrokenPipeException using bpe as a reference variable, and then it will call the method logProblem(bpe), which will eventually log the problem, and then finally call the method stopFaucet().

What will happen to the flow of the code if we put return inside the catch block? Will it still call the finally block?

1 public class TestFinally1 {

2

3   public static void main(String[] args) {

4  

5     System.out.println("Inside the main method..");

6     doStuff();

7     System.out.println("After doStuff() call..");

8  }

9

10 public static void doStuff() {

11  

12  System.out.println("Inside doStuff");

13  try {

14    

15    System.out.println("Inside the try block..");

16    throw new Exception();

17  }

18  catch(Exception e) {

19    

20    System.out.println("Exception caught..");

21    return;

22  }

23  finally {

24    System.out.println("Inside the " +

25               "finally block..");

26   }

27  }

28 }

If you compile and run the above code, it will give you this output:

Inside the main method..
Inside doStuff
Inside the try block..
Exception caught..
Inside the finally block..
After doStuff() call..

In this sample code, take a few moments to analyze why we got this output. Remember that if the finally block is present, it always runs regardless of the circumstances. In this case, before the return statement in line 21 is called, the finally block gets to run.

Here’s another example:

1 public class TestFinally2 {

2  

3    public static void main(String[] args) {

4  

5    System.out.println("Enter the for loop..");

6    for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) {

7    

8       try {

9      

10       if (i == 2)

11          break;

12       System.out.println("i = " + i);

13     }  

14    catch(Exception e) {

15    

16       System.out.println("inside catch block..");

17     }

18     finally {

19     System.out.println("inside the " +

20             "finally block..");

21     }

22   }

23   System.out.println("after the loop..");

24   }

25 }

If you compile and run this code, this will give you this output:

Enter the for loop..
i = 1
inside the finally block..
inside the finally block..
after the loop..

In line 10, you have a break statement called inside a for-loop. We know that once the break statement is encountered inside a loop, you're supposed to end the loop. That is still true, but as you can see, we have a finally block. We have to execute the finally block regardless of the circumstances. So, before we break out of the for loop, we have to run the finally block first.

The finally block in Java is generally executed regardless of what happens within the try or catch blocks. However, there are a few scenarios where it might not run. One common situation is if the System.exit(0); method is called within the try or catch blocks, causing the JVM to terminate immediately. Another case would be if the program crashes due to a fatal error, such as running out of memory or encountering a severe system-level failure, which prevents the finally block from executing.

There are specific scenarios where finally may not execute if System.exit() is called:

try {

   System.exit(0);

} catch (Exception e) {

   // ...

} finally {

   // this will NOT execute

}

The JVM exits immediately and finally has no chance to execute.

## [6.4The Exception Hierarchy in Java](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch6-4)

In Java, an exception is an instance of a class derived from Throwable.

Figure 6.2 Java Exception Hierarchy

![image](Images/fig6-2.jpg)

[Table 6.1](#19_Chapter06.xhtml_tab6-1) provides a list of the most commonly used Exceptions and their description.

Table 6.1 Commonly used Exceptions and their description

| Exception Name | Description |
| --- | --- |
| NullPointerException | Thrown when an application attempts to access a method or field with a null object reference. |
| ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException | Thrown to indicate that an array has been accessed with an illegal index. |
| ArithmeticException | Thrown when an illegal arithmetic operation occurs, such as division by zero. |
| ClassCastException | Thrown when an object is cast to a subclass that it is not an instance of. |
| IOException | Thrown when an I/O operation fails or is interrupted, such as file reading or writing errors. |
| FileNotFoundException | A subclass of IOException, thrown when an attempt to open a file that does not exist fails. |
| NumberFormatException | Thrown when attempting to convert a string to a numeric type fails due to an invalid format. |
| IllegalArgumentException | Thrown when a method receives an invalid argument. |
| InterruptedException | Thrown when a thread is interrupted while it is sleeping, waiting, or otherwise paused. |
| SQLException | Thrown to indicate errors related to database access or other SQL-related issues. |

## [6.5Multiple Exceptions in a catch Block](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch6-5)

Prior to Java SE 7, if you needed to catch several Exception objects of different types and do the same error management, you would do it this way:

try {

  // some codes that throws some Exception object

}

catch(IOException ex) {

  logProblem(ex);

}

catch(SQLException ex) {

  logProblem(ex);

}

In Java SE 7, multiple Exception objects can be caught inside a single catch block like this:

try {

  // some codes that throws some Exception object

}

catch(IOException | SQLException ex) {

  logProblem(ex);

}

In this sample code, if the try block throws an IOException or a SQL Exception object, either Exception will be caught by the same catch block. This code is shorter, cleaner, and less redundant compared to the first one.

## [6.6The Parameterized try Block (Try-With-Resources)](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch6-6)

Starting Java SE7, a try block can now have resource parameters. These resources are objects that need to be closed after execution. Any object that implements the interface java.lang.AutoCloseable, which includes all types of objects that implement the interface java.io.Closeable, can be used as a resource that can be passed as a parameter to a try block.

Here is a code from before Java SE 7 that does not implement a parameterized try block:

1 import java.io.*;

2 public class SampleIO {

3

4  public static void main(String[] args) {

5  

6    try {

7    

8        BufferedReader in =

9            new BufferedReader(new

10            InputStreamReader(System.in));

11        System.out.println("What is your name?");

12        String name = in.readLine();

13        System.out.println("How old are you?");

14        int age = Integer.parseInt(in.readLine());

15        System.out.println("Hello " + name +

16            ", next year you will be " + (age + 1)

17            + " years old.");

18        in.close();

19    }

20    catch(IOException ioe) {

21    

22       System.err.println("An exception occurred.");

23    }

24  }

25 }

In line 8, we see that the BufferedReader in object was created and used in the rest of the code, but at the end of the try block in line 18, the in object was closed.

Here is a code that includes Java SE 7’s try-with-resources.

1 import java.io.*;

2 public class SampleIO2 {

3

4  public static void main(String[] args)

5                 throws IOException {

6  

7    try(BufferedReader in = new BufferedReader(new

8          InputStreamReader(System.in ))) {

9    

10        System.out.println("What is your name?");

11        String name = in.readLine();

12        System.out.println("How old are you?");

13        int age = Integer.parseInt(in.readLine());

14        System.out.println("Hello " + name +

15            ", next year you will be "

16            + (age + 1) + " years old.");

17    }

18  }

19 }

Notice that in lines 7 and 8, you have a try block that has parameters in it. The object BufferedReader in that was declared inside the try block parameter is considered a resource and will be closed automatically after the try block is finished in line 17\. No need to call the in.close() method because all resources declared inside the parameterized try block will be closed as long as those objects are AutoCloseable objects. This will lessen the risk of having memory leaks.

## [6.7The Handle or Declare Rule](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch6-7)

There are two approaches in dealing with an Exception object: you can either handle it or you can declare it. Handling the Exception object means you can use the try-catch-finally blocks like we have done so far.

Declaring an Exception object, on the other hand, offers an entirely new approach by using the keyword throws. Sample methods for declaring an Exception object:

void doStuff() throws IOException {}

void someMethod() throws SQLException, IOException {}

Here is a sample code that declares an Exception object.

1   // SampleIO3.java

2   import java.io.*;

3   public class SampleIO3 {

4  

5      public static void main(String[] args)

6                    throws IOException {

7      

8         BufferedReader in =

9             new BufferedReader(new

10             InputStreamReader(System.in));

11      System.out.println("What is your name?");

12      String name = in.readLine();

13      System.out.println("How old are you?");

14      int age = Integer.parseInt(in.readLine());

15      System.out.println("Hello " + name +

16           ", next year you will be " + (age + 1)

17           + " years old.");

18     in.close();

19  

20    }

21 }

Declaring an Exception is only done for checked exceptions and uses the throws keyword after the method signature, just like in line 6\. It is like issuing a waiver to allow the checked exception to pass the compilation process. RuntimeException objects or unchecked exceptions, on the other hand, should not be declared; instead, RuntimeException objects can be handled if desired. If an exception is declared and actually occurs at runtime, an abnormal termination will still happen. In most cases, the declaration of Exception is only done during the software development stage and is slowly converted to handled exceptions during software quality assurance and testing.

## [6.8Improved Final or Effectively Final Variable Rethrow (JDK 11+)](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch6-8)

Starting with JDK 11, the compiler allows more flexible use of rethrowing exceptions when the caught exception is effectively final. This allows rethrowing without explicit casting, improving readability. For example:

try {

    // risky operation

} catch (IOException | SQLException e) {

    throw e; // no need to cast explicitly

}

In earlier versions, developers needed to cast e to a common superclass, which cluttered code and introduced unnecessary verbosity.

Here are two examples of the before and after improvement of final variable rethrow feature.

Before JDK 11:

1 public class BeforeJDK11 {

2   public static void main(String[] args)

3          throws Exception {

4      try {

5        processData(null);

6      } catch (Exception e) {

7         // Need a new variable to rethrow

8         Exception ex = new Exception("Updated" +

9              " message: " + e.getMessage());

10        throw ex;

11      }

12   }

13

14    static void processData(String input) throws Exception {

15    if (input == null) {

16      throw new Exception("Input cannot be null!");

17     }

18   }

19 }

After JDK 11:

1 public class AfterJDK11 {

2   public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception {

3     try {

4         processData(null);

5   } catch (Exception e) {

6      // Reassign and rethrow the same variable

7      e = new Exception("Updated message: " +

8      e.getMessage());

9      throw e; // Allowed in JDK 11+

10    }

11   }

12

13   static void processData(String input)

14             throws Exception {

15    if (input == null) {

16       throw new Exception("Input cannot be null!");

17    }

18   }

19 }

Before JDK 11, you could not reassign and then rethrow the same variable without using a separate variable or marking it as final. This improvement makes exception handling more flexible and code easier to maintain.

In JDK 11+, you no longer need to create a second variable just to rethrow an updated exception. This makes your code shorter and easier to read.

## [6.9Rules on Overriding Methods and Exceptions](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch6-9)

Consider [Figure 6.3](#19_Chapter06.xhtml_fig6-3).

Figure 6.3 Overriding Methods with Exceptions

![image](Images/fig6-3.jpg)

Let’s break this down into three cases.

●Case 1: The relationship of TestSuper and TestSub1: In this inheritance relationship, everything is well. It will compile and run because the superclass TestSuper declared an IOException, and the subclass TestSub1 has no declared exceptions. This is acceptable.

●Case 2: The relationship between TestSuper and TestSub2: This too is acceptable. In the superclass TestSuper, there is the IOException declared, while the subclass TestSub2 declares two exceptions, the FileNotFoundException, and the EOFException. This is acceptable because both FileNotFoundException and the EOFException are subclasses of IOException.

●Case 3: The relationship of TestSuper and TestSub3: This relationship will cause an error. In the superclass TestSuper, there is the IOException declared, while the subclass TestSub3 declares two exceptions, IOException and SQLException. IOException is allowed because it was already declared in the superclass TestSuper. The SQLException, however, is the one that will cause an error because it is NOT declared in the superclass, and it is also NOT a subclass of IOException.

## [6.10Create Your Own Exception](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch6-10)

To create your own Exception object, you simply create a class that extends the class Exception. Since we have both checked and unchecked (RuntimeException), let us see how we can create our own checked and unchecked exceptions.

Creating your own checked Exception:

class BadTastingFoodException extends Exception {

   // put code here

  }

With this example, you have just created a new Exception class called BadTastingFoodException. Now, let's put it to use.

1 // MyOwnException.java

2 class BadTastingFoodException extends Exception {

3    // you can leave this part empty or call the constructor

4      // of the Exception class to pass a String message.

5   }

6  

7 public class MyOwnException {

8  

9  private static boolean candy = false;

10  private static boolean sourBall = true;

11  

12  public static void main(String[] args) {  

13    try  {

14         System.out.println("Inside try block of "

15                + " candy.");

16         isSour(candy);  

17    }

18    catch(BadTastingFoodException btfe) {  

19        System.out.println("Inside catch block "

20                + "of candy.");

21    }  

22    try {  

23        System.out.println("Inside try block "

24                + " of sourBall.");

25        isSour(sourBall);  

26    }

27    catch(BadTastingFoodException btfe) {  

28        System.out.println("Inside catch block "

29                + " of sourBall.");

30    }

31  }

32  private static void isSour(boolean taste)

33        throws BadTastingFoodException {

34    if (taste)

35      throw new BadTastingFoodException();  

36    }

37  }

Sample Output:

java MyOwnException
Inside try block of candy.
Inside try block of sourBall.
Inside catch block of sourBall.

Checked exceptions are checked at compile time. So when you create your class and that class extends the class Exception, you're creating your own checked exceptions, which means this exception can be seen during compile time.

## [6.11Creating Your Own Unchecked RuntimeException](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch6-11)

As for the creation of your own RuntimeException, you have to extend the RuntimeException class. Here’s an example:

1 // MyOtherExceptionExample.java

2 import java.util.Scanner;

3  

4   class LoginException

5     extends RuntimeException { // unchecked exception

6   }

7  

8   public class MyOtherExceptionExample {

9    private static String username = "admin";

10    private static String password = "password";

11

12  public static void main(String[] args) {

13    // try-with-resources ensures Scanner

14    // is closed automatically

15    try (Scanner s = new Scanner(System.in)) {

16     System.out.println("Enter your username: ");

17     String uname = s.nextLine();

18

19     System.out.println("Enter your password: ");

20     String pword = s.nextLine();

21

22     if (uname.equals(username)

23       && pword.equals(password)){

24       System.out.println("Access Granted");

25     } else {

26       throw new LoginException();

27     }

28   } catch (LoginException ae) {

29     System.err.println("Incorrect username "

30           + " / password");

31   }

32  }

33 }

As you can see, the class LoginException is considered an unchecked exception because it extends the RuntimeException class. This means the compiler cannot see this type of exception during compile time. This also means that the best way to deal with these types of Exception classes is by using the try-catch-block and not just using the keyword throws.

## [6.12Assertions](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch6-12)

Assertions check the programmer's assumptions. Assertions in Java are a crucial tool for validating a programmer’s assumptions about how code should behave at specific points during execution. By embedding assertion checks directly into the code, developers can proactively identify and address bugs early in the development process, ensuring that program logic aligns with expected conditions. Assertion checks can be programmatically placed in code, but whether they are executed depends on the runtime configuration. Assertions can run in two modes: enabled or disabled. When enabled (using the -ea or -enableassertions flag), the assertion condition is evaluated, and if it fails, an AssertionError is thrown. When disabled (the default), assertions are ignored by the JVM and have no effect. This flexibility allows assertions to be used for debugging during development without impacting performance in production, making them a powerful approach for building more reliable and error-free code.

### 6.12.1Syntax for the Use of the assert Keyword

assert <boolean_expression> ;

assert <boolean_expression> : <detail_expression> ;

If you want to customize the displayed message, you can customize the <detail_expression> part. If the <boolean_expression> returns a false value, it will throw an AssertionError.

Here's a sample code:

1 // TestAssert.java

2 public class TestAssert {

3  public static void main(String[] args) {

4    int hour = Integer.parseInt(args[0]);  

5    int mins = Integer.parseInt(args[1]);  

6    

7    assert hour > 0 && hour < 24 :

8          "Invalid hour value";

9    assert mins > 0 && mins < 60 :

10          "Invalid mins value";

11

12    System.out.println(hour + ":" + mins);

13  }

14 }

Sample Output:

java TestAssert 15 30
15:30

Here's another one:

java TestAssert 15 300
15:300

Notice the inconsistency? The second argument (300) should have caused an error. Why is there no error at runtime?

Java version 1.4 has a different behavior compared to Java 1.5 and onwards as shown in [Table 6.2](#19_Chapter06.xhtml_tab6-2).

Table 6.2 Default assertion setting in Java 1.4 and Java 1.5

|  | Compile Time | Runtime |
| java 1.4 | javac -source 1.4 Filename.java | Default behavior: enabled to disable:
java -da Filename |
| java 1.5 and 1.6 | java Filename.java | Default behavior: disabled to enable:
java -ea Filename |

In the last runtime example, there is no error because the runtime environment used was Java 1.5 or above. This means that during program execution or runtime, the assertion checking is disabled in your code, thus ignoring the assertion check. If you want to enable the assertion checks, you may want to rerun your application this way:

java -ea TestAssert 15 300
Exception in thread "main" java.lang.AssertionError: Invalid mins value
at TestAssert.main(TestAssert.java:9)

By putting -ea or -enableassertions, as a VM (Virtual Machine) option, you're actually enabling the assertion check on your application at runtime.

Also, you need to note that the error message states that it is an AssertionError and not an Exception, therefore, you need to recheck or revalidate either your condition or your assumptions, or your input values.

### 6.12.2Assertion Support and Unicode Output (JDK 13+)

From JDK 13 onwards, assertions can also display Unicode characters properly in their error messages, which means you can include symbols, emojis, or text in other languages. Although the assert keyword remains unchanged, the overall experience when using assertions has improved due to better Unicode handling in console output and tooling support across IDEs. This makes debugging non-English or symbol-rich messages more reliable during development.

1 public class UnicodeAssertionExample {

2  public static void main(String[] args) {

3    int score = 45;

4

5    // This assertion uses Unicode symbols

6    assert score >= 50 :

7      "![image](Images/wrong1.jpg) Score too low! 최소 점수는 50 입니다.";

8    // ("최소 점수는 50입니다"

9    // means "Minimum score is 50" in Korean)

10

11    System.out.println("![image](Images/tic1.jpg) Test passed!");

12  }

13 }

Here’s how you should run this code:

java -ea UnicodeAssertionExample

You can now add Unicode characters (like ![image](Images/tic2.jpg), ![image](Images/wrong2.jpg), or text in other languages) to make assertion messages clearer and more expressive. This helps make debugging output more readable, especially in multilingual applications.

Before JDK 13 (Possible Output):

assert false : "![image](Images/wrong3.jpg) 점수가 너무 낮습니다!";

May appear as:

java.lang.AssertionError: ??? ????? ??
(depending on terminal or environment support)

JDK 13 and Later (Correct Output):

assert false : "![image](Images/wrong3.jpg) 점수가 너무 낮습니다!";
java.lang.AssertionError: ![image](Images/wrong3.jpg) 점수가 너무 낮습니다!

Before vs. After JDK 13 – Unicode in Assertions

Table 6.3 Comparison table of Unicode in Assertions

| Before JDK 13 | JDK 13 and Later |
| Assertion messages with certain Unicode symbols or non-Latin characters might display incorrectly or as garbled text in some environments. | Assertion messages correctly display Unicode symbols, emojis, and text in various languages. |
| ![image](Images/wrong2.jpg) Limited readability in multilingual or symbol-rich error messages. | ![image](Images/tic2.jpg) Clear, expressive, and more user-friendly assertion messages. |

![](Images/note.jpg) [Chapter Summary](#08_Contents.xhtml_sum6)

●Learning how to handle exceptions in Java is crucial because it enables students to write robust and error-resistant programs, ensuring that the application can gracefully manage unexpected situations. Proper exception handling also improves code readability and maintenance by clearly separating error-handling logic from regular code execution.

●An Exception is a condition that a program can anticipate and handle, allowing the program to recover or take corrective action, while an Error represents a serious problem that typically indicates a failure in the runtime environment, such as OutOfMemoryError, and is usually not meant to be caught or handled by the application. Exceptions are intended for conditions that a program should catch, whereas Errors are usually beyond the program's control.

●This is why in Java, we use the try-catch block to allow the program to test a block of code that might throw an exception (the try block) and to handle the exception gracefully if one occurs (catch block), preventing the program from crashing. This structure helps isolate error-handling code and maintain the normal flow of the program even when an error arises.

●Another keyword that a Java programmer should know in exception handling is the finally block. It is used to execute code that must run regardless of whether an exception was thrown or caught in the associated try block, such as closing resources like files or network connections. It ensures that important cleanup actions are always performed, maintaining resource integrity and preventing potential resource leaks.

●The handle and declare rule in Java exception handling requires that any checked exception must either be caught and handled using a try-catch block (handle) or be declared in the method's signature using the throws keyword (declare). This rule enforces explicit error handling, ensuring that exceptions are either dealt with directly or acknowledged and passed up the call stack for higher-level methods to manage. It helps in building more reliable and predictable software by clearly outlining how exceptions should be treated.

●To create your own exception in Java, you need to write a new class that extends the Exception class (for a checked exception) or the RuntimeException class (for an unchecked exception). This custom exception class can include additional constructors and methods to provide specific error details or behavior tailored to your application's needs, or it can be just an empty class.

●When overriding a method, the overriding method cannot declare to throw more or broader checked exceptions than the method it overrides. However, it can declare fewer or narrower checked exceptions or any number of unchecked exceptions, ensuring that the subclass remains consistent with the exception handling expectations of the superclass.

●The assert keyword in Java is used to create an assertion, which is a statement that tests an assumption in the code, typically used for debugging purposes. If the assertion is false, an AssertionError is thrown, allowing developers to detect and correct logic errors during development.

Coding Task 2:

The Pythagorean Theorem is one of the most well-known mathematical principles. It asserts that in a right triangle, the sum of the squares of the two shorter sides is equal to the square of the longest side, known as the hypotenuse. This can be expressed as: a2 + b2 = c2, where a and b are the lengths of the two shorter sides, and c is the length of the hypotenuse.

Your task is to complete the provided Java code that allows the user to input two numeric values, double a and double b. The program should then calculate and display the length of the hypotenuse double c. For example, if the user inputs a = 3 and b = 4, the program should output c = 5.0.

Incorporate exception handling into your code:

●If the user inputs a negative value, throw an IllegalArgumentException.

●If the user inputs a non-numeric value, throw a NumberFormatException or an InputMismatchException.

●Ensure that these exceptions are caught, and display a clear and informative message to the user explaining the nature of the incorrect input.

Additionally, add comments after each line of code to describe its purpose and functionality.

# [CHAPTER 7](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch7)

# [Building Java Text-Based Applications](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch7)

![](Images/klo.jpg)Key Learning Objectives

●Understand the fundamentals of Java I/O streams

●Utilize java.util.Scanner and String.format() for input and output

●Perform file operations using the File class and file streams

●Convert string data into numeric types safely and efficiently

●Chain streams together to process data in flexible and reusable ways

●Develop text-based applications that read from and write to files

This chapter focuses on how Java handles input and output of text-based applications, a fundamental aspect of developing interactive and data-centric programs. Understanding input and output (I/O) streams is essential for managing the flow of data within your application. Here, you’ll learn about the fundamentals of streams, including key concepts such as stream classes, how data is processed within streams, and node streams for direct data handling.

The chapter also covers how I/O stream chaining can enhance functionality and flexibility in your code, and it delves into Java’s versatile I/O capabilities. It shows you how to perform common tasks such as reading input from the keyboard, converting strings to numeric data, and using java.util.Scanner to accept formatted input. Additionally, you’ll learn how to produce nicely formatted output with String.format() for a more polished appearance.

This chapter also explores working with files through the File class, explaining the nature of a file object, its connection to physical files on your hard drive, and useful methods for file testing and operations. Finally, you’ll gain hands-on experience reading from and writing to files, allowing you to efficiently handle persistent data in your Java applications. By the end of this chapter, you’ll have a solid understanding of how to perform a range of I/O operations to develop interactive, file-centric, and flexible Java programs.

## [7.1I/O Stream Fundamentals](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch7-1)

A stream is defined as a flow of data from a source to a destination or a sink. A source stream initiates the flow of data, while the destination (sink) terminates the flow of data. Sources are also called input streams, and sinks are also called output streams. Sources and sinks are both node streams, also called endpoints. These streams can be opened and closed before and after the input and output of data is finished.

There are different types of nodes such as file nodes, memory nodes, pipes, or inter-thread (pipe) nodes.

## [7.2Fundamental Stream Classes](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch7-2)

Java maintains two groups of streams, byte streams and character streams, both of which have their respective input and output stream classes.

Table 7.1 Fundamental Stream Classes - Byte Streams and Character Streams

| Stream | Byte Stream | Character Stream |
| Source Streams | InputStream | Reader |
| Sink Streams | OutputStream | Writer |

## [7.3Data Within Streams](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch7-3)

There are classes under the java.io package that allow the conversion of byte streams to Unicode text or character streams and vice versa. Reader and Writer classes handle the Unicode text, while byte streams handle raw byte data.

For each kind of node, whether it's a file, a memory array, or even a network connection, there is a specific Java class designed to handle its input and output streams.

This means that when you want to read from or write to a particular data source — like a file on your computer's hard drive or a block of memory — Java provides a specialized class to do just that.

Some classes work directly with byte streams, which deal with raw binary data, while others work with character streams, which handle Unicode text.

For example, FileInputStream and FileOutputStream are designed to read and write bytes from a file, whereas FileReader and FileWriter are tailored for reading and writing character data.

This lets you choose the most appropriate tool for your specific needs and makes it easier to perform I/O operations in a way that is efficient, convenient, and reliable. [Table 7.2](#20_Chapter07.xhtml_tab7-2) lists common node stream classes.

[Table 7.2](#20_Chapter07.xhtml_tab7-2) shows sample node streams together with their corresponding classes.

Table 7.2 Sample Node Streams - Byte Streams and Character Streams

| Type | Byte Streams | Character Streams |
| File | FileInputStream FileOutputStream | FileReader FileWriter |
| Memory Array | ByteArrayInputStream ByteArrayOutputStream | CharArrayReader CharArrayWriter |
| Memory String | N/A | StringReader StringWriter |
| Pipe | PipedInputStream PipedOutputStream | PipedReader PipedWriter |

This table provides some of Java's Node Streams, which are used for input and output operations. Node streams directly read from or write to various data sources like files, memory arrays (this is means a block of memory that temporarily stores data while your program is running), or pipes (is a way for two parts of a Java program to communicate directly with each other — it's like a tube that lets data flow from one side – the output, to the other side – the input).

Most methods from the byte stream classes are also present in the character stream classes. That is why, in most cases, whatever functionality a byte stream can do can also be done by the character streams. The only difference between these two streams is the type of character they process. Byte streams can process non-text-based or compressed data, while character streams can process text-based or uncompressed data. Sample file types that can be processed by byte streams are JPG, PDF, MP3, AVI, etc.. For character streams, we have txt, html, css, xml, js, csv, and other uncompiled source codes like py, cpp, and java files.

## [7.4I/O Stream Chaining](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch7-4)

[Figure 7.1](#20_Chapter07.xhtml_fig7-1) shows that it is possible to convert and pass data from a byte stream to a character stream and vice versa so that the JVM processes the said data in a Unicode format, then it can be returned to become non-Unicode data for either storage, display, streaming or further manipulation.

Figure 7.1 I/O Stream Chaining

![image](Images/fig7-1.jpg)

An example of this scenario is when you accept values from your keyboard. The character encoding used in Java 1.0 was ASCII (American Standard Code for Information Interchange). It encodes 128 characters using 7 bits, including English letters, digits, and some control characters. Usually, all these characters are found on your standard keyboard. In 1997, when Java 1.1 was released, one of the major changes was the character encoding. From ASCII, it was changed to Unicode to address the need for internationalization support, allowing other characters aside from the English characters to be included. Unicode is a comprehensive standard that can encode over 1.1 million characters using different encoding forms (UTF-8, UTF-16, UTF-32). It covers characters from various languages, symbols, and emojis, providing broader global support compared to ASCII. So when we accept inputs from our keyboard, which is in ASCII format, it will be piped or converted into Unicode so that the JVM can process it.

## [7.5Java I/O](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch7-5)

Java I/O (Input and Output) provides a set of classes and methods for reading data from and writing data to various data sources such as files, memory, and network connections. It supports both byte streams for handling binary data and character streams for managing text data, making it versatile for different types of input and output operations. Java I/O provides a way to do the following:

1.Reading input from the standard input device (the keyboard)

2.Writing to the standard output device (the screen)

3.Reading and writing data from and to a file.

## [7.6Reading Input via Keyboard](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch7-6)

Reading from the standard input device uses a predefined object called System.in. The System.in is an input stream object that is, by default, open and ready to supply input data. Typically, this stream supports data input from the keyboard. We use this code snippet to set up our input stream:

BufferedReader br = new

       BufferedReader(new InputStreamReader(System.in));

The System.in object accepts an ASCII input from your keyboard, then it passes the input stream object to the InputStreamReader. The InputStreamReader is a bridge from byte streams to character streams. It reads bytes and decodes them into Unicode characters.

The BufferedReader class, on the other hand, reads text from a character-input stream, buffering characters so as to provide for the efficient reading of characters, arrays, and lines. You can then use the method readLine() under BufferedReader to read a line of input from the standard input device.

Sample Program:

1 import java.io.*;

2 public class KeyboardInput {

3

4   public static void main(String[] args) throws IOException {

5  

6         BufferedReader br = new BufferedReader(new

7              InputStreamReader(System.in));

8         System.out.println("Enter text " +

9             "and press Ctrl + D or Ctrl + Z to end.");

10         String str = br.readLine();

11         while(str != null) {

12    

13             System.out.println("Read: " + str);

14             str = br.readLine();

15         }

16         br.close();

17   }

18 }

In the Windows OS command prompt, Ctrl + Z is equivalent to the null character, while in the Unix / Linux / Mac OS terminal window, that would be equivalent to Ctrl + D. In the given code, the null character is needed to terminate the loop.

### 7.6.1The Conversion of String to Primitive Data Types

It is common to encounter scenarios where a value stored as a string needs to be converted into a primitive data type for calculations or logic processing. Converting strings to numeric and boolean types is essential when working with data in Java. Here are different ways to convert a String value into different numeric and boolean types.

1.Conversion of String to integral values

To convert a String representing a whole number into integral data types like byte, short, int, or long, the respective parse methods of the wrapper classes can be used. Consider the following code snippets:

String strNum = "100";

byte b = Byte.parseByte(strNum);   //Converts to byte

short s = Short.parseShort(strNum);//Converts to short

int i = Integer.parseInt(strNum); //Converts to int

long lNum = Long.parseLong(strNum); // Converts to long

These methods are particularly useful when working with data input as strings, such as user inputs or file data, that need to be processed numerically.

2.Conversion of String to floating-point values

Strings representing decimal numbers can be converted to float or double using the corresponding methods. Examine the code snippets below:

String sNum = "1.1";

float f = Float.parseFloat(sNum);  //Converts to float

double d = Double.parseDouble(sNum); //Converts to double

These conversions allow precise handling of numbers with fractional values, such as when performing calculations in scientific or financial applications.

3.Conversion of String to boolean values

When dealing with logical data stored as strings, the Boolean.parseBoolean() method converts the string into a boolean value. The method returns true if the string equals "true" (case-insensitive) and false for any other value:

String str = "true";

boolean b = Boolean.parseBoolean(str); // Converts to boolean

This is useful for processing flags or toggles represented as strings, such as configuration settings or user preferences.

By understanding these conversions, developers can seamlessly transform string-based inputs into appropriate data types, enabling accurate and efficient computations.

### 7.6.2Accepting Formatted Input By Using the java.util.Scanner Class – Revisiting the Scanner Class

As of Java version 5.0, accepting formatted input is now possible by using the Scanner class under the java.util package. The Scanner class can be used to accept formatted inputs from any data source using your Scanner object.

Here is a sample code that uses the Scanner class to accept input. The filename is ScannerExample.java.

1   import java.util.Scanner;

2  

3   public class ScannerExample {

4  

5   public static void main(String[] args) {

6    

7    Scanner s = new Scanner(System.in);

8    System.out.println("What is your name?");

9    String name = s.nextLine();

10

11    System.out.println("How old are you?");

12    int age = s.nextInt();

13

14    System.out.println("Enter your GPA: ");

15    double gpa = s.nextDouble();

16

17    System.out.println("Hello " + name

18    + ", next year you'll be "

19    + (age + 1) + " years old." + "\nGPA is: " + gpa);

20    s.close();

21   }

22 }

The runtime output would be:

What is your name?
Lawrence <ENTER>
How old are you?
21 <ENTER>
Enter your GPA:
1.75 <ENTER>
Hello Lawrence, next year you'll be 22 years old.
GPA is: 1.75

The Scanner class has methods that will allow formatted inputs as shown in [Figure 7.2](#20_Chapter07.xhtml_fig7-2).

Figure 7.2 Scanner methods

![image](Images/fig7-2.jpg)

### 7.6.3Displaying Formatted Output Using String.format() Method

In the good old days of Java 1.4, it would take several lines of code to format an output the way you wanted it to appear. However, since the birth of Java 5.0, formatting output has never been easier. With the use of String.format() or the System.out.printf()methods, you can use these methods to format an output.

Common formatting codes are listed in [Table 7.3](#20_Chapter07.xhtml_tab7-3).

Table 7.3 String Format Specifiers

| Code | Description |
| --- | --- |
| %s | Formats the argument as a String, usually by calling the toString() method on the object. |
| %d %o %x | Formats an integer as a decimal, octal or hexadecimal value |
| %f %g | Formats a floating point value, %g uses scientific notation |
| %n | Inserts the newline character to the String or stream. |
| %% | Inserts the % character to the String or stream. |

Here's an example:

1 import java.io.*;

2 import java.util.Scanner;

3  

4 public class DisplayAverage {

5    public static void main(String[] args) throws IOException {

6      Scanner s = new Scanner(System.in);

7      System.out.println("Enter first number: ");

8      int first = s.nextInt();

9      System.out.println("Enter second number: ");

10      int second = s.nextInt();

11      double quotient = (double)first / second;

12      String result = String.format("%6.2f", quotient);

13      System.out.println("Quotient is: " + result);

14      s.close();

15  }

16 }

In the statement String.format("%6.2f", quotient); the values inside the "%f" are 6 and 2. They define how the floating-point number will be formatted. The 6 specifies the minimum width of the output, including the digits, the decimal point, and any padding spaces. The 2 indicates the number of digits to display after the decimal point. So, the format ensures the output aligns neatly, especially when printing multiple numbers in a column.

Here's the sample output:

java DisplayAverage
Enter first number:
10
Enter second number:
3
Quotient is: 3.33

## [7.7The File Class and the File Object](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch7-7)

In Java, the File class is a versatile tool that represents both files and directories, making it easier to manage and manipulate them. It doesn't directly read or write data; instead, it provides methods to check file properties, create or delete files, and navigate directory structures. You can create a File object using a simple file name, a directory path with a file name, or even by combining an existing File object representing a directory with a file name.

For example:

// creates a reference to a file

// in the current working directory

File myFile = new File("myfile.txt");  

Similarly:

// represents a file inside the "MyDocs" folder.

File myFile = new File("MyDocs", "myfile.txt");

When dealing with directories, the File class treats them similarly to files, allowing you to navigate or locate other files within them.

Using File myDir = new File("MyDocs"); followed by File myFile = new File(myDir, "myfile.txt"); makes it easier to manage files within a specific directory.

Additionally, the File class offers methods like exists(), canRead(), and canWrite() to check file properties and ensure safe file operations.

### 7.7.1The Relationship Between the File Object and the Physical File on Your Hard Drive

Once a File object has been instantiated, a link of that File object to the physical file you have on your hard drive will be established. The File object serves as a mirror to your physical file.

Let’s say you have this:

File myFile = new File("myfile.txt");

A File object will then be created in the heap memory, linking itself to your file myfile.txt on your hard drive. You can then gain access to that file object by its reference variable myFile.

Figure 7.3 Stack-heap diagram for file objects

![image](Images/fig7-3.jpg)

### 7.7.2Common File Tests and Utilities Methods

Under the java.io.File class, there are helper methods that can retrieve information, test the file, or even check whether the file was modified. Here are some of the helper methods under the File class.

Table 7.4 Common File Methods

| Category | Method Name | Purpose |
| --- | --- | --- |
| File Information | String getName() | Retrieves the name of the file or directory. |
|  | String getPath() | Returns the file path as a string. |
|  | String getAbsolutePath() | Provides the complete absolute file path. |
|  | String getParent() | Returns the parent directory of the file or directory. |
|  | long lastModified() | Shows the last modified timestamp of the file. |
|  | long length() | Displays the size of the file in bytes. |
| File Modification | boolean renameTo(File newName) | Renames the file to the specified new name. |
|  | boolean delete() | Deletes the file or directory. |
| Directory Utilities | boolean mkdir() | Creates a new directory. |
|  | String[] list() | Lists the names of files and directories within a directory. |
| File Tests | boolean exists() | Checks if the file or directory exists. |
|  | boolean canWrite() | Verifies if the file is writable. |
|  | boolean canRead() | Verifies if the file is readable. |
|  | boolean isFile() | Tests whether the file denoted is a regular file or not. |
|  | boolean isDirectory() | Checks if the path is a directory. |
|  | boolean isAbsolute() | Confirms if the path is absolute. |
|  | boolean isHidden() | Checks if the file or directory is hidden. |

## [7.8Reading Inputs From a File](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch7-8)

So far, all the input has been done from the keyboard, which happens to be the standard input device. This allows the program to read data directly from a user. However, there are cases in which programs are required to receive input from a file. Reading and writing data to a file is possible in application programs.

The class that is responsible for reading data from a file is the FileReader class:

BufferedReader br = new BufferedReader(new FileReader(myFile));

The br is a reference name variable, while myFile is the name of the file where data will be read.

Java File Input Programs:

1   // ReadFile.java

2   import java.io.*;

3   public class ReadFile {

4   public static void main(String[] args) {

5   File myFile = new File(args[0]);

6   try {

7       BufferedReader br = new

8          BufferedReader(new FileReader(myFile));

9       String str;

10     while ((str = br.readLine())!= null) {

11       System.out.println("Read: " + str);

12     }

13     br.close();

14   }

15   catch (FileNotFoundException fnfe) {

16     System.out.println("File Not Found "+ myFile);

17   }

18   catch (IOException ioe) {

19     System.out.println("IOException");

20   }

21  }

The given program will ask the user to input the name of the file in the command line (args[0]), and the program will open the file. If the given file is present, it will read each line of text and display all the contents of the file on the screen. If the file is not present, a FileNotFoundException will be thrown.

## [7.9Writing Inputs to a File](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch7-9)

To enable the write capability to a file, we use the FileWriter class and the PrintWriter class.

PrintWriter out = new

     PrintWriter(new FileWriter(myFile));

●out is a reference variable that will display the stream to the specified file.

●myFile is the name of the file where the input String will be stored.

Java File Output Programs:

1 // WriteFile.java

2 import java.io.*;

3 public class WriteFile {

4  public static void main(String[] args) {

5   File myFile = new File(args[0]);

6   try {

7     BufferedReader bf = new BufferedReader(new

8           InputStreamReader (System.in));

9     // this allows the actual writing to the file.

10     PrintWriter out =

11       new PrintWriter(new FileWriter(myFile));

12

13     String s;

14     System.out.println("Enter File Text: ");

15     System.out.println("Press ctrl + Z to end! ");

16     while ((s = bf.readLine()) != null) {

17       out.println(s);

18     }

19     bf.close();

20     out.close();

21   }

22   catch (FileNotFoundException fnfe) {

23      System.out.println("File Not Found " + args[0]);

24    }

25    catch (IOException ioe) {

26      System.out.println("IOException occurred");

27    }

28  }

29 }

In the given code, a BufferedReader object was created to be able to get keyboard inputs from the user, but instead of writing it to the screen, it was written to the file by using the PrintWriter object.

Optionally, if you used: PrintWriter out = new PrintWriter(new FileWriter(myFile, true)); with true as the append value, it will allow you to write appended pre-written text to your file.

![](Images/note.jpg) [Chapter Summary](#08_Contents.xhtml_sum7)

●Java I/O streams provide a standard way to read from and write to various data sources, such as files, network connections, or memory buffers. They are categorized into byte streams (for handling raw binary data) and character streams (for handling text data), allowing efficient and flexible input/output operations in Java.

●Fundamental stream classes in Java include InputStream and OutputStream for handling byte-based data, and Reader and Writer for handling character-based data. These classes serve as the foundation for more specialized stream classes, providing the basic framework for reading and writing data in Java applications.

●Data within streams in Java is processed sequentially, where byte streams handle raw binary data, and character streams manage text data. Streams can either be input streams, which read data from a source, or output streams, which write data to a destination, ensuring a smooth flow of data between the program and external resources.

●I/O stream chaining in Java refers to the practice of connecting multiple streams together to perform complex data processing tasks. By chaining streams, you can combine functionalities, such as reading from a file while buffering the input or compressing data before writing it, creating a more efficient and flexible way to handle I/O operations.

●The BufferedReader and InputStreamReader classes in Java work together to efficiently read text data from an input stream. InputStreamReader converts byte streams to character streams, while BufferedReader adds buffering capabilities, allowing for more efficient reading of text, especially when dealing with large amounts of data or reading line-by-line.

●The Scanner class in Java is a versatile utility for parsing and reading input from various sources, such as user input; it can also be used for file IO. It provides methods to easily parse and retrieve different data types, like integers, strings, and doubles, making it a convenient tool for handling input in Java programs.

●The String.format() method in Java formats strings by returning a formatted string based on a specified format and arguments, without modifying the original string. The System.out.printf() method also formats strings similarly but directly prints the formatted output to the console, combining formatting and output in a single step.

●To read and write to files in Java, you typically use classes from the java.io package, such as FileReader and FileWriter for character-based data, and FileInputStream and FileOutputStream for byte-based data. These classes allow you to open files for reading or writing, and when used in combination with buffering classes like BufferedReader and BufferedWriter, they can efficiently handle large files by reducing the number of I/O operations.

Coding Task 1: Usernames and Passwords

Create a Java Program that will read a text file named: userList.txt. The filename should be passed via the command line. It contains a list of usernames and passwords. The odd-numbered lines contain the usernames, while the even-numbered lines contain the passwords for the usernames in the previous lines. Here’s a sample content for the list of Strings in the userList.txt:

| 1 | user1 |
| 2 | password1 |
| 3 | user2 |
| 4 | password2 |
| 5 | user3 |
| 6 | password3 |

Once the String values are read in the file userList.txt, you are going to create two text files named: usernames.txt and password.txt. The file usernames.txt will contain all the usernames located in the odd-numbered lines, while the passwords will be written in the password.txt file. No output message is required, but it will be nice to give a confirmation that your code worked. usernames.txt should contain the following:
user1
user2
user3

password1
password2
password3

Coding Task 2: File IO in Java

Objective:

Write a Java program that reads data from one text file, processes the data, and writes the processed data to another text file.

Exercise Details:

Input File:

You will be given a text file named input.txt that contains multiple lines of text. Each line consists of a person's name followed by their age, separated by a comma and a space. Assume that the maximum number of names in the list is 10\. In this example, there are only 4 names in the list:

Lawrence Decamora, 28

Michelle Crisostomo, 34

Danielle Lauren, 23

Elisha Bernice, 42

Task 1: Reading the File

Read the content of the input.txt file using a BufferedReader or FileReader.

Store each line in an array of String for further processing.

Task 2: Processing the Data

For each line in the list, extract the person's name and age.

Convert the age to an integer and increase it by 1 (simulating a birthday).

Task 3: Writing to a New File

Write the updated information (name and new age) to a new file named output.txt using a BufferedWriter or FileWriter. The format of each line in the output.txt file should be the same as the input file, but with the updated ages. For example:

Lawrence Decamora, 29

Michelle Crisostomo, 35

Danielle Lauren, 24

Elisha Bernice, 43

Task 4: Handle Exceptions

Ensure that your program handles exceptions such as FileNotFoundException, IOException, etc. If the input file is missing, display an appropriate error message to the user.

Task 5: Close Resources

Ensure that all file streams are properly closed after reading and writing operations to prevent resource leaks.

Task 6: Displaying the contents of the file

Display the contents of both the input file (input.txt) and the output file (output.txt) in the console.

# [CHAPTER 8](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch8)

# [Introduction to Java Collection and the Generics Framework](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch8)

![](Images/klo.jpg)Key Learning Objectives

●Understand the role of the Collection interface in Java

●Distinguish the difference between Set, List, and Map and their use.

●Learn how to iterate over collection elements using the Iterator interface

●Become comfortable with Generics and the benefits of type-safe collections

●Utilize the diamond operator (<>) to simplify code when instantiating collection classes

●Develop skills to choose the appropriate collection for a given problem

This chapter introduces you to the powerful Java Collections Framework, a set of classes and interfaces designed to efficiently store, retrieve, and manipulate groups of objects. Here, you’ll learn about the main collection types — Set, List, and Map — and how each is used to solve different kinds of problems. You’ll also discover the Iterator interface, a convenient way to iterate through elements in a collection. Furthermore, this chapter covers generics, a key feature that lets you create type-safe collections, reducing bugs and making your code more reliable and easier to maintain. Lastly, you’ll learn about the diamond operator (<>), a helpful shortcut for instantiating collection classes without needing to repeat the type parameters. By the end of this chapter, you will be well-equipped to leverage Java’s collection framework to store and manipulate data effectively in your own applications.

## [8.1The Collection Interface](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch8-1)

The Collection interface is the root interface in the collection hierarchy. A collection represents a group of objects known as its elements.

Some collections allow duplicate elements, and others do not. Some are ordered and others are unordered. The Java development kit does not provide any direct implementation of this interface. It only provides implementation of more specific sub-interfaces like the set collection and the list collection. This interface is typically used to pass collections around and manipulate them where maximum generality is desired. Now, if you look at this hierarchy, you will see that the collection interface is at the top, and some of the known subclasses or sub-interfaces are set and list.

To explore the full spectrum of sub-interfaces under the Collection interface, the most reliable resource is the official Java API documentation. Within this documentation, navigate to the java.util package, where you will find all interfaces pertinent to the Collection framework. Focusing on the Collection interface, you'll observe several direct sub-interfaces listed.

Key among these are Set and List, both of which are prominent sub-interfaces of Collection. These interfaces inherit the method signatures defined by Collection. For instance, the add() method, shared by both Set and List, allows you to insert elements into the collection. The method returns a boolean value—true if the addition is successful, and false if the element cannot be added.

Figure 8.1 Collection Interface

![image](Images/fig8-1.jpg)

However, List distinguishes itself by offering overloaded variants of the add() method, one of which accepts two arguments. This provides greater flexibility in how elements are added to a list, illustrating the nuanced differences between these interfaces within the collection hierarchy.

We can also remove elements from a collection. However, List provides the additional capability of removing an element based on its index. It's important to note that sets maintain uniqueness—every element in a Set must be unique, and the order of elements is not guaranteed. Conversely, List allows duplicate elements and maintains a specific order.

When considering the implementing classes of these interfaces, Set has prominent implementations like HashSet and TreeSet. For List, notable implementations include Vector, ArrayList, and LinkedList.

Returning to the fundamental concept of what a Collection is, it represents a group of objects referred to as its elements. This concept is somewhat analogous to arrays, which are also objects representing a group of elements. However, the distinction lies in the flexibility and type of elements they can contain. A Collection can only hold objects, while an array can hold either a group of objects or a group of primitive values. For example, you can have an array of objects, such as an array of String or Student objects, or an array of primitive values, such as int or boolean. To convert an array of primitives to a collection of objects, the wrapper classes come into play.

Another key difference is in their memory management and thread safety. Modern collections are not automatically thread-safe, though some, like synchronized lists, provide thread safety. Thread-safe collections tend to be heavier in terms of memory usage compared to arrays, which are not thread-safe and therefore lighter. This thread safety allows collections to be shared by multiple concurrent users, whereas arrays are typically intended for single-user scenarios.

Consider the example of a social media platform like Facebook: you would refer to your friends as a collection rather than an array of friends. Referring to friends as a collection rather than an array is more appropriate because collections, such as List or Set in Java, provide flexible and dynamic storage that allows easy addition or removal of friends without predefined size limitations. Unlike arrays, collections are better suited for handling real-world data, where the number of elements can vary and operations like searching or sorting are commonly required.

Every time you approve a friend request, you add them to your collection of friends. The size of your friends' collection increases dynamically. In the same way, when you unfriend someone from your collection, you remove them from your collection, and the number of your friends drops dynamically every time you unfriend someone. On the contrary, the size of an array is always fixed upon creation; its size cannot change dynamically.

Additionally, collections are mutable; they grow as you add more elements and shrink as you remove them. Arrays, in contrast, are fixed in length—once an array is created, its size remains fixed for the duration of its lifecycle.

[Table 8.1](#21_Chapter08.xhtml_tab8-1) lists the key differences between Collections and Arrays.

Table 8.1 Collections vs. Arrays

| Collections | Arrays |
| --- | --- |
| A single object that represents a group of objects, known as its elements. | A single object that represents a group of objects or primitive values. |
| Some are thread-safe, heavyweight if synchronized. | NOT thread-safe, lightweight. |
| Mutable, the Collection object can grow larger or smaller depending on whether you're adding more objects or removing objects from inside the collection. | The length is fixed upon the creation of the array object. |
| Uses the method .size() to check the number of elements inside the Collection. | Uses the attribute .length to check the size of the array. |

## [8.2The Set Interface](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch8-2)

Let's delve into one of the subinterfaces of the Collection interface, known as the Set interface. The Set interface is designed to store a collection of unordered elements, meaning that the order of elements is not guaranteed.

One of the fundamental characteristics of a Set is its prohibition of duplicate elements. This implies that every element within a Set must be unique. If you attempt to add a duplicate element, only the first instance will be retained, while subsequent duplicates will be ignored.

Moreover, a Set permits at most one null element. To illustrate this, consider the sample code provided below. Here, we declare a Set object, instantiated with a HashSet, which is one of the most commonly used implementations of the Set interface. From lines 6 to 12, various objects are added to the Set, including some that are duplicates. These duplicate objects do not trigger any runtime errors. Instead, only the first occurrence of each object is stored in the Set. When the Set is printed, only the unique elements are displayed, as duplicates are automatically excluded.

Here’s a sample saved as SetExample.java.

1 import java.util.*;

2 public class SetExample {

3  public static void main(String[] args) {

4    Set set = new HashSet();

5

6    set.add("Lawrence");

7    set.add(100);

8    set.add(false);

9    set.add("java");

10    set.add("java");

11    set.add("Lawrence");

12    set.add(3.0);

13

14    System.out.println(set);

15  }

16 }

If you compile the given code directly using the javac command, you will get a notification:

javac SetExample.java <ENTER>
Note: SetExample.java uses unchecked or unsafe operations.
Note: Recompile with -Xlint:unchecked for details.

To use an option to display the compilation warning, we use this command:

javac -Xlint:unchecked SetExample.java <ENTER>

Using the -Xlint:unchecked compilation options will display the following compilation warnings:

to add(E) as a member of the raw type Set

         ^
   where E is a type-variable:
      E extends Object declared in interface Set
SetExample.java:7: warning: [unchecked] unchecked call
to add(E) as a member of the raw type Set
    set.add(100);
         ^
   where E is a type-variable:
      E extends Object declared in interface Set
SetExample.java:8: warning: [unchecked] unchecked call
to add(E) as a member of the raw type Set
    set.add(false);
         ^
   where E is a type-variable:
      E extends Object declared in interface Set
SetExample.java:9: warning: [unchecked] unchecked call
to add(E) as a member of the raw type Set
    set.add("java");
         ^
   where E is a type-variable:
      E extends Object declared in interface Set
SetExample.java:10: warning: [unchecked] unchecked call
to add(E) as a member of the raw type Set
    set.add("java");
         ^
   where E is a type-variable:

         ^
