# [CHAPTER 10](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch10)

# [Event Handling Techniques](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch10)

![](Images/klo.jpg)Key Learning Objectives

●Understand the basic principles of event handling in Java GUI applications

●Explore the Delegation Event Model and its components: event sources and listeners

●Learn how to implement listener interfaces to respond to various user actions

●Discover how to simplify event handling using adapter classes, inner classes, and anonymous classes

●Use lambda expressions for more concise and readable event-handling code

●Apply your knowledge by building a simple calculator using Java event-handling techniques

This chapter dives into the core principles of event-handling techniques in Java, providing a solid foundation for managing user interactions in graphical applications. You'll begin by exploring the Delegation Event Model, where an event source delegates the handling of events to a listener—allowing for cleaner, modular code.

We will cover the fundamentals of event handlers and examine different ways to respond to events using listener interfaces, as well as simplified strategies through adapter classes, inner classes, and anonymous classes. These techniques help you control program behavior based on user actions such as button clicks, mouse movements, or key presses.

To modernize your approach, we will also introduce lambda expressions, which allow for more concise and readable event-handling logic. By the end of the chapter, you will apply what you’ve learned by building a simple calculator, giving you practical experience in designing interactive, event-driven Java applications.

## [10.1Fundamentals Of Event Handlers](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch10-1)

Let’s dive into the basics of event handling in Java. Events are simply objects that describe actions or occurrences, such as clicking a button, moving a mouse, or pressing keyboard shortcuts like CTRL-S. These user interactions are called events because they initiate something within the interface. Each interaction is tied to an event source, which refers to the component involved in the interaction, such as a button, window, or icon.

Figure 10.1 Event Handling Process Diagram

![image](Images/fig10-1.jpg)

[Figure 10.1](#23_Chapter10.xhtml_fig10-1) illustrates Java’s Event Delegation Model, which is the foundation for handling events in graphical user interfaces. In this model, an Event Source is a component, such as a button, that generates events. An Event Listener is an object that wants to respond to those events. To do so, the listener must implement a specific Listener Interface, which defines the required event-handling methods. The listener is then registered to the event source, establishing a connection where the event source notifies the listener when an event occurs. This setup supports a one-to-many relationship, allowing a single event source to notify multiple listeners. By separating the event generation from its handling, the delegation model promotes a cleaner, more modular design.

For example, in a Word document, clicking the bold button or using the shortcut CTRL-B both trigger events. The components responsible for generating these events are known as event sources, and they notify listeners whenever an event occurs. Each event is encapsulated in an event object, and Java’s java.util.EventObject class is the parent for all event objects, with subclasses like ActionEvent and WindowEvent.

Various components in your interface can produce different types of events. Buttons typically generate ActionEvent objects, while Windows may send WindowEvent objects. To understand these interactions better, you can explore the java.awt package, where you’ll find the necessary classes and interfaces to work with events. Different types of events, like ActionEvent, AdjustmentEvent, and FocusEvent, exist for various kinds of user interactions.

When an ActionEvent occurs, such as clicking a button or pressing the spacebar, the event is triggered, and an event handler responds. Event handlers are methods designed to manage the event and execute the intended behavior. So, for instance, clicking the bold button or pressing CTRL-B both trigger the same event handler to make text bold. These event handlers ensure that, regardless of how you interact with a component, the desired action is performed.

## [10.2Event Handling Techniques](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch10-2)

When a user interacts with a specific user interface component, known as the event source, an appropriate method, referred to as an event handler, handles the interaction. In the following sections, we will explore different techniques to create event handlers.

### 10.2.1The Event Delegation Model

These techniques include the delegation model, which involves using listeners or interfaces, adapter or abstract classes, and defining inner classes within other classes to handle events. This approach allows you to delegate the event-handling logic to separate classes, resulting in a loosely coupled structure between the GUI components and the event handler logic. Each event handler class can reside in its own Java source file, promoting modularity and maintainability. You can create as many event handlers as needed; however, after defining them, it is essential to register them properly so they can respond to the events generated by the GUI components.

[Figure 10.2](#23_Chapter10.xhtml_fig10-2) illustrates the Event Delegation Model in Java. When a user interacts with a component—such as clicking a button in a graphical user interface (GUI)—an event is generated, specifically an ActionEvent in this case. Instead of the button handling the event itself, the event is delegated to one or more external event handlers that implement the actionPerformed(ActionEvent e) method. These handlers contain the logic to respond to the user's action. This model promotes a clean separation between the GUI and the event-handling logic, allowing different classes or methods to process the same event in various ways depending on how they are registered.

Figure 10.2 The Event Delegation Model

![image](Images/fig10-2.jpg)

This is the source code for your GUI, saved as: MyGUI.java

1    // MyGUI.java

2  import java.awt.*;

3  public class MyGUI

4  {

5    private Frame f;

6    private Button b;

7

9    public MyGUI()

10    {

11        f = new Frame("Delegation Event Model");

12        b = new Button("Click me!");

13    }

14

15    public void startApp()

16    {

17        f.add(b);

18        f.pack();

19        f.setVisible(true);

20

21        // register your event handler

22

23        // this is event registration #1

24        b.addActionListener(new MyEvent());

25

26        // this is event registration #2

27        b.addActionListener(new MyEventToo());

28    }

29

30    public static void main(String args[])

31    {

32        MyGUI m = new MyGUI();

33        m.startApp();

34    }

35  }

Here’s the source code for your event handler, saved as: MyEvent.java. This file was registered in MyGUI.java in line 24.

1    // MyEvent.java

2    import java.awt.event.*;

3

4    public class MyEvent implements ActionListener {

5        public void actionPerformed(ActionEvent e) {

6        System.out.println("I was clicked!");

7        }

8    }

Here’s another source code for our second event handler, saved as: MyEventToo.java. This file was registered in MyGUI.java in line 27.

1    // MyEventToo.java

2    import java.awt.event.*;

3    public class MyEventToo implements ActionListener {

4        public void actionPerformed(ActionEvent e) {

5          System.out.println("I was piggy backing!");

6        }

7    }

You can create as many event handlers as you want, and they will all work as long as you register them with your GUI component.

### 10.2.2The Use of Listeners

Listeners are interfaces. If you can recall the rules on interfaces, once you implement an interface, you are required to override ALL the methods in the interface. [Table 10.1](#23_Chapter10.xhtml_tab10-1) shows the commonly used interfaces in the java.awt.event package.

Table 10.1 Listeners under the java.awt.event package

| Category | Interface Name | Methods |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Action | ActionListener | actionPerformed(ActionEvent) |
| Item | ItemListener | itemStateChange(ItemEvent) |
| Mouse | MouseListener | mousePressed(MouseEvent) |
|   |   | mouseReleased(MouseEvent) |
|   |   | mouseEntered(MouseEvent) |
|   |   | mouseExited(MouseEvent) |
|   |   | mouseClicked(MouseEvent) |
| Mouse motion | MouseMotionListener | mouseDragged(MouseEvent) |
|   |   | mouseMoved(MouseEvent) |
| Key | KeyListener | keyPressed(KeyEvent) |
|   |   | keyReleased(KeyEvent) |
|   |   | keyTyped(KeyEvent) |
| Focus | FocusListener | focusGained(FocusEvent) |
|   |   | focusLost(FocusEvent) |
| Adjustment | AdjustmentListener | adjustmentValueChanged (AdjustmentEvent) |
| Component | ComponentListener | componentMoved(ComponentEvent) |
|   |   | componentHidden(ComponentEvent) |
|   |   | componentResized(ComponentEvent) |
|   |   | componentShown(ComponentEvent) |
| Window | WindowListener | windowClosing(WindowEvent) |
|   |   | windowOpened(WindowEvent) |
|   |   | windowIconified(WindowEvent) |
|   |   | windowDeiconified(WindowEvent) |
|   |   | windowClosed(WindowEvent) |
|   |   | windowActivated(WindowEvent) |
|   |   | windowDeactivated(WindowEvent) |
| Container | ContainerListener | componentAdded(ContainerEvent) |
|   |   | componentRemoved(ContainerEvent) |
| Text | TextListener | textValueChanged(TextEvent) |

The complete set can always be seen in the API documentation. Here’s a sample code that makes use of listeners. The filename used is: MultiListeners.java.

1    import java.awt.*;

2    import java.awt.event.*;

3  

4    public class MultiListeners implements

5        MouseListener, MouseMotionListener {

6     private Frame f;

7     private TextField tf;

8     private Label label;

9  

10   public MultiListeners() {

11     f = new Frame("I have Two listeners example");

12     tf = new TextField(30);

13     label = new Label("Click and drag the mouse");

14   }

15  

16   public void startApp() {

17     f.add(label, BorderLayout.NORTH);

18     f.add(tf, BorderLayout.SOUTH);

19  

20     f.setSize(300, 200);

21     f.setVisible(true);

22  

23    // event handler registration

24       f.addMouseMotionListener(this);

25       f.addMouseListener(this);

26   }

27

28       // The event handlers

29       public void mouseDragged(MouseEvent e) {  

31        String s = "Mouse dragging: X = "

32          + e.getX() + " Y = " + e.getY();

33        tf.setText(s);

34       }

35

36       public void mouseEntered(MouseEvent e) {  

37        String s = "Mighty Mouse is coming in..";

38        tf.setText(s);

39       }

40

41       public void mouseExited(MouseEvent e) {

42        String s = "The mouse has left the building";

43        tf.setText(s);

44       }

45       // Unused event handlers

46       // that needs to be overridden

47       public void mouseMoved(MouseEvent e)

48       {}

49       public void mousePressed(MouseEvent e)

50       {}

51       public void mouseClicked(MouseEvent e)

52       {}

53       public void mouseReleased(MouseEvent e)

54       {}

55   

56       public static void main(String args[]) {

57        MultiListeners ml = new MultiListeners();

58        ml.startApp();

59       }

60  }

As you can see in the implementation declaration in line 5, this sample code implements both MouseListener and MouseMotionListener interfaces. And since these interfaces are implemented, we have to override ALL the methods on both interfaces. Even if the methods are unused, they still need to be overridden.

### 10.2.3The Use of Adapter Classes

There are situations when it is better to use adapter classes rather than listeners. Not all listeners have a corresponding adapter class. Only listeners that contain two or more methods have a corresponding adapter class. For example, if you implement WindowListener, you are required to override all seven methods even if you only need one. If this is the case, it is better to use the WindowAdapter instead.

Here’s a sample code on how to use an adapter class with a file named SampleAdapter.java:

1    import java.awt.*;

2    import java.awt.event.*;

3  

4    public class SampleAdapter extends WindowAdapter

5    {

6      private Frame f;

7      private TextField tf;

8      private Label label;

9  

10    public SampleAdapter()

11    {

12       f = new Frame("A sample adapter code");

13       tf = new TextField(30);

14       label = new Label("You can quit this app");

15    }

16

17     public void startApp()

18    {

19       f.add(label, BorderLayout.NORTH);

20       f.add(tf, BorderLayout.SOUTH);

21

22        f.setSize(300, 200);

23        f.setVisible(true);

24

25        f.addWindowListener(this);

26     }

27

28    public void windowClosing(WindowEvent we)

29    {

30        System.exit(0);

31    }

32

33    public static void main(String args[])

34    {

35       SampleAdapter sa = new SampleAdapter();

36        sa.startApp();

37     }

38  }

Here’s the output of the code SampleAdapter.java:

Figure 10.3 Screenshot of the runtime of the code SampleAdapter.java

![image](Images/fig10-3.jpg)

Since an adapter class is an abstract class, we have to use the keyword extends, which sometimes makes it a disadvantage to use an adapter class. Lines 28 to 31 are the only lines needed because you only need to override one method in the WindowAdapter class.

Table 10.2 shows a list of adapter classes in the java.awt.event package.

| Category | Adapter Class | Methods |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Mouse | MouseAdapter | mousePressed(MouseEvent) |
|   |   | mouseReleased(MouseEvent) |
|   |   | mouseEntered(MouseEvent) |
|   |   | mouseExited(MouseEvent) |
|   |   | mouseClicked(MouseEvent) |
| Mouse motion | MouseMotionAdapter | mouseDragged(MouseEvent) |
|   |   | mouseMoved(MouseEvent) |
| Key | KeyAdapter | keyPressed(KeyEvent) |
|   |   | keyReleased(KeyEvent) |
|   |   | keyTyped(KeyEvent) |
| Focus | FocusAdapter | focusGained(FocusEvent) |
|   |   | focusLost(FocusEvent) |
| Component | ComponentAdapter | componentMoved(ComponentEvent) |
|   |   | componentHidden(ComponentEvent) |
|   |   | componentResized(ComponentEvent) |
|   |   | componentShown(ComponentEvent) |
| Window | WindowAdapter | windowClosing(WindowEvent) |
|   |   | windowOpened(WindowEvent) |
|   |   | windowIconified(WindowEvent) |
|   |   | windowDeiconified(WindowEvent) |
|   |   | windowClosed(WindowEvent) |
|   |   | windowActivated(WindowEvent) |
|   |   | windowDeactivated(WindowEvent) |
| Container | ContainerAdapter | componentAdded(ContainerEvent) |
|   |   | componentRemoved(ContainerEvent) |

### 10.2.4Inner Classes

The purpose of inner classes is to make tightly coupled components. As for GUI apps, it is used to group the UI code and event handler code together in a single code base.

In an AWT GUI application, an inner class is often used for event handling because it allows the event listener to access the outer class’s methods and fields without requiring additional references. This makes the code more organized and easier to manage. By defining an event handler as an inner class, the event-handling logic remains encapsulated within the main class, reducing clutter and improving readability. Additionally, since an inner class can access private members of its enclosing class, it simplifies interactions with UI components. For example, if a button click needs to update a label or change another component’s state, an inner class can do this directly without requiring extra methods or references. This approach keeps the event-handling code close to the components it manages, making the application more structured and maintainable.

Using inner classes for event handling in AWT apps can make the code cleaner and more organized. An inner class can directly access the variables and methods of the outer class, which means you don't need to pass additional parameters to work with components. This reduces the amount of boilerplate code and makes the event-handling logic easier to understand and maintain. Additionally, because the inner class is tied to the outer class, it can be used specifically for handling events of that particular instance, keeping related code together.

Here’s how we can implement inner classes in building your GUI app.

5

6  public class SimpleCalculator implements ActionListener

7  {  

...    ...    ...

116    private class MyCloseButtonHandler extends WindowAdapter

117    {

118        public void windowClosing(WindowEvent we)

119        {

120          System.exit(0);

121        }

122    }

...    ...    ...

129  }

### 10.2.5Anonymous Classes

Anonymous classes allow you to create objects on the fly without giving them a formal name. These objects act as event handlers that do not require a separate class reference. Once the event handler has been executed, the anonymous object is automatically discarded, helping to conserve memory and improve performance.

This approach is particularly useful in building efficient mobile or desktop applications, where minimizing memory usage is key to ensuring smoother runtime performance. By reducing the number of active objects, the system can run more efficiently.

The general syntax for using an anonymous class is as follows:

// Syntax for anonymous class

ClassOrInterface obj = new ClassOrInterface() {

    // Override methods or add new code here

};

Here’s a sample of how to implement anonymous classes in your GUI app.

1    // SampleAnonymousClassImplementation.java

2    import java.awt.*;

3    import java.awt.event.*;

4    

5    public class SampleAnonymousClassImplementation {

6       private Frame f;

7       private TextField tf;

8       private Label label;

9  

10     public SampleAnonymousClassImplementation() {

11        f = new Frame("A sample anonymous code");

12        tf = new TextField(30);

13        label = new Label("You can quit this app");

14     }

15  

16     public void startApp() {

17        f.add(label, BorderLayout.NORTH);

18        f.add(tf, BorderLayout.SOUTH);

19

20        f.setSize(300, 200);

21        f.setVisible(true);

22

23        // Using an anonymous class for event handling

24        f.addWindowListener(new WindowAdapter() {

25          public void windowClosing(WindowEvent we) {

26             System.exit(0);

27          }

28        });

29     }

30  

31     public static void main(String args[]) {

32        SampleAnonymousClassImplementation saci =

33           new SampleAnonymousClassImplementation();

34        saci.startApp();

35     }

36  }

As you can see in the code
SampleAnonymousClassImplementation.java, we used an anonymous class declaration in lines 23 to 28.

### 10.2.6The Use of Lambda Expression

Lambda expressions allow you to pass functions without names as arguments to methods. This provides a cleaner, more concise way to handle events compared to anonymous classes, making event handling simpler and more readable.

The general syntax for the use of the lambda expression is as follows:

component.addEventListener(event -> {

    // Code to handle the event

});

Here are two examples, with a single statement:

b.addActionListener(

     e -> System.out.println("Handled by "

              + "Lambda Expression"));

Here’s with multiple statements:

b.addActionListener(

   e -> {

     System.out.println("My Event Handler");

     System.out.println("Handled by "

        + "Lambda Expression");

   }

);

## [10.3Building a SimpleCalculator App With Event Handlers](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch10-3)

After we’ve built the structure of the SimpleCalculator app in the previous chapter using components from the java.awt package. The calculator consists of labels, text fields for input, and buttons for arithmetic operations. However, at this stage, the calculator is only a static interface—it doesn’t perform any calculations yet. We are now ready to complete it by including the event handlers.

Our calculator’s functionality is housed inside a constructor, where all the components are created. A method called startApp() arranges these components within the frame using a layout manager. Currently, the calculator looks functional, but doesn’t respond to any user interactions. Clicking on any button (such as +, -, *, /) won’t produce results, and the close button won't work either. Why? Because we haven’t implemented any event handling yet.

Implementing Event Handling

To make the buttons functional, we need to implement event handling. Specifically, we’ll use an ActionListener to capture button clicks. The ActionListener interface is part of the java.awt.event package, and it requires us to override the actionPerformed() method, which handles the action events.

Let’s add this to our code before the main() method. The actionPerformed() method will determine which button was clicked by calling the getSource() method. Once we know the source of the event (the button), we can perform the appropriate action, whether it's adding, subtracting, multiplying, or dividing the numbers entered in the text fields.

Here’s the actual code:

1  // SimpleCalculator.java

2  import java.awt.*;

3  import java.awt.event.*;

4

5  public class SimpleCalculator implements ActionListener {

6    // Containers

7    private Frame f;

8    private Panel p1, p2, p3, p4;

9

10   // Components

11     private Label l1, l2, l3;

12     private TextField tf1, tf2, tf3;

13     private Button bAdd, bSub, bMul, bDiv, bClear;

14  

15     public SimpleCalculator() {

16       // Frame and Panels

17       f = new Frame("Simple Calculator");

18

19       p1 = new Panel();

20       p2 = new Panel();

21       p3 = new Panel();

22       p4 = new Panel();

23  

24       // Labels and Text Fields

25       l1 = new Label("First Number: ");

26       l2 = new Label("Second Number: ");

27       l3 = new Label("Result: ");

28  

29       tf1 = new TextField("0.0", 15);

30       tf2 = new TextField("0.0", 15);

31       tf3 = new TextField("0.0", 15);

32

33       // Buttons

34       bAdd = new Button("+");

35       bSub = new Button("-");

36       bMul = new Button("*");

37       bDiv = new Button("/");

38       bClear = new Button("C");

39     }

40

41     public void startApp() {

42       // Layout setup

43       p1.add(l1);

44       p1.add(tf1);

45       p2.add(l2);

46       p2.add(tf2);

47       p3.add(l3);

48       p3.add(tf3);

49       p4.add(bAdd);

50       p4.add(bSub);

51       p4.add(bMul);

52       p4.add(bDiv);

53       p4.add(bClear);

54

55       f.setLayout(new GridLayout(4, 1));

56       f.add(p1);

57       f.add(p2);

58       f.add(p3);

59       f.add(p4);

60       f.pack();

61       f.setVisible(true);

62

63       // Register event listeners for buttons

64       bAdd.addActionListener(this);

65       bSub.addActionListener(this);

66       bMul.addActionListener(this);

67       bDiv.addActionListener(this);

68       bClear.addActionListener(this);

69     f.addWindowListener(new MyCloseButtonHandler());

70     }

71  

72     // Event handler for button clicks

73     public void actionPerformed(ActionEvent e) {

74        Object source = e.getSource();

75        double num1 = 0.0, num2 = 0.0, num3 = 0.0;

76  

77        if (!tf1.getText().isEmpty() &&

78          tf2.getText().isEmpty()) {

79          num1 = Double.parseDouble(tf1.getText());

80          num2 = Double.parseDouble(tf2.getText());

81

82          if (source == bAdd) {

83             num3 = num1 + num2;

84          } else if (source == bSub) {

85             num3 = num1 - num2;

86          } else if (source == bMul) {

87             num3 = num1 * num2;

88          } else if (source == bDiv) {

89             num3 = num1 / num2;

90          } else if (source == bClear) {

91             tf1.setText("0.0");

92             tf2.setText("0.0");

93             tf3.setText("0.0");

94          }

95          tf3.setText(String.valueOf(num3));

96        }

97     }

98

99     // Window close handler

100     private class MyCloseButtonHandler

101             extends WindowAdapter {

102      public void windowClosing(WindowEvent we) {

103          System.exit(0);

104       }

105    }

106    public static void main(String[] args) {

107       SimpleCalculator sc = new SimpleCalculator();

108       sc.startApp();

109    }

110  }

This should give you a runtime output with working events. [Figure 10.4](#23_Chapter10.xhtml_fig10-4) shows a sample screenshot of our SimpleCalculator App.

Figure 10.4 SimpleCalculator App

![](Images/note.jpg) [Chapter Summary](#08_Contents.xhtml_sum10)

●Event handling in Java involves interactions such as button clicks, mouse movements, or keyboard shortcuts, which are captured as events and tied to specific components called event sources. Each event triggers a method, known as an event handler, that executes the desired action, ensuring consistent behavior across different types of user interactions.

●There are different event handling techniques, namely the delegation model, using the listener interface, using adapter classes, inner classes, anonymous classes, and using the lambda expression.

Coding Task 1: My Color Calculator (with Event Handler)

●Revisit the Coding Task in Chapter 9\. Provide the needed functionality on your Color Calculator. It should be able to accept the (Red, Green, Blue, Alpha) values from the user’s input on their respective textfields. The Red, Green, and Blue values are the primary colors, while Alpha represents the transparency level of a color. It controls how opaque or transparent a color should be. The red, green, blue, and alpha value ranges from 0 to 255\. If the alpha value is 0, it means it is fully transparent (completely see-through), and 255 means fully opaque (no transparency).

●Catch invalid values entered in the text fields. Throw an exception if the user entered any of the following:

▪A negative value

▪A value beyond the range from 0 to 255

▪A non-integer value

▪A blank text field.

●Update the background color of the Panel with the computed values from the four text fields. You may want to check out the constructor from the Color class in the API where you can pass four integer values to instantiate the color.

# [CHAPTER 11](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch11)

# [Building a Swing Desktop App](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch11)

![](Images/klo.jpg)Key Learning Objectives

●Understand the key differences between AWT and Swing in Java GUI development

●Learn how to convert AWT applications into Swing-based applications

●Explore commonly used Swing components, such as JCheckBox, JRadioButton, JComboBox, JList, and JLabel

●Gain hands-on experience in designing graphical user interfaces using Swing

●Learn how to package and distribute your Swing applications as Java Archive (JAR) files

In our final chapter, we explore the key differences between AWT and Swing, focusing on how Swing offers a more flexible and modern approach to building graphical user interfaces in Java. While AWT laid the foundation for GUI development, Swing enhances it with a richer set of components and greater control over look and feel. You'll learn how to transition an existing AWT-based application into a Swing-based one, unlocking more advanced features and improved visual design. We’ll also take a closer look at several commonly used Swing components—such as JCheckBox, JRadioButton, JComboBox, JList, and JLabel—and see how they can enhance user interaction. Finally, the chapter will guide you through packaging your Swing application into a Java Archive (JAR) file, making it easy to share, run, or deploy across platforms. By the end of this chapter, you’ll be ready to design, build, and distribute full-featured Java desktop applications using Swing.

## [11.1AWT vs. Swing](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch11-1)

Java AWT (Abstract Window Toolkit) and Swing are both used for building graphical user interfaces (GUIs) in Java, but they have key differences.

Swing is part of the Java Foundation Classes (JFC) and is built on top of AWT. While AWT relies on native operating system components, Swing provides lightweight, platform-independent components that look the same across all operating systems.

In AWT, components like Frame and Button are directly tied to the platform, meaning their appearance can vary depending on whether you're using Windows, macOS, or Linux. In contrast, Swing components like JFrame and JButton look consistent across different platforms since they don't depend on the native OS.

Figure 11.1 AWT Sample Frame for both Solaris and Windows OS

![image](Images/fig11-1.jpg)

Figure 11.2 Swing Sample Frame

![image](Images/fig11-2.jpg)

Swing adds more features and flexibility. It offers additional components like JRadioButton, JComboBox, JCheckBox, and JLabel, which are not available in AWT. Swing also supports a pluggable look-and-feel, meaning you can customize the appearance of components, something AWT does not support.

Another key difference is that AWT components are generally heavier in memory and slower, while Swing components are lightweight, offering better performance. Swing also follows the MVC (Model-View-Controller) design pattern, which AWT doesn't support.

In summary, AWT provides basic, platform-dependent components, while Swing offers a richer, more flexible, and platform-independent set of components. Swing was also introduced in Java 1.2 to replace the AWT package.

## [11.2Converting Your AWT App to a Swing-Based App](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch11-2)

In this section, we will focus on converting an AWT (Abstract Window Toolkit) application into a basic Swing-based application. To achieve this, it is essential to import the javax.swing package into our simple calculator application.

As a brief recap, the original calculator application utilizes various AWT components, including panels, frames, labels, text fields, and buttons. It also incorporates the AWT event package to manage event handling, such as using an ActionListener to respond to button clicks or implementing a close button handler that enables the application to close when the user presses Command+Q (for macOS) or Alt+F4 (for Windows). This forms a fully functional AWT application.

Here’s the original AWT SimpleCalculator.java source code:

1     // SimpleCalculator.java

2     import java.awt.*;

3     import java.awt.event.*;

4  

5     public class SimpleCalculator implements ActionListener

6     {

7      // Containers

8      private Frame f;

9    private Panel p1, p2, p3, p4;

10

11    // Components

12    private Label l1, l2, l3;

13    private TextField tf1, tf2, tf3;

14    private Button bAdd, bSub, bMul, bDiv, bClear;

15  

16    public SimpleCalculator()

17    {

18        // container

19        f = new Frame("My First GUI App");

20  

21        p1 = new Panel();

22        p2 = new Panel();

23        p3 = new Panel();

24        p4 = new Panel();

25  

26        // components

27        l1 = new Label("First: ");

28        l2 = new Label("Second: ");

29        l3 = new Label("Result: ");

30

31        tf1 = new TextField("0.0", 15);

32        tf2 = new TextField("0.0", 15);

33        tf3 = new TextField("0.0", 15);

34  

35        bAdd = new Button("+");

36        bSub = new Button("-");

37        bMul = new Button("*");

38        bDiv = new Button("/");

39        bClear = new Button("C");

40    }

41

42    public void startApp()

43    {

44        // use the default layout manager

45        // for all panels, use the FlowLayout.

46

47        p1.add(l1);

48        p1.add(tf1);

49    

50        p2.add(l2);

51        p2.add(tf2);

52

53        p3.add(l3);

54        p3.add(tf3);

55  

56        p4.add(bAdd);

57        p4.add(bSub);

58        p4.add(bMul);

59        p4.add(bDiv);

60        p4.add(bClear);

61  

62        // change the layout manager for our

63        // Frame f; use GridLayout(4, 1);

64        f.setLayout(new GridLayout(4, 1));

65

66        f.add(p1);

67        f.add(p2);

68        f.add(p3);

69        f.add(p4);

70

71        f.pack();

72        f.setVisible(true);

73  

74        // register your event handler to

75        // your UI (event source)

76        bAdd.addActionListener(this);

77        bSub.addActionListener(this);

78        bMul.addActionListener(this);

79        bDiv.addActionListener(this);

80        bClear.addActionListener(this);

81  

82        f.addWindowListener(new MyCloseButtonHandler());

83    }

84  

85    // event handler

86    public void actionPerformed(ActionEvent e)

87    {

88        Object source = e.getSource();

89        double num1 = 0.0, num2 = 0.0, num3 = 0.0;

90

91        if (tf1.getText() != null

92          && tf2.getText() != null) {

93         num1 = Double.parseDouble(tf1.getText());

94         num2 = Double.parseDouble(tf2.getText());

95  

96         if (source == bAdd)

97         {

98          num3 = num1 + num2;

99         }

100         else if (source == bSub)

101         {

102           num3 = num1 - num2;

103         }

104         else if (source == bMul)

105         {

106           num3 = num1 * num2;

107         }

108         else if (source == bDiv)

109         {

110           num3 = num1 / num2;

111         }

112         else if (source == bClear)

113         {

114          tf1.setText("0.0");

115          tf2.setText("0.0");  

116          tf3.setText("0.0");        

117         }

118         else {} // do nothing

119         // this also works

120         // tf3.setText(new Double(num3).toString());

121         tf3.setText("" + num3);

122       }    

123    }

124  

125    private class MyCloseButtonHandler

126          extends WindowAdapter {

127        public void windowClosing(WindowEvent we)

128        {

129          System.exit(0);

130        }

131    }

132  

133    public static void main(String args[])

134    {

135        SimpleCalculator sc = new SimpleCalculator();

136        sc.startApp();

137    }

138  }

To convert the application to Swing, we will begin by declaring a package for the application. Although the inclusion of a package declaration is advisable when preparing to create a JAR file, it is not strictly necessary at this stage if the JAR file is not immediately required.

Next, we will convert the AWT components and containers to their corresponding Swing components by adding a "J" prefix to each. For instance, we will use JFrame, JPanel, JLabel, JTextField, and JButton instead of their AWT counterparts. However, the layout managers will remain unchanged. For example, GridLayout should not have a "J" prefix as there is no equivalent Swing-specific layout manager.

To better illustrate the difference between AWT and Swing, a comparison can be made between the two versions of the same code. When running the AWT version on a Windows machine, the buttons and other UI elements appear less visually appealing compared to their Swing counterparts. Swing offers a more refined appearance, with customizable features such as background colors that provide a more modern look and feel. While the difference in appearance is subtle on macOS, the distinction is more noticeable on Windows.

Figure 11.3 Runtime Comparison between AWT and Swing Version

![image](Images/fig11-3.jpg)

To initiate the conversion of our calculator application, we begin by modifying the component and container declarations, adding the "J" prefix where appropriate, both in the declarations and within the constructor, where components and containers are instantiated. This transforms them into Swing-based components. Importantly, no changes should be made to the layout managers, as Swing does not provide its own versions.

Regarding the close button handler, there is an option to retain the existing functionality, or it may be modified to use Swing’s frame method for closing. Once the changes are saved, we can compile the code (using javac SimpleCalculator.java), and the application will now operate as a Swing-based component.

While the general appearance remains similar to the AWT version, the use of Swing provides enhanced functionality, and the UI elements—such as buttons—should now work as expected, including the "clear" and "close" buttons.

Here’s the updated Swing version of the SimpleCalculator App:

## [11.3Common Swing Components](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch11-3)

### 11.3.1JCheckBox: RGBCheckBox.java

The JCheckBox class in Java is a part of the Swing package, which provides a way to create checkboxes in a graphical user interface. These checkboxes can be either selected or deselected by the user. Unlike radio buttons, multiple checkboxes can be selected at the same time.

In the example RGBCheckBox.java given below, we will create a simple graphical interface with three checkboxes—Red, Green, and Blue—using Swing components. We will also add a button labeled "Click Me!" for further interaction.

Code Breakdown

1.Imports: The program starts by importing java.awt.* and javax.swing.*, which provide the necessary classes for creating the GUI components and handling the layout, the java.awt package is required for the layout managers.

import java.awt.*;

import javax.swing.*;

2.Class declaration: The class RGBCheckBox is declared as a public class. Inside this class, we define private fields for the JFrame, JButton, and JCheckBox components that will make up the UI.

public class RGBCheckBox {

   private JFrame f;

   private JButton b;

   private JCheckBox red, green, blue;

3.Constructor: The constructor RGBCheckBox() initializes the JFrame and JButton objects and creates three checkboxes: red, green, and blue. These components will be part of the user interface.

public RGBCheckBox() {

   f = new JFrame("JCheckBox Example");

   b = new JButton("Click Me!");

   red = new JCheckBox(" Red ");

   green = new JCheckBox(" Green ");

   blue = new JCheckBox(" Blue ");

}

4.StartApp method: The startApp() method configures the layout of the frame, which uses a GridLayout with 4 rows and 1 column to display the checkboxes and the button vertically. The setSelected(true) method is used to pre-select the Red and Blue checkboxes.

The setLayout(new GridLayout(4, 1)) ensures that the components are arranged in four rows.

The f.add() method adds the components to the frame in the desired order.

f.setBounds(100, 100, 300, 200) specifies the position (100,100) of the top-left corner of the window and its size (300x200).

The f.setVisible(true) makes the window visible to the user.

public void startApp() {

   f.setLayout(new GridLayout(4, 1));

   red.setSelected(true);

   blue.setSelected(true);

   f.add(red);

   f.add(green);

   f.add(blue);

   f.add(b);

   f.setBounds(100, 100, 300, 200);

   f.setVisible(true);

}

5.Main method: The main method serves as the entry point for the program. It creates an instance of the RGBCheckBox class and calls the startApp() method to display the UI.

public static void main(String args[]) {

   RGBCheckBox rgb = new RGBCheckBox();

   rgb.startApp();

}

How the Code Works

When the program runs, a window appears with three checkboxes: Red, Green, and Blue. The Red and Blue checkboxes are pre-selected. Users can check or uncheck any combination of these boxes.

A button labeled "Click Me!" is also displayed, though it currently has no functionality implemented. Future extensions could involve adding event listeners to the button and checkboxes to perform actions when clicked.

Figure 11.4 RGBCheckBox Runtime

![image](Images/fig11-4.jpg)

Key points

●JCheckBox is part of the Swing framework, and it inherits many methods from its superclass, allowing for extensive customization.

●The setBounds() method is used to both position and size the window, offering more control over its layout.

This simple example demonstrates how to create a basic GUI with multiple checkboxes using Swing components in Java. You can further extend this by adding event handling to make the UI interactive.

Here’s the complete source code:

9       private JButton b;

10      private JCheckBox red, green, blue;

11   

12      public RGBCheckBox()

14         f = new JFrame("JCheckBox Example");

15         b = new JButton("Click Me!");

16         red = new JCheckBox(" Red ");

18         blue = new JCheckBox(" Blue ");

Instructions for Running

2.Compile the code using javac RGBCheckBox.java.

3.Run the program using java RGBCheckBox.

When you run it, a window will appear with three checkboxes (Red, Green, and Blue), with Red and Blue pre-selected, along with a "Click Me!" button.

### 11.3.2JRadioButton: RGBRadioButton.java

In this explanation, we will break down how to create a basic Java Swing application using JRadioButton components, showing the difference between radio buttons and checkboxes.

Key Difference Between Radio Buttons and Checkboxes

●Radio buttons: Allow the user to select only one option at a time. When you select a radio button, any previously selected button in the same group is automatically deselected.

●Checkboxes (JCheckBox): Allow the user to select multiple options simultaneously. You can select or deselect multiple checkboxes independently.

Code Breakdown

1.Class and import statements: We start by importing the necessary packages:

// For GridLayout and other layout managers

import java.awt.*;

// For JFrame, JButton, JRadioButton

import javax.swing.*;

2.Creating the main class: In this case, the class is called RGBRadioButton:

public class RGBRadioButton {

   // Declare the components:

   // JFrame, JButton, and JRadioButton

   private JFrame f;

   private JButton b;

   private JRadioButton red, green, blue;

   // Constructor to initialize the components

   public RGBRadioButton() {

     f = new JFrame("RGBJRadioButton Example");

     red = new JRadioButton(" Red "); // Red

   green = new JRadioButton(" Green ");// Green

   blue = new JRadioButton(" Blue "); // Blue

   b = new JButton("Click Me!"); // Button

  }

}

3.The startApp method: This method sets up the layout and adds the components (radio buttons, buttons, etc.) to the frame:

public void startApp() {

   // Set a GridLayout with 4 rows and 1 column for

   // arranging components vertically

   f.setLayout(new GridLayout(4, 1));

   // By default, the Red radio button will

   // be selected at startup

   red.setSelected(true);

   // Add the radio buttons to the frame

   // so they appear in the UI

   f.add(red);

   f.add(green);

   f.add(blue);

   // Group the radio buttons together to ensure only

   // one can be selected at a time

   ButtonGroup colorGroup = new ButtonGroup();

   // add the red, green and blue

   // buttons to the group

   colorGroup.add(red);

   colorGroup.add(green);

   colorGroup.add(blue);

   // Add the "Click Me!" button to the frame

   f.add(b);

   // Set the size and location of the frame

   f.setBounds(200, 200, 300, 200);

   // Make the frame visible to the user

   f.setVisible(true);

}

4.Main method: This method is the entry point of the program. It creates an instance of the RGBRadioButton class and calls the startApp() method to launch the application.

public static void main(String args[]) {

   RGBRadioButton rgb = new RGBRadioButton();

   rgb.startApp(); // Start the application

}

Key Concepts

●JRadioButton: Represents a radio button in Swing. Only one button in a group of radio buttons can be selected at a time. If one is selected, the other is automatically deselected.

●ButtonGroup: Groups the radio buttons together. Without this, each radio button would function independently, meaning multiple selections could be made.

●GridLayout: This layout manager arranges components in a grid of rows and columns. In this case, we use a 4x1 grid to display three radio buttons and a button vertically.

●setSelected(true): This method pre-selects the Red radio button when the application starts.

●setBounds(): Defines the frame's position and size on the screen. In this case, the frame starts at (200, 200) with a width of 300 and a height of 200 pixels.

Grouping Radio Buttons

To make sure that only one radio button can be selected at a time, the buttons are grouped using a ButtonGroup. When the buttons are added to the group, it ensures mutual exclusivity:

ButtonGroup colorGroup = new ButtonGroup();

// add the red, green and blue buttons to the group

colorGroup.add(red);

colorGroup.add(green);

colorGroup.add(blue);

Visual Representation and Interaction

When you run the program, you'll see:

●Three radio buttons labeled Red, Green, and Blue

●A button labeled "Click Me!"

●The Red radio button is pre-selected by default

Clicking on any other radio button will automatically deselect the current selection, ensuring only one option is active at any given time.

Figure 11.5 RGBRadioButton Runtime

![image](Images/fig11-5.jpg)

Exit Behavior

In Swing, even when the close button is clicked on the window, the application may not fully terminate unless a specific code is added to handle the window closure. The default behavior is that the window will hide but not exit unless specified otherwise.

Here’s the full Java source code with a file named: RGBRadioButton.java

4     import javax.swing.*;

5

8        private JButton b;

9        private JRadioButton red, green, blue;

10

12         f = new JFrame("RGBJRadioButton Example");

13         red = new JRadioButton(" Red ");

14         green = new JRadioButton(" Green ");

15         blue = new JRadioButton(" Blue ");

16         b = new JButton("Click Me!");

17      }

18   

19      public void startApp() {

20         f.setLayout(new GridLayout(4, 1));

21         red.setSelected(true);

22         f.add(red);

23         f.add(green);

24         f.add(blue);

25

27         colorGroup.add(red);

28         colorGroup.add(green);

29         colorGroup.add(blue);

30         f.add(b);

31         f.setBounds(200, 200, 300, 200);

### 11.3.3JComboBox: JComboBoxExample.java

A JComboBox in Java is a Swing component that allows users to select an item from a drop-down list. It can either be a non-editable list where users pick from predefined values or an editable one where users can also type their own input. This component combines the functionality of a button and a text field, making it a versatile input method for users.

In this example, we’ll walk through how to create a basic JComboBox, populate it with values, and implement event handling so that when a user selects an option, it updates a label to reflect the chosen value.

Components Used in the Code

●JFrame: A window or container that holds all the components of the application.

●JButton: A clickable button that triggers an event.

●JComboBox: The drop-down list that contains options for users to select from.

●JLabel: A label to display messages or results based on user actions.

●String Array: An array containing a list of colors, which will be displayed in the JComboBox.

Code Breakdown

1\. Declaring the class

The class JComboBoxExample starts by declaring private variables to represent the JFrame, JButton, JComboBox, and JLabel, which will be used throughout the application.

private JFrame f;

private JButton b;

private JComboBox<String> cb;

private JLabel label;

private String colorList[];

●f represents the frame or window.

●b is the button labeled "Show".

●cb is the JComboBox containing color options.

●label will display the selected color.

●colorList[] holds the predefined color values for the JComboBox.

2\. Constructor setup

The constructor initializes all the components of the GUI (Graphical User Interface). It creates the frame, button, JComboBox, and label, while also assigning positions using the setBounds() method, which defines their location and size within the frame.

public JComboBoxExample() {

   f = new JFrame("JComboBox List Example");

   label = new JLabel();

   colorList = new String[] {"Red", "Green",

            "Blue", "Yellow", "Purple"};

   cb = new JComboBox<>(colorList);

cb.setBounds(50, 100, 90, 20);

   label.setHorizontalAlignment(JLabel.CENTER);

   label.setSize(400, 100);

   b = new JButton("Show");

   b.setBounds(200, 100, 75, 20);

}

Here’s what happens:

●A new JFrame is created with the title "JComboBox List Example".

●The JLabel is aligned to the center, and its size is set.

●The JComboBox is populated with the values from the colorList array.

●The JButton labeled "Show" is created.

3\. Starting the application

The startApp() method is responsible for adding the components to the frame and making the frame visible.

public void startApp() {

   f.setLayout(null);    // No layout manager is used,

                         // so positions are set manually

                         // with setBounds.

   f.add(cb);            // Add JComboBox to the frame.

   f.add(label);        // Add label to the frame.

   f.add(b);            // Add button to the frame.

   f.setSize(350, 350); // Set the size of the frame.

   f.setVisible(true);  // Make the frame visible.

}

The method also disables the default layout manager by calling f.setLayout(null), allowing manual positioning of components.

4\. Event handling for the button

To make the button functional, an ActionListener is added. When the button is clicked, the selected item from the JComboBox is retrieved and displayed in the JLabel.

b.addActionListener(new ActionListener() {

   public void actionPerformed(ActionEvent ae) {

      String str = "My Color List: "

          + cb.getItemAt(cb.getSelectedIndex());

      label.setText(str); // Update the label with

             // the selected color.

   }

});

●The ActionListener listens for a button click.

●When the button is clicked, the selected value from the JComboBox is retrieved using cb.getSelectedIndex() and is set in the label.

●The label text is updated with the selected color name.

5\. Main method

The main method creates an instance of the JComboBoxExample class and starts the application by calling startApp().

public static void main(String args[]) {

   JComboBoxExample jcbe = new JComboBoxExample();

   jcbe.startApp();

}

Output and Behavior

When the application is run:

●A window (JFrame) appears with a JComboBox containing the color options.

●The user can select a color from the drop-down.

●Upon clicking the "Show" button, the selected color is displayed on the label.

Figure 11.6 JComboBoxExample Runtime 1

![image](Images/fig11-6.jpg)

Figure 11.7 JComboBoxExample Runtime 2

![image](Images/fig11-7.jpg)

Here is the complete code for your convenience. Save it as JComboBoxExample.java

1  // JComboBoxExample.java

2  import javax.swing.*;

3  import java.awt.event.*;

4    

5  public class JComboBoxExample {

6    private JFrame f;

7    private JButton b;

8    private JComboBox<String> cb;

9   private JLabel label;

10   private String[] colorList;

11   

12   public JComboBoxExample() {

13      f = new JFrame("JComboBox List Example");

14      label = new JLabel();

15      colorList = new String[] {"Red", "Green",

16        "Blue", "Yellow", "Purple"};

17      cb = new JComboBox<>(colorList);

18      cb.setBounds(50, 100, 90, 20);

19      label.setHorizontalAlignment(JLabel.CENTER);

20      label.setSize(400, 100);

21      b = new JButton("Show");

22      b.setBounds(200, 100, 75, 20);

23    }

24   

25    public void startApp() {

26      f.setLayout(null);

27      f.add(cb);

28      f.add(label);

29      f.add(b);

30      f.setSize(350, 350);

31      f.setVisible(true);

32      b.addActionListener(new ActionListener() {

33      public void actionPerformed(ActionEvent ae) {

34        String str = "My Color List: "

35         + cb.getItemAt(cb.getSelectedIndex());

36         label.setText(str);

37         }});

38      }

39   

40      public static void main(String args[]) {

41        JComboBoxExample jcbe = new JComboBoxExample();

42        jcbe.startApp();

43      }

44  }

### 11.3.4JList: RGBJList.java

In Java, the JList component is used to display a list of items from which users can select one or more elements. With a JList, you have the flexibility to enable single or multiple selections, allowing users to choose several items at once.

To create a JList, it is necessary to have a "list model," which serves as a container for the elements you want to display in the JList. Think of the list model as the data holder, while the JList acts as the visual interface that shows the items.

In the provided sample code, a class named RGBJList is created. This class declares a frame (JFrame) and a label (JLabel) to display a list of color options. First, the frame is instantiated and set to use a FlowLayout, which helps arrange components from left to right, similar to text in a paragraph.

In the startApp() method, a DefaultListModel is created to hold the list data. This model is of type String, meaning it will store a collection of string values representing the color names. These values are added to the model using the addElement() method, which adds items such as "Red," "Green," and "Blue."

Once the list model is populated with these colors, a new JList is created and linked to the model. This connection ensures that the JList (colorList in the code) will display the items from the list model. By default, the first item in the list (index 0, "Red") is pre-selected using the setSelectedIndex(0) method.

Next, a JLabel is used to display some text ("Colors:"). Both the label and the JList are added to the frame, and the frame's size and position are set using setBounds(200, 200, 300, 200), which specifies the frame's location and size in pixels.

Finally, the frame is made visible with the setVisible(true) method, allowing the application window to be displayed to the user.

When you run the code, you will see a list of color options ("Red," "Green," and "Blue"). Initially, "Red" is selected because of the setSelectedIndex(0) call. Users can click on any of the colors to make a selection. They can also make multiple selections by holding down the Shift or Command (Mac) / Control (Windows) keys while clicking on additional colors.

Here’s the sample code saved as: RGBJList.java

1    // RGBJList.java

2    import java.awt.*;

3    import javax.swing.*;

4    

5      public class RGBJList {

6       private JFrame f;

7       private JLabel label;

8    

9       public RGBJList() {

10         f = new JFrame("JList Color Example");

11      }

12   

13      public void startApp() {

14         f.setLayout(new FlowLayout());

15         DefaultListModel<String> listModel =

16           new DefaultListModel<>();

17         listModel.addElement("Red");

18         listModel.addElement("Green");

19         listModel.addElement("Blue");

20   

21         JList<String> colorList = new JList<>();

22         colorList.setModel(listModel);

23         colorList.setSelectedIndex(0);

24         label = new JLabel();

25         label.setText("Colors: ");

26         f.add(label);

27         f.add(colorList);

28         f.setBounds(200, 200, 300, 200);

29         f.setVisible(true);

30      }

31   

32      public static void main(String args[]) {

33         RGBJList rgb = new RGBJList();

34         rgb.startApp();

35      }

36   }

This code creates a simple graphical interface with a JList displaying color names and enables interaction through single and multiple selections.

[Figures 11.8](#24_Chapter11.xhtml_fig11-8) and [11.9](#24_Chapter11.xhtml_fig11-9) show a sample runtime.

![image](Images/fig11-8.jpg)

Figure 11.9 RGBJList Runtime with multiple chosen options

![image](Images/fig11-9.jpg)

### 11.3.5JLabel: CounterApp.java

Using Label in the java.awt package is too plain; the Swing version of JLabel provides a more exciting use of labels.

The following example breaks down the working of a CounterApp that is a basic counter application using Swing components such as JLabel, JButton, and event handling through ActionListener.

[Figure 11.10](#24_Chapter11.xhtml_fig11-10) shows what it is supposed to look like.

Figure 11.10 CounterApp Runtime

![image](Images/fig11-10.jpg)

This Java Swing application creates a counter that increments each time the "Click Me!" button is pressed. The counter value is displayed using three JLabel components representing the hundreds, tens, and ones places. The app leverages event handling to detect button clicks and updates the counter accordingly.

Explanation of Key Components

1. Imports and packages:

●java.awt.*: This package is included to handle the layout of the components using classes like GridLayout and FlowLayout.

●java.awt.event.*: This is needed to manage user interactions like button clicks, by handling ActionListener and ActionEvent.

●javax.swing.*: Swing components like JFrame, JLabel, and JButton are used to build the graphical user interface (GUI).

2.Class definition: The main class, CounterApp, implements ActionListener, which allows the app to respond to user actions, such as button clicks.

3.Instance variables:

●JFrame f: The main window of the application.

●Panel pan: A container to hold the labels (counter digits).

●JLabel l1, l2, l3: Labels to display the hundreds, tens, and ones digits of the counter.

●JButton button: A button that increments the counter when clicked.

●int counter: A static variable to store the current counter value.

4.Constructor: The constructor CounterApp() initializes the frame, panel, labels, and button. The labels are set with the initial value "0" and styled using the Font class (bold, serif, size 100). The background of the panel is set to black, while the button is initialized with the text "Click Me!".

5.startApp method: This method sets up the layout, adds components to the frame, and makes the window visible:

●Panel layout: A grid layout with 1 row and 3 columns is used for the panel to align the labels horizontally.

●Label configuration: Labels 1 and 2 are set to have a white foreground, and Label 3 (representing the ones place) has a black foreground with an opaque background for better visibility.

●Frame configuration: The panel is added to the center of the frame, and the button is positioned at the bottom (south) of the frame using BorderLayout.SOUTH.

●Event registration: The button is linked to an event handler using button.addActionListener(this), which connects the button click event to the actionPerformed() method.

6.actionPerformed method: When the button is clicked, this method is triggered:

●The current counter value is retrieved by concatenating the values from the three labels and converting the result into an integer.

●The counter is incremented by one (counter++), and the new value is split into hundreds, tens, and ones digits.

●The text of each label is updated using setText(), converting the integer digits back to strings.

7.Main method: The main method creates an instance of the CounterApp class and starts the application by calling startApp().

Key Concepts

●JLabel customization: The code demonstrates how to style JLabel components by changing fonts, colors, and opacity using methods like setFont(), setForeground(), and setOpaque().

●Layout management: The panel uses a grid layout to arrange the labels horizontally, while the frame uses a border layout to position components in specific regions (center for the panel and south for the button).

●Event handling: The application listens for user interactions (button clicks) using the ActionListener interface, making the app responsive to user input.

Running the Application

When the program runs, it displays a window with three large digits, all initialized to 0, and a button labeled "Click Me!" Every time the button is clicked, the counter increments, updating the labels to reflect the new counter value (with hundreds, tens, and ones positions).

In summary, this Java Swing application showcases a simple yet interactive counter using JLabel for display, JButton for interaction, and event handling to make the button functional. The use of Swing's flexible components allows for a highly customizable interface.

Here’s the complete code: CounterApp.java

1   // CounterApp.java

2   import java.awt.*;

3   import java.awt.event.*;

4   import javax.swing.*;

5   

6   public class CounterApp implements ActionListener {

7     private JFrame f;

8     private Panel pan;

9     private JLabel l1, l2, l3;

10     private JButton button;

11     private static int counter = 0;

12   

13    public CounterApp() {

14      f = new JFrame("My Counter App");

15      pan = new Panel();

16      pan.setBackground(Color.BLACK);

17      l1 = new JLabel("0");

18      l1.setFont(new Font("Serif", Font.BOLD, 100));

19      l2 = new JLabel("0");

20      l2.setFont(new Font("Serif", Font.BOLD, 100));

21      l3 = new JLabel("0");

22      l3.setFont(new Font("Serif", Font.BOLD, 100));

23      button = new JButton("Click Me!");

24    }

25   

26    public void startApp() {

27      l3.setOpaque(true);

28      l1.setForeground(Color.WHITE);

29      l2.setForeground(Color.WHITE);

30      l3.setForeground(Color.BLACK);

31      pan.setLayout(new GridLayout(1, 3));

32      pan.add(l1);

33      pan.add(l2);

34      pan.add(l3);

35      f.add(pan, BorderLayout.CENTER);

36      f.add(button, BorderLayout.SOUTH);

37      f.pack();

38      f.setVisible(true);

39      f.setDefaultCloseOperation(JFrame.EXIT_ON_CLOSE);

40      // register your event handler

41      button.addActionListener(this);

42    }

43   

44    public void actionPerformed(ActionEvent ae) {

45      counter = Integer.parseInt(l1.getText() +

46               l2.getText() + l3.getText());  

47      counter++;

48      int hund = counter / 100;  

49      int temp = counter % 100;    

50      int tens = temp / 10;        

51      int ones = temp % 10;        

52      l1.setText("" + hund);

53      l2.setText("" + tens);

54      l3.setText("" + ones);

55    }

56   

57    public static void main(String args[]) {

58      CounterApp counterApp = new CounterApp();

59      counterApp.startApp();

60    }

61   }

## [11.4Packaging Your Application to a Java ARchive (JAR) File](#08_Contents.xhtml_ch11-4)

### 11.4.1JAR Files Overview

JAR stands for Java Archive and is a file format used to distribute Java applications or libraries in a single file. Similar to ZIP files, JAR files bundle multiple files together, but they are designed specifically for Java-related files, such as .class files, resources, and metadata. JAR files make it easy to distribute Java programs and their associated files across different platforms. There are two common types of JAR files:

●Regular JAR file: Bundles Java classes and resources, but doesn't specify a main class.

●Executable JAR file: Includes a MANIFEST.MF file that declares the main class, making the JAR file directly executable.

### 11.4.2Step-By-Step Guide for Creating JAR Files

Setting up a basic project directory

Before creating a JAR file, you need to organize your Java project files. Suppose you are working on a Swing Desktop App with the following directory structure:

![image](Images/pg485.jpg)

Key directories:

src/: Contains your source code (.java files)

classes/: This is where the compiled .class files will be stored.

dist/: This is where the JAR file will be stored after packaging.

Adding the package declaration to your Java file

When converting your application into a JAR file, it's crucial to ensure that the Java classes are part of a package. For example, if your SimpleCalculator.java file is inside the src/demo/ folder, your source code should start with the following package declaration:

// should be placed on top of your Java Source Code.

package demo;

The package declaration should reflect the folder structure of your project. The folder that contains your .java file (in this case, demo/) should be the package name.

Compiling the Java source code (command line)

To compile the source code, open a terminal and navigate to the src/ directory of your project. The following command will compile the SimpleCalculator.java file:

javac -d ../classes demo/SimpleCalculator.java

Explanation:

●javac: Is the Java compiler command

●-d ../classes: Specifies the output directory for the compiled .class files (in this case, the classes/ folder)

●demo/SimpleCalculator.java: The path to the Java source file to be compiled

Once compiled, the classes/ folder will look like this:

![image](Images/pg486.jpg)

Creating a regular JAR file (command line)

Now that you have your compiled .class files, you can create a regular JAR file (without a manifest file). From the classes/ directory, run the following command:

jar cvf MyApp.jar demo/

Explanation:

●jar: Command to create or manage JAR files

●cvf: Options used for creating the JAR file:

▪c: Create a new JAR

▪v: Verbose output (optional, for showing details during creation)

▪f: Specifies the name of the JAR file

●MyApp.jar: The name of the resulting JAR file

●demo/: The folder containing the compiled .class files to include in the JAR

This command will create a file named MyApp.jar in the classes/ directory, which contains the contents of the demo/ folder.

Running a regular JAR file (command line)

A regular JAR file doesn't have a manifest file, so to run it, you need to specify the classpath and the fully qualified name of the main class. The command would look like this:

java -cp MyApp.jar demo.SimpleCalculator

Explanation:

●java: The Java runtime command

●-cp MyApp.jar: Specifies the JAR file as the classpath

●demo.SimpleCalculator: The fully qualified name of the main class (including the package name)

Creating an executable JAR file (command line)

An executable JAR file contains a MANIFEST.MF file that specifies which class should be executed when the JAR is run. Here's how to create one:

Step 1: Create a manifest file

A manifest file contains metadata about the JAR file, including the main class to be executed. Create a file named manifest.txt in the classes/ directory with the following content:

Main-Class: demo.SimpleCalculator

Important: Make sure there is a blank line at the end of the manifest file. This is required by the manifest format.

Step 2: Create the executable JAR

Use the following command to create the JAR file with the manifest included:

jar cvfm MyAppExecutable.jar manifest.txt demo/

Explanation:

●cvfm: The m option is used to specify the manifest file.

●MyAppExecutable.jar: The name of the resulting executable JAR file.

●manifest.txt: The path to the manifest file.

●demo/: The folder containing the compiled .class files.

Step 3: Running the executable JAR file

After creating the executable JAR, you can run it simply by using the java -jar command:

java -jar MyAppExecutable.jar

Since the MANIFEST.MF file contains the Main-Class entry; you don't need to specify the class name or the classpath explicitly.

Creating JAR files using the NetBeans IDE

If you're using an IDE like NetBeans, the process of creating JAR files is simplified.

Step 1: Create a new Java project in NetBeans

1.Open NetBeans and create a new Java project (File > New Project).

2.Choose Java with Ant as the project type and give your project a name like SwingDesktopAppProject.

3.Ensure that "Create Main Class" is unchecked during project setup, as you'll manually add the class later.

Step 2: Add source code and package

1.In the Projects window, right-click on Source Packages, and choose New > Java Class.

2.Create a package named demo and a class named SimpleCalculator (same as before).

3.Add the Java code for your calculator to the SimpleCalculator.java file, including the package declaration.

Step 3: Run and test your application

1.Right-click on SimpleCalculator.java in the Projects tab and select Run File.

2.NetBeans will compile and execute your application. You should see your calculator in action.

Step 4: Create the executable JAR in NetBeans

1.Add the Main-Class declaration: Go to the Files tab in NetBeans. Open the manifest.mf file located under the project folder.

2.Add the following entry to the manifest.mf file:

Main-Class: demo.SimpleCalculator

Ensure there's a blank line after this entry.

3.Build the project: Right-click on your project in the Projects tab and select Clean and Build. This will compile your project and package it into a JAR file.

4.Locate the JAR: The executable JAR file will be located in the dist/ folder of your project directory.

Step 5: Run the executable JAR

You can run the JAR file by navigating to the dist/ folder in a terminal and using the command:

java -jar SwingDesktopAppProject.jar

By following the steps above, you can create both regular and executable JAR files using either the command line or an IDE like NetBeans. The critical difference between the two types of JAR files lies in the presence of a MANIFEST.MF file with a Main-Class declaration, which allows you to run the JAR file without specifying the classpath or main class explicitly.

![](Images/note.jpg) [Chapter Summary](#08_Contents.xhtml_sum11)

●Java AWT and Swing are both used for building GUIs, but AWT relies on native OS components, making it platform-dependent, while Swing provides lightweight, platform-independent components that look consistent across platforms. Swing also offers more features, such as additional components and support for a customizable look and feel, and follows the MVC design pattern, unlike AWT.

●This lesson focuses on converting an AWT-based calculator application to a Swing-based one by importing the javax.swing package. The transformation involves replacing AWT components, such as Frame and Button, with their Swing counterparts, like JFrame and JButton, while retaining the same layout managers and event handling. Although both versions function similarly, Swing offers enhanced visual appeal and platform-independent UI features, which are particularly noticeable on Windows systems.

●A JAR (Java ARchive) file is used to bundle Java applications or libraries into a single file, making distribution easier. There are two main types: regular JARs, which bundle class files but don’t specify a main class, and executable JARs, which include a manifest file with a `Main-Class` declaration for direct execution. You can create a JAR file by compiling your Java code, organizing it into directories, and using the `jar` command. Alternatively, an IDE like NetBeans simplifies this process, automatically packaging your project and creating the necessary manifest file for executable JARs.
